# Preface.

---

Everything passes away; everything here below has an end; God alone is eternal and unchanging. Principalities, kingdoms, and great fortunes—all are subject to change. Yet, commonly, these changes occur only imperceptibly. One sees them in advance from the incidents that announce them and which precede them. Never has one occurred so swiftly and so suddenly as the fall of the Order of the Knights Templar <term>Tempelherren-Orden: A wealthy and powerful medieval Catholic military order suppressed in the early 14th century</term>. In a single day, in the same hour, they were destroyed; and this was a tragic proof of the terrible consequences that the hatred and wrath of kings can bring about. They—these likenesses of the Most High Lord <note>A reference to the "Divine Right of Kings," the belief that monarchs derive their authority directly from God</note>—are sometimes, like Him, creators and destroyers on earth.

Such was the fate of the Order of the Knights Templar, the history of whose dissolution we now undertake to write.

<summary>This preface introduces a historical account of the sudden and violent suppression of the Knights Templar, using it as a meditation on the fleeting nature of worldly power and the absolute authority of monarchs.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, dissolution of the Templars, monarchy, divine right, historical change, fall of empires</keywords>

<meta>Continuing the preface regarding the origins and sudden downfall of the Knights Templar. This page follows the introduction of the Order's tragic end, now looking back at its rise. Catchword: were.</meta>

## Preface.

Nothing in the world at the beginning of the fourteenth century was more brilliant than this famous Order, which had spread throughout all Christian kingdoms. It had reached the pinnacle of its greatness at that time, and this was also the very moment in which it vanished.

It had already taken its beginning in the eleventh century. The frequent pilgrimages <term>Wallfahrten: Religious journeys to sacred sites, a central practice of medieval Christian life</term>, which the faithful undertook driven by their piety to visit the Holy Places where the great mystery of our redemption was completed <note>Refers to the locations in Jerusalem associated with the life and death of Jesus Christ</note>, inspired the zeal of a few private individuals. They joined together to make the paths there free and to shield these devout travelers from the dangers to which they were exposed.

Following this, the Crusades <term>Kreuzzüge: A series of religious wars sanctioned by the Church to recover the Holy Land from Islamic rule</term> occurred and were accompanied by glorious conquests. As the number of pilgrims doubled because of these victories, the assistance of these brave defenders became all the more necessary. These were for the most part Frenchmen; initially, there were only eight of them. Hugh of Payens <note>original: "Hugo von Payens"; the first Grand Master of the Templars</note> and Godfrey of Adhemar <note>original: "Gottfried von Adhemar"; likely referring to Godfrey de Saint-Omer, the co-founder of the Order</note> were

<summary>This page describes the rapid ascent of the Knights Templar to the height of their power in the 14th century and traces their 11th-century origins as a small group of French knights protecting pilgrims in the Holy Land.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, Crusades, Pilgrimage, Hugh of Payens, Godfrey de Saint-Omer, 14th Century, 11th Century, Holy Land</keywords>

## Preface.

...were their leaders. They took their vows <term>Gelübde: Solemn religious promises, typically of poverty, chastity, and obedience</term> into the hands of the Patriarch of Jerusalem <note>The highest-ranking bishop in the Holy Land</note> and submitted themselves to the Rule of the Canons of Saint Augustine <note>A set of guidelines for religious life focused on communal living and poverty, attributed to St. Augustine of Hippo</note>.

This small band increased very soon, and the reputation of their bravery brought a great number of noblemen to them. Everything among them was held in common. They knew neither avarice nor ambition, and they renewed the conduct and unity of the first Christians, who had but one heart and one soul <note>original: "ein Herz und eine Seele"; a biblical reference to the communal life of the early church in Acts 4:32</note>.

The Kings of Jerusalem granted them a room for their dwelling in their palace, which lay near Solomon's Temple <note>The Al-Aqsa Mosque on the Temple Mount was identified by the Crusaders as the Temple of Solomon</note>. For this reason, they took the name of the Knights Templar. Initially, they possessed no estates; however, they did not lack for sustenance because of this. The King, the Patriarch, and the great lords provided them abundantly with provisions and cared for all their needs.

Hugh of Payens was the first Grand Master of the Order; his successors were just as brave as he, and were much more skillful.

Their fame spread throughout all of Europe. They distinguished themselves in Palestine through the greatest deeds. Nothing was spoken of but their courage, their fearlessness, and their virtues.

The younger sons of the greatest houses soon joined this Order with eagerness. All Christian princes vied with each other to give them gifts. King Baldwin IV <note>The "Leper King" of Jerusalem, who reigned from 1174 to 1185</note> gave them the city of Gaza, which became the headquarters of the Order. They were given estates, fiefs, and even cities with all their revenues in almost all realms. From these, they established <term>Commanderies: Local administrative centers and estates that provided income and recruits for the Order</term> (original: *Comthureyen*), which the Grand Master distributed: they had magnificent churches built there, which were splendidly decorated...

These knights, who in the beginning were so poor, so modest, and so submissive to their rulers, found themselves just as wealthy as those very sovereigns and now wished to keep pace with them. The King of France, **Louis VII**, in a letter to the Grand Master de Blanquefort, even accorded him the title <note>original: *Prädicat*</note> **by the Grace of God**, and his successor **Philip II** bequeathed him 40,000 Livres <term>Livre: A currency based on a pound of silver; 40,000 was an immense sum at the time</term>, a sum that was then sufficient for the dowries of four princesses of France. The Grand Masters also held the rank of princes at all courts.

Since the year 1129, they had withdrawn themselves from the jurisdiction of the Patriarch of Jerusalem and had submitted themselves to Pope **Gelasius II** <note>The text likely means Pope Honorius II or Innocent II, as Gelasius II died in 1119, well before the Council of Troyes in 1129</note>, who eagerly seized this opportunity to increase his power by becoming the superior of such a famous Order. He and his successors lavished the greatest privileges upon the Order. **Innocent III** gave the

## Preface.

But these astonishing riches, this prosperity, and so many fortunate occurrences, which raised the Order to such a high degree of fame, also had the unfortunate consequences that constant bliss commonly brings in its wake. They instilled in the Grand Masters and the knights a pride which gave them the delusion that they were superior to other men; they seduced them toward lust <note>original: *Wollust*; in this 18th-century context, the term refers to a life of indulgence in sensual pleasures and debauchery made possible by great wealth</note>, which wealth and abundance provide to those who surrender to their passions, and toward greed, which never has enough and always seeks to accumulate more.

These vices did not, to be sure, make them soft; because it was undoubtedly their boldness and bravery which provided them the means to expand themselves and to continue their revelry and sensual life; however, they became a source of proud conceits and injustices, which they often committed against the kings among whom they resided, even to the detriment of these princes. It was nevertheless difficult for them to defend themselves against the accusation made against them, that in the year 1159, under the Grand Mastership of Eudes de Saint-Amand <note>Historical records usually place the Grand Mastership of Eudes de Saint-Amand between 1171 and 1179; the date 1159 provided here may be an error by the original author or a reference to an earlier stage of the negotiations.</note>, they had prevented the conversion of the Prince of the Assassins <note>original: *Meuchelmörder*; this refers to the Nizari Ismaili state in Syria, whose leader was known to Crusaders as the "Old Man of the Mountain."</note>.

This prince, whose small state bordered the Holy Land, recognized the errors of the Mohammedan law <note>The text uses the historical term *Mahometanischen* to refer to Islamic law and faith.</note>, intended to adopt the Christian religion, and sent an envoy to Amaury, King of Jerusalem, to make an arrangement with him regarding this matter. He made one condition: namely, that the Knights Templar <note>original: *Tempelherren*</note> should waive a tribute of 2,000 Gold Ecus <term>Gold Ecus: Or *Écus d'or*, a French gold coin used here to denote the value of the annual tribute paid by the Assassins to the Order.</term> which he paid to them. This motive was perhaps not entirely pure for a prince who wished to become a Christian; however, the matter was of such minor importance in comparison to the advantage that religion would draw from it, that the King had no hesitation in promising the envoy the remission of the tribute...

# Preface.

...compensate them. It is even said that he undertook to keep the Order indemnified. The Knights Templar <note>original: *Tempelherren*</note> were incensed by an alliance entered into without their participation; they did not trust the King’s promise, and the fear of losing the sum—which in truth barely diminished their vast revenues—led them to a crime that dishonored them and caused a scandal throughout Europe. The knight Gautier Dümenil lay in wait for the envoy, or had others lie in wait, as he was traveling back; he had him murdered and took the treaty <note>original: *Tractat*</note> from him. 

Perhaps the Grand Master took no part in such a dark and abominable deed; however, he was nevertheless suspected because he went to so little trouble to punish it; and when the King had Dümenil arrested, the Order used its influence to remove him from the prince’s jurisdiction by appealing to the authority of the Pope <note>original: *Pabst*</note> as the superior of the Templars.

The Grand Master de Beaujeu <note>Guillaume de Beaujeu (c. 1230–1291), the 21st Grand Master of the Knights Templar</note> was killed there in the breach, after having performed deeds of heroic bravery.

The Templars <note>original: *Tempelherren*</note> withdrew to the island of Cyprus, where they held large estates. There, they elected a Grand Master who, from this new seat of the Order, governed and ruled all the knights scattered throughout Europe. The power of the Order was not diminished by the loss of their possessions in Palestine, as those territories cost them more to maintain than they brought in; they consoled themselves by means of the vast estates they held in all the kingdoms of Europe, and especially in France.

Nevertheless, from this island, where they maintained a well-organized naval force, they continued to wage war bravely against the infidels <note>original: *Ungläubigen*; a historical term used by the author to refer to Muslim forces</note> and protected the Greek states and other borders of Christendom.

<meta>This page serves as the formal opening of the "First Book" of the history. # History
### of the Abolition
# of the Order of the Knights Templar.

## First Book.

<margin>
1304.

I.
The state of the Knights Templar.
Dupuy, *The Condemnation of the Templars*. <note>original: "Düpüis, Verurtheilung der Tempelherren"; referring to Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar who wrote on the trial of the Templars</note>
Vertot, *History of the Knights Hospitaller*. <note>original: "Vertot, Geschichte der Johanniter Ritter"; referring to René-Aubert Vertot, an influential historian of the military orders</note>
Gürtler, *History of the Templars*. <note>original: "Gürtler, Geschichte der Tempelherren"; referring to Nicolaus Gürtler’s "Historia Templariorum" (1691)</note>
</margin>
The Order of the Knights Templar <term>Knights Templar: A wealthy and powerful medieval military order of "monk-soldiers" founded to protect pilgrims in the Holy Land</term> was never in such a flourishing state as it was at the beginning of the fourteenth century; their bravery, their conduct, and their wealth had earned them such precedence that they were esteemed as highly as kings and the greatest generals. They had rendered great services to Christendom in Palestine, and although they had been driven out of the Kingdom of Jerusalem in the year 1291 along with the other Christian nations, their glory had not suffered because of it, as it was quite evident that this misfortune could not be blamed on them. Their bravery was famous, and it was commonly said that a single Templar was capable of fighting and overcoming ten Saracens <note>original: "Sarazenen"; a term used in the Middle Ages by Europeans to refer to Muslims</note>.

<summary>This opening page of the first book establishes the immense prestige, wealth, and military reputation of the Knights Templar around 1304, shortly before their downfall.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, 1304, Fall of Acre 1291, Palestine, Jerusalem, Saracens, Pierre Dupuy, Abbé de Vertot, Nicolaus Gürtler</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Order of the Knights Templar at the height of its power around 1304. Following the discussion of their military reputation on the previous page, the narrative now shifts to their immense financial resources and the sophisticated hierarchical structure of their governance across Europe.</meta>

->— ) o ( —<-

<margin>1304.</margin>
Their wealth was equal to their bravery; it was immeasurable. The kings, who were convinced of their utility and regarded them as a bulwark of Christendom, had showered them with estates; they possessed nearly nine thousand commanderies <term>commanderies: the basic administrative divisions of the Order, consisting of an estate or manor managed by a "Commander" (Comthur)</term> throughout almost all European kingdoms; for in Asia, the Christians held only the kingdoms of Cyprus and Little Armenia. The infidels <note>original: "Ungläubigen"; a common historical term for the non-Christian forces in the Levant</note> had swallowed up the small states that the Crusaders had established there. Of these nine thousand commanderies, two thousand were in France, which had been, as it were, the cradle of the Order—not only because that nation had founded it, but also because the generosity of the kings had proven quite lavish toward the first knights, who were nearly all French. Their magnificent buildings, their splendid churches, and the pomp and order prevailing in their divine services were indeed admirable.

The Grand Master held the rank of a prince. He styled himself "by the grace of God" <note>original: "von Gottes Gnaden"; a prestigious formula typically used by sovereign rulers to denote that they answer only to a higher power</note>; he kept a court more numerous and more brilliant than those of the rulers of states. Two commanders were constantly with him as his assistants; with their help, he set the affairs of the Order in order and granted the vacant commanderies. The High Marshal was, in a manner of speaking, his Lieutenant General in matters of war. In every kingdom, there was a General Visitor, Grand Priors, and then the commanders.

<meta>catchword: To</meta>

<summary>This page describes the immense wealth of the Knights Templar, specifically their nine thousand commanderies across Europe, and details the sovereign-like hierarchy and court of the Grand Master.</summary>
<keywords>wealth, commanderies, France, Grand Master, High Marshal, Order hierarchy, Cyprus, Armenia</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Knights Templar's organizational structure in France and transitions into the historical account of the fall of Acre in 1291, focusing on the leadership of Grand Master Imbert de Beaujeu.</meta>

->— ) 0 ( —<-

<margin>1304.</margin>
In France, there were three Grand Priories <term>Grand Priory: a major administrative district of the Order, overseen by a Grand Prior who answered to the Grand Master</term>, namely those of France, Normandy, and Aquitaine.

The Grand Prior of France resided in Paris in a magnificent palace given the name of the Temple, as it is still called today. It was so splendid, and contained so many rooms, that the kings occasionally chose it for their residence and held their court there. <note>The Palais du Temple served as the Order's European treasury and headquarters; centuries later, it became famous as the prison of Louis XVI during the French Revolution.</note> Its revenues amounted to 10,000 francs—a very considerable sum for those times. Looking at the income the Grand Priory yields today, one can see that much must have been stripped from it, for there is no proportion between the incomes of the two eras; since the discovery of the Indies <note>original: "Entdeckung der Indien"; a reference to the arrival of gold and silver from the Americas and the expansion of trade routes, which drastically devalued currency through inflation</note>, the value of gold and silver has risen far beyond the ratio of one to six. Today, the Grand Priory brings in only 60,000 livres annually.

In the year 1281, Imbert de Beaujeu was Grand Master of the Order. His high birth and personal merit made him venerable to all of Europe, and these sentiments were also held regarding the Order itself, whose members were men of rank from every state. This gentleman was in Acre <note>original: "Acra"; a vital port city in the Levant and the last major stronghold of the Crusader states</note> when that city was besieged by the Sultan of Egypt, Melec Arafa <note>Historically identified as the Mamluk Sultan Al-Ashraf Khalil</note>. It was the only important place remaining to the Christians in the Orient; he defended it with unbelievable bravery, and it was only captured,

After they had been driven out of the Kingdom of Jerusalem in this manner, just as the Knights of St. John <term>Knights of St. John: also known as the Hospitallers, a military order founded to provide care for poor, sick, or injured pilgrims</term> were, both groups retired to Cyprus, where they held great estates. After him came Johann von Gaudin <note>The text likely refers to Theobald Gaudin, though the dates provided in the manuscript (1304) conflict with the historical record of his death in 1292</note>, who was still Grand Master in the year 1304. Roger had moved the headquarters of the Order to Limisso <note>Limassol</note>, a city in Cyprus belonging to the Order.

<margin>
II.
Vices of the
Templars.
The same.
</margin>
The great talents and brilliant qualities of these knights were balanced by equally great vices—indeed, these vices perhaps even surpassed their virtues. They possessed an unbearable pride, an insatiable greed, and lived in uncommonly great luxury. Their pride often entangled them in disputes with kings, whom they dared to resist and even to wage war against. Their greed drove them to amass riches, quite often in an unjust manner; and their prosperity had led them toward pleasure, toward the sensual lusts that follow it, and even toward debauchery. They kept a magnificent table, and for a long time there has been a common proverb: to drink like a

<margin>

III.
The King of France’s displeasure.
Mezerai, in the chronological excerpt. <note>François Eudes de Mézeray (1610–1683), a prominent French historian.</note>
Dupuis. <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), author of a famous history of the Templars' trial.</note>

They were feared by all rulers, who did not even dare to avenge the insults received from them; their boldness, or their recklessness, drove them to not even spare the King of France, whose character was certainly not one to allow himself to be insulted with impunity. This was Philip IV, nicknamed "the Fair" because of his incomparable beauty—a quality that was one of his least significant virtues. He was a prince who possessed infinite intelligence and sound judgment, who was well-versed in state affairs, a great general, and more powerful than any of his predecessors. Through his marriage to Joan of Évreux <note>The author appears to mean Joan I of Navarre (1273–1305). Philip IV married her in 1284, thereby acquiring Navarre and Champagne. Joan of Évreux was actually the wife of the later King Charles IV.</note>, he had united with his own states those of which she was the heiress: namely Navarre, the counties of Champagne and Brie, and many large estates that the House of Évreux held in France.

In addition to these qualities that make great kings, Philip, jealous of the rights of his crown, pushed them to the extreme. Steadfast in their defense, proud, bold, and defying the greatest dangers, he knew how far his power extended and set almost no limits to it. The war in which he was engaged with the Flemings <note>The people of Flanders, who were frequently in conflict with the French crown during this period.</note>, one of the most stubborn...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of King Philip IV's reign, specifically his financial struggles and the escalating conflict with Pope Boniface VIII, in which the Templars unwisely took the Pope's side.</meta>

->— ) 0 ( —<-

<margin>1304.</margin>
...me, and his involvement with a stubborn and unconquerable nation <note>Likely a reference to the King's long and costly wars with the Flemish or the English.</note> had forced him to burden his subjects with new taxes. Although he lost some of their affection because of this, they nonetheless bore them almost without a murmur out of fear.

The disputes between this lord and Pope Boniface VIII <note>Pope Boniface VIII (reigned 1294–1303) was a fierce proponent of papal supremacy, which led to a historic clash with Philip IV over the King's right to tax the clergy.</note> did honor to his courage and steadfastness. The matter was driven to the greatest extremes by both sides, and violence as well as established rights secured the King's victory over this unjust and irreconcilable Pope. The Pope did not lack supporters, among whom the Templars <note>original: "Tempelherren"</note> particularly distinguished themselves in an imprudent manner. Indeed, since the Order stood under the jurisdiction of the Papal See, was under its protection and dependent upon it, and hoped to receive continuous favors from it, it was not natural that they would imitate the French clergy, who had loudly declared themselves for their King. In contrast, the Knights—whose Order was spread throughout all European states—had to behave more cautiously toward the Pope; however, they should not have declared themselves for him so publicly. Failing to observe this political prudence, they not only took up his cause but also assisted him with money; and one among them, who was the Treasurer of the Royal Treasury <note>original: "Schatzmeister der Finanzkammer"</note>, even advanced a considerable sum to the Pope from his own coffers.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the growing rift between King Philip IV of France (the Fair) and the Knights Templar following the death of Pope Boniface VIII. The previous page established that the Templars had secretly funded the Pope against the King.</meta>

<margin>1304.</margin>
All of this took place in secret; however, everything becomes known at the court of a clever, watchful, and suspicious King; Philip received word of it. Extremely enraged, he sought every opportunity to take revenge for it. One soon presented itself, in which he perhaps exceeded the bounds of justice.

After Pope Boniface—presumably out of vexation over the insult done to him by the King's servants, Colonna and Nogaret <note>A reference to the "Outrage of Anagni" in 1303, where Guillaume de Nogaret and Sciarra Colonna captured and humiliated the Pope, who died shortly thereafter.</note>—had died, he was succeeded by Benedict XI. The latter did not engage in the chimerical project of his predecessor and was only intent on restoring a good relationship between France and the Papal See; for this reason, he even granted the King the tithe <note>A "tenth" part of income or produce; in this case, a tax levied on the clergy's land and assets.</note> from the church estates in his realm. The Knights Templar were free from this levy according to the privileges of their Order. The King began to show signs of his resentment toward them by ordering all their commanderies <note>original: "Comthureyen"; these were the administrative centers and estates managed by a Knight Commander.</note> to be included in the registers. Their protests against this were in vain; their tenants were forced to pay. The entire Order was thrown into unrest by this and took offense at it; they harbored a secret hatred against the King in their hearts, which was all the more intense because it had to be suppressed.

As the King’s finances were exhausted due to the heavy expenses incurred by the war in Flanders <note>The Franco-Flemish War (1297–1305), which put immense financial strain on the French crown.</note>, he resorted to a measure that is always harmful to states, and from whose initial benefit for the Prince there follows a disadvantage for the subjects that is very difficult to remedy. This consisted of a reminting of the currency <term>currency debasement: the practice of lowering the intrinsic value of coins (weight or purity) while maintaining their face value to increase the state's wealth</term>, which always begins with a devaluation and ends with an increase in the value of the new coins—which are nevertheless lower in weight and content than the old ones—for the benefit of the Prince.

The people, upon whom the entire loss fell, complained loudly and refused to bring their money to the mint. The Knights Templar became even more unwilling and indignant about it. They possessed large sums of money, as they were the wealthiest private individuals in France; their greed caused them to view this incident as the greatest misfortune that could befall them. They spoke very insolently. The people gathered together, and they spread insulting speeches against the King among the masses. These made the mob even bolder, so that they took up arms and sought to prevent the execution of the edict by force.

<summary>In 1304, King Philip IV's debasement of the French currency to pay for the war in Flanders sparked a violent uprising in Paris, which the wealthy and indignant Knights Templar helped incite.</summary>

The King flew into a furious rage when he found himself besieged and without provisions or enough soldiers. This lasted three days, during which he struggled significantly to defend the entrance to the Temple; but when the troops he had summoned finally arrived, the people, seized by fear, dispersed, and the uprising ended completely.

The King’s anger, however, did not cease immediately; the danger in which he had found himself and the arrogant boldness of the people led him to the decision to take revenge. The Governor reported that he had delayed the execution of the sentence for this reason and was awaiting the King’s command on the matter.

The King replied that this man should be sent to him, securely bound. He arrived in **Paris** and was brought before the King. Florian threw himself at the King’s feet and begged for his life. The King promised it to him, and even a reward, provided the information was as important as he claimed.

Thereupon Florian told him that he had been condemned to death alongside a renegade **Knight Templar**. In the Middle Ages, when a priest was unavailable, prisoners would sometimes perform a "lay confession" to one another, which is what Florian describes here.</note> to criminals—they had, as was common practice in such circumstances, confessed to one another. The hatred the King harbored against the Order, from which they had been expelled, was not unknown to them; perhaps Florian’s testimony had also become known. They sent word to the King that if he would pardon them, they would reveal various secret circumstances regarding the **Order of the Templars** <term>Tempelherren-Orden</term>, which until then had been buried in deep mystery. They gave a loathsome description of it and declared that every Knight of the Temple, upon his admission, first acknowledged that there was only one God, who had not died and does not die; secondly, that Jesus Christ was not God, but only a man who had suffered the death penalty on the cross for the sake of his crimes; thirdly, that as a result, they were made to deny Jesus Christ three times, spit on the cross, and trample it underfoot; and finally, regarding the **vow of chastity** <term>Gelübde der Keuschheit</term> they were made to take, the explanation was given to them that it applied only to women, but they were permitted not to observe it regarding their fellow brothers <note>This is a direct accusation of sodomy, which in the medieval period was a powerful legal tool used to justify the seizure of property and the dissolution of organizations.</note>, and that they began to impart this loathsome permission to them...

The King resolves to have the Order of the Templars abolished.

<margin>1305.</margin> ...was most deeply hated, so that he resolved to do everything in his power to effect its complete extermination.

If one were to suggest that Philip’s sensitivity regarding the insults he had suffered from the Order, and his self-serving intentions to profit from the spoils of a wealthy institution, had played some part in such a startling resolution, one would perhaps be asserting a bold proposition; yet one may judge this for themselves from the subsequent course of this history. One can assume, meanwhile, that the King was inwardly convinced of the corruption of the Order, and that the desire to abolish and punish these monstrous excesses <note>The "excesses" refer to the accusations of blasphemy and sodomy leveled against the knights.</note> had been his primary intention.

One must be astonished, however, that such a wise and insightful prince was not more attentive to the weakness of the evidence that was presented. Could one really rely on the testimony of Florian, who only reported what a man condemned to death had told him? This source had not been judicially questioned, could have been driven to confusion by the approaching execution, and in his deathbed statement <note>original: "teſtament de mort"; a final declaration made before death, often considered legally and spiritually binding</note>—in which he surely should have reported the matter for the peace of his conscience, since it concerned both the Church and the State—had said absolutely nothing about it?

-> — ) 0 ( — <-

<margin>1305.</margin>
As for the testimony of the two apostates <note>These "apostates" or defectors are generally identified as Noffo Dei, a Florentine, and Esquieu de Floyran.</note>, it was not impossible that they might have heard of Florian’s testimony; it was easy to see that they had only testified to these things to save their lives, and that, since the King’s hatred toward the Order was known to them, they believed they would do him a pleasure if they gave him weapons against his enemies. To this must be added that these two people accused themselves, and therefore, as participants in the same crimes, could not be heard against their accomplices <note>Under the legal standards of the time, the testimony of a self-confessed criminal was often considered inadmissible or "tainted" when used against others.</note>, and their accusation was suspicious because they had been expelled from the Order in a shameful manner.

The King believed them and formed the plan to eradicate the Knights Templar. This was a massive undertaking: to abolish so powerful an Order, so firmly established throughout all Europe, whose bravery was so famous, and which had rendered—and was still rendering—such great service to Christendom by warring against the infidels and securing trade throughout the entire **Mediterranean Sea**. These knights were dependent upon the Papal See, with which the King did not stand on very good terms, and which had a great interest in maintaining the Order.

1305.
Election of the [Grand Master] of the Temple.
<note>References to historians: Pierre Dupuy, François de Mézeray, Claude Fleury, and Nicolaus Gürtler.</note>
Dupuy.
Mezerai.
Fleuri.

The <term>chapter</term> <note>The governing body or assembly of the religious order.</note> assembled here to appoint a successor to him. This is conjectured from the behavior of the French court and from the movements made by the great figures of which it consisted, in order to steer the election toward someone who would be devoted to them. This great position of honor—which made those who held it equal to the highest regents, provided them with great income, and placed them in a position to make the fortunes of so many people of noble descent—was sought after by all the <term>Commanders</term> <note>original: "Comthuren." These were high-ranking officials in charge of regional districts or estates known as commanderies.</note>; in France, there was no great house that had not had one of its children admitted as a Knight of the Temple. Among them were counted the Montmorencys, Hangests, Longuevals, and even the sons of sovereign rulers.

The one for whom all these gentlemen exerted themselves was **Jacques de Molay**, Grand Prior of the Order; he was so well-loved and respected that it was easy for them to have him elected.

Molay was a younger son of one of the greatest houses in the County of **Burgundy**. His elder brother possessed large estates there and cut a great figure. Molay had entered the Order in his early youth, had earned a great name for himself within it, had risen through all the ranks, and had reached the <term>Grand Priory</term> <note>A high administrative office within the Order, ranking just below the Grand Master.</note>. He was a gentleman of true merit, brave, full of spirit, and of a gentle and moderate character; his morals were pure, and his conduct was beyond reproach. He had always [remained] at the French Court...

<margin>IX.</margin>
**Molay**, who had been elected so unanimously, lost no time; he left France and traveled to **Cyprus**, where he pursued the intentions of his predecessor to wage war with vigor against the unbelievers <note>A reference to the Saracens or Muslim forces in the Levant following the loss of the Holy Land.</note>.

Death of Pope **Benedict XI**.

He could accomplish this in no other way than with the help of the Pope, under whose protection the Order stood and upon whom it was dependent. <margin>XI.
Conclave at
Perugia.
Fleury <note>Likely a reference to Claude Fleury, a famous 17th-century French ecclesiastical historian whose works were standard references for church history.</note>.</margin>The **Cardinals** had entered the **Conclave** <note>The private meeting where Cardinals are locked away to elect a new Pope.</note> in **Perugia** on July 17, but not with particularly favorable sentiments toward the King. This was especially true for the Cardinals from the faction of **Pope Boniface**, who were indignant over his death—which they attributed to this Lord <note>Referring to King Philip IV, whose agents had physically assaulted and arrested Pope Boniface VIII shortly before he died.</note>—and wished only to elect a Pope who would be hostile to **France** and would defend and honor the memory of their benefactor. This party was the most numerous; however, the **Colonna** faction <note>A powerful Roman noble family that had been excommunicated by Boniface VIII and subsequently allied with the French King.</note>, which had declared for the King, was likewise very powerful, and without them, the election could not proceed. Since they were completely devoted to the King, they demanded a Pope who would be favorable to him and who would also suit their own interests, as they had made common cause with this Prince.

Because the interests of these two parties were directly opposed, they could in no way reach an agreement; their stubborn-

...stubbornness was insurmountable on both sides, and they spent ten months without being able to find a means that could bring them to an agreement. Finally, such a great scandal—by which the entire Church remained without a shepherd—made an impression on the *Colonnas* <note>A powerful Roman noble family and cardinal faction that often clashed with the Papacy.</note>; and since the opposing party had proposed the Archbishop of *Bordeaux*, they seemed inclined to agree to it, and only requested some time to make a decision.

The Archbishop of *Bordeaux* was named *Bertrand de Got* <note>The original German text writes "von Goust," a phonetic variation of "de Got." This man would eventually become Pope Clement V.</note>; he was from one of the best families of *Guienne* <note>The region of Aquitaine in southwestern France, which at this time was a duchy held by the Kings of England.</note>, a province belonging to the English at that time. He was a son of *Beraud*, Lord of *Got*, and had been one of *Boniface's* <note>Pope Boniface VIII, whose conflict with King Philip IV of France triggered this ecclesiastical crisis.</note> most zealous followers, who had given him a canonry at *Bordeaux* in 1300, appointed him Bishop of *Comminges* in 1301, and named him Archbishop of *Bordeaux* one year later.

The devotion of this prelate <note>A high-ranking clergyman, such as a bishop or archbishop.</note> toward *Boniface* had made him hated by the French court; and the Count of *Valois*, the King's brother, had—in order to punish him for it—devastated the lands of his <term>diocese|Kirchensprengel: the administrative district under a bishop's jurisdiction.</term> and especially those in which the Archbishop had rights and revenues. Since he was very self-interested, he had shown much resentment over this, and for various reasons, he was regarded as an enemy of the King. For this reason, the party of *Boniface* had proposed him.

The messenger hurried as much as possible and arrived in Paris in the middle of the month of May.

The King received this news with joy. The Archbishop was known to him as one of the best minds in his kingdom—a prelate of great spirit and insight—but also simultaneously as the most ambitious and greediest of all men. These last two qualities made the King believe that he could easily succeed in winning him over and obtaining everything he wanted from him. On the second day thereafter, he dispatched a messenger who delivered a letter to the Archbishop, written in the most gracious and friendly terms. The Archbishop, who was disgruntled with the French court and harbored a kind of coldness toward the King, was pleasantly surprised when he saw that this lord was seeking him out. His ambition led him to believe that the meeting could be favorable for him, and he traveled to the specified abbey within six days. The King was already there, showed him great courtesy, and even made several apologies for the complaints the Archbishop had reason to maintain against the **Count of Valois** <note>Charles of Valois, the King's brother, who had been involved in military campaigns in Italy and had clashed with the Archbishop's interests.</note>. He then told him that they were on the verge of electing a Pope in **Perugia** <note>The site of the 1304–1305 papal conclave, which lasted nearly a year due to the deadlock between pro-French and pro-Roman factions.</note>, that the election depended on him, and that he could direct it toward the Archbishop.

At the brilliant prospect of being elected Pope and seeing himself clothed in the highest dignity in the Christian world, the prelate was swept away by such great joy and so blinded by his future honor that he threw himself at the King’s feet. He begged the King to help him to this great elevation, giving him—should he attain it—the assurance of the greatest devotion and eternal gratitude, and promised to do everything for His Majesty and France that would depend on the supreme dignity he would have received through him.

These sentiments were very pleasing to the King; he answered him that he relied

<margin>1305.</margin>
on his promise and would have him elected; but under four conditions, which he disclosed to him. The first was to grant absolution to **Nogaret** <note>Guillaume de Nogaret, the King’s advisor who had led the daring assault on Pope Boniface VIII at Anagni in 1303.</note>, the lord who had taken Pope Boniface into custody; the second, to condemn the memory of this Pope; the third, to grant the King the <term>tithe</term> <note>A ten-percent tax on church income.</note> from all the clergy in Spain for five years. The fourth condition he did not tell him, but reserved the right to make it known to him at the appropriate time.

The Archbishop submitted to everything in order to attain the papal dignity; he promised everything and bound himself to it through the most solemn oath of religion, namely by the body of Jesus Christ. He took this terrible oath without any doubts of conscience, although it must have been very difficult for him to commit himself to a matter whose justice and possibility were unknown to him.

This fourth condition, which the King kept hidden from him, was the abolition of the **Order of the Knights Templar**. This lay so close to the King's heart—since he considered them guilty of all the crimes that the two apostates <note>Likely referring to Esquieu de Floyran and a companion, former Templars whose accusations of heresy and immorality sparked Philip's crusade against the Order.</note> had burdened them with—that this project occupied all his thoughts.

<margin>
XIV.
Coronation of the Pope.
Fleury <note>Likely a reference to Claude Fleury (1640–1723), a famous French ecclesiastical historian.</note>.

The Pope did not wish to travel to Italy. The constant unrest of the Roman people and the hostilities of the minor princes in the surrounding regions deterred him. He resolved to establish his residence in France and to move his court to **Avignon**, which at that time did not yet belong to the Papal See, but was a part of the lands of **Charles II, King of Naples**, a <term>vassal</term> <note>original: "Lehnsträger" - one who holds land from a superior lord in exchange for loyalty and service.</note> of the Papal See. He commanded the College of Cardinals to travel to **Lyon**, where he intended to be crowned. He arrived there with a procession and retinue befitting the dignity of a Pope. The King likewise traveled there, accompanied by the most prominent lords of France. The Cardinals obeyed the Pope, and this coronation was one of the most magnificent festivities ever seen. It took place on the 14th of November. Nevertheless, a distressing incident occurred during the event, which was seen as a somber omen for his <term>pontificate</term> <note>The period of a Pope's reign.</note>. XV. A few days later, the King met in private with the Pope and presented him with the fourth demand, which he had not previously made known to him. This was one of the conditions of his election, and one to which he had finally bound himself. This was the abolition of the Order of the Knights Templar. He gave him news of the ruin of the entire Order as a settled matter, speaking of the abominations of which they were accused, and of which he told the Pope he had certain proof. He added that this would be a terrible scandal for religion, whose sanctity demanded this sacrifice.

The Pope was extraordinarily taken aback by such a proposal. It seemed impossible to him to abolish the most famous of all military orders—the one which was the most useful in Christendom and, as it were, its bulwark—an Order that had spread into all the states of Christian princes and consisted of the high nobility of all realms. He was terrified by it and only now realized the audacity and recklessness of an oath which he had sworn without knowing what he was promising, out of a...

The obscurity and uncertainty of these crimes, and this oath—which according to all laws was invalid and somewhat similar to the oath of *Herod* <note>A reference to the biblical King Herod, who made a reckless promise to Salome that forced him to execute John the Baptist against his better judgment.</note>—moved the Pope, who had enough insight to believe that he was released from keeping it; however, he would thereby have come into conflict with a proud, absolute, and vengeful prince who had triumphed over Pope *Boniface* <note>Pope Boniface VIII (r. 1294–1303), who was famously assaulted and humiliated by King Philip’s agents at Anagni, leading to his death shortly thereafter.</note>, the proudest of all men, and one who was supported by almost the entire Christian world. The Pope's power was not yet firmly enough established, and he had everything to fear from the sensitivity <note>In this 18th-century context, "Empfindlichkeit" refers to the King’s volatile temperament and his tendency to take personal offense at political resistance.</note> of this domineering king.

Fear and political prudence <term>political prudence|German: *Staatsklugheit*, the practice of governing through diplomatic caution and strategic pragmatism</term> thus prevented the Pope from letting the King see the reluctance he felt in granting him this request. He dissembled and gave a gentle answer, saying that this was a weighty matter which deserved to be considered and investigated; he did not reject it outright, but hoped much from time and the events that might occur, especially since the other powers also [took an interest] in the fate of the knights...

Several months passed without the King taking a single step; he wished to give the Pope time to make his arrangements and to establish his authority. But when the Pope arrived in Poitiers with his court, the King—delighted to have him in the heart of his realm and, so to speak, within his own hands—ordered the Prosecutor General <term>Prosecutor General|German: *General-Procurator*, the high legal officer of the French crown</term> to send him the testimony of Florian <note>Esquieu de Floyran, the man often credited with first bringing the accusations against the Templars to King Philip.</note> and the statements of the two apostate Knights Templar. He accompanied these legal documents with a pressing letter to bring the Pope to a decision.

The Pope read them and was astonished by these grave accusations; and, whether it was that he truly believed them, or that he could not be released from satisfying his obligations to the King, he decided to begin this important trial with prudence and the observation of the deepest silence regarding his intent.

Prince Guy was born in 1285, and as the Dauphin and the Dauphine loved him dearly, when they made the division among their children in the year 1292, they assigned as his share the Barony of Montauban with 200 <term>Livres</term> <note>The primary currency of France during this period</note> in rents from landed estates, and 15,000 Livres in ready cash. He distinguished himself to such an extent that around the year 1304, he was appointed to the Grand Priorate of Normandy.

He held frequent audiences with him; he seemed to take pleasure in his intellect and his character. He spoke with him about his desire to once again aid the cause of Christendom in Palestine; together they considered the means to be successful in this, and he proposed the method of a <term>Crusade</term> <note>A religious military campaign intended to reclaim the Holy Land for Western Christianity</note>, despite the far from fortunate outcomes of all those that had been undertaken over the previous two centuries.

He also revealed another plan he had devised, which he perhaps believed would be beneficial for this restoration. It consisted of merging all the <term>Military Orders</term> <note>Religious organizations of knights, such as the Templars and the Hospitallers, who took monastic vows but also fought in defense of the faith</note> together; having become more powerful through this union, they might have maintained themselves by their own strength and would not have required external assistance. He shared this proposal with the <term>Grand Master</term> <note>The supreme head of the Order of the Temple, at this time Jacques de Molay</note>, asking him to examine it and provide his opinion.

It remains unknown whether these deliberations were sincere, or whether the Pope—who found the abolition proposed by the King <note>King Philip IV of France, also known as Philip the Fair</note> repugnant, even if he had perhaps consented to it with too much compliance—hoped that by uniting these orders, the <term>Knights Templar</term> could be cleared of the crimes of which they were accused, thereby thwarting the King’s designs. Alternatively, he may have made this proposal to the Grand Master merely to stall him, to deceive him, and the deadly blow,

Be that as it may, Molay examined this draft with great attention and responded to it with a treatise that he delivered to the Pope. It contained irrefutable reasons that made this union impossible; these were: the differences in the <term>Rules of the Orders</term> <note>The specific codes of conduct and religious statutes governing each group, such as the Rule of Saint Benedict or the Rule of Saint Augustine</note>, the inequality of their assets, the jealousy between the heads of the Orders, the resistance of kings and princes, and the opposing characters and mindsets of the different nations, as some of these Orders—for example, those in Germany and Spain—consisted of the subjects of a single sovereign. The Pope listened to his opinion and seemed to abandon a project that was exposed to so many difficulties.

<margin>The rumor of the crimes of the Templars spreads. <term>Düpüy</term> <note>A reference to Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar who wrote an influential history of the Templars' trial</note>.</margin> The Pope and the King very carefully concealed their intention to abolish the Order of the Temple; however, the rumor of the crimes specified in the denunciation by the two <term>apostates</term> <note>Likely referring to Esquieu de Floyran and a companion, former Templars whose accusations of heresy and blasphemy triggered the King's investigation</note> had already become known. Too many people knew of it for it to remain a secret. It reached the ears of the <term>Grand Master</term> and the other knights while they were in Poitiers; they complained about it to the Pope as a hideous slander. They begged him to conduct an investigation to inform himself of the truth, and they submitted themselves to the severest punishments if they were found guilty. The Pope did not answer; his si-

This proposal <note>referring to the suggestion to merge the military orders</note> seemed to be nothing more than an opportunity to seize all the property of the **Templars**, the use and allocation of which could only be settled with great difficulty. The **Pope** made this proposal only with the intention of preventing the **King** from taking all the <term>commanderates</term> <note>original: "Comthureyen"; these were the administrative districts or estates managed by the military orders</note> for himself. The King, who had his own secret intentions, but who primarily considered the Templars to be loathsome people and wished to abolish the Order, seemed to agree to it. It was therefore decided between the Pope and the King, although the Pope always insisted on the condition that the crimes should be proven by means of a lawful investigation.

<margin>
Measures taken by the King against the Templars. <term>Düpuy</term>, <term>Fleuri</term>, <term>Vertot</term>, <term>Gürtler</term> <note>These refer to historical chroniclers and legal scholars: Pierre Dupuy, Claude Fleury, René-Aubert Vertot, and Nicolaus Gürtler</note>.
</margin>
Regarding the regularity of these procedures, the King thought quite differently than the Pope. He believed that by observing formalities, their length and procedure would provide the guilty with a means to escape justice; that through <term>chicanery</term> <note>the use of legal trickery or petty arguments to delay a process</note>, the rejection of judges, and appeals, they would prolong the trial indefinitely, and that the knights spread throughout the Christian world would find protectors and create obstacles in every kingdom, through which they would undoubtedly be saved.

To him, therefore, the Pope's consent to bring them to trial was enough; from

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of King Philip IV's aggressive legal strategy against the Knights Templar, moving from the previous page's discussion of legal formalities to the King’s decision to bypass standard criminal procedures in favor of a coordinated, secret strike.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>— ) 0 ( —</header>

<margin>1306.</margin>
Regarding the manner of conducting the case, he himself provided the interpretation; and as hatred provided him with the necessary means, he devised a method of proceeding for which there were no existing precedents, and for which there may perhaps never be another example. He assembled his **Privy Council** <note>original: "geheimen Rath"; a body of advisors chosen by the King to oversee state affairs</note>, which was chaired by **William of Nogaret**, a relative of the man who had rendered such faithful service to him against **Pope Boniface** <note>referring to Pope Boniface VIII, with whom King Philip IV had a famously bitter and violent conflict</note>, and whom he trusted completely because of his skill and because he conformed to the King's will in everything.

<margin>Result of the Royal Privy Council. The same authors.</margin>
In this council, it was decided that they would not proceed according to the usual rules applied in criminal trials. Instead, they would have to have all the **Knights Templar** throughout all of France taken into custody on a single day and at the same hour, without them knowing anything of it beforehand. They were to be kept separated in various prisons, and at the same time, all of their assets and property were to be seized to prevent their friends and followers from using these resources for the Order's benefit.

<margin>1307.</margin>
At the same time, it was decided to inform all other Kings of what was intended in France, to give them notice of the crimes of the Knights and of the evidence they held against them, and to urge and exhort all these princes to imitate the King by proceeding in the same way in their own states by seizing the persons and property of the Templars. This was a certain way to strike terror into all of them, and [to deprive them] of protection
and

<meta>catchword: und</meta>

<summary>King Philip IV and his advisor William of Nogaret deviate from standard legal practices to orchestrate a simultaneous, nationwide mass arrest of the Knights Templar and the seizure of their wealth. The plan also includes an international diplomatic campaign to convince other European monarchs to follow suit, effectively isolating the Order.</summary>

<keywords>King Philip IV, William of Nogaret, Knights Templar, mass arrest, asset seizure, Privy Council, Pope Boniface VIII, criminal procedure, 1307, diplomatic strategy</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the plot against the Knights Templar, following the decision of King Philip IV's Privy Council to coordinate a secret, nationwide arrest. It details the logistical execution of these orders and the absolute secrecy required to prevent the Order from defending itself or appealing to the Pope.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
->— ) o ( —<-

<margin>1307.</margin>
and to deprive them of the assistance they might have expected from these Kings and from the other Knights, their fellow brothers. To execute these orders, impenetrable secrecy was necessary; and this was observed with the greatest precision. Had it not happened this way, the Templars could have taken measures among themselves, as well as with the Pope, to oppose it. They could have hidden, taken flight, or even placed themselves in a state of defense <note>The Templars were a highly trained military elite; King Philip feared that if they had warning, they could have fortified their commanderies and started a civil war</note>, and thereby thwarted this terrible execution.

<margin>Orders to take all Knights Templar in France into custody.</margin>
As a result of this decision, orders were issued to all provincial governors, to all bailiffs <term>Amtleute: local administrative and judicial officials representing the crown</term> and district governors <term>Landvögte: high-ranking officials who exercised the King's authority over a specific territory</term>, and to all magistrates to take into custody the Knights Templar who were within their jurisdiction. They were instructed to use force for this purpose and to be accompanied by soldiers or such men as were under their command. They were ordered to bring these Knights into good and secure custody and then immediately to seize their movable and immovable property, to take inventories of it, and to appoint commissioners over it to manage it and provide an accounting of its value.

These orders were terrible, and the execution of them was commanded under penalty of death; they were sent out sealed, with the prohibition not to open them until the night between the 12th and 13th of October, which day was set for this undertaking throughout all of France <note>This event—Friday, October 13, 1307—is often cited in popular history as a possible origin for the superstition surrounding Friday the 13th</note>.

<summary>This page details the logistical coordination and extreme secrecy behind the mass arrest of the Knights Templar across France, ensuring they could not flee or use their wealth to defend themselves.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, King Philip IV, mass arrest, 1307, property seizure, secrecy, French administration, Friday the 13th</keywords>

<margin>1307.</margin>
At the same time, letters were prepared for foreign princes, in which the King informed them of what he had done, and he entreated and exhorted them to do the same in their own states, to rid them and all of Christendom of an Order guilty of such abominable crimes. In these letters, as well as in the orders dispatched to the magistrates in France, it was further added that everything was being done based on an agreement reached with the Pope and on the advice of all the high nobles of the realm. <note>King Philip IV was strategically using the Pope’s name to grant his actions religious authority, even though the Pope was initially hesitant about the King's aggressive timeline.</note>

These couriers departed on different days according to the distance of their destinations. The princes to whom they were dispatched were the King of the Romans <note>The title used by the German ruler who was chosen by the electors but not yet crowned Emperor by the Pope</note>, the King of Naples, the King of England, the King of Castile, the King of Aragon, the King of Navarre, the King of Portugal, the Prince-Electors <term>Churfürsten: the elite group of German princes who held the right to elect the King of the Romans</term>, the Italian princes, and the Count of Flanders. It is not reported that letters were sent to the northern crowns where the Knights Templar had no estates. In those regions, and in the parts of Germany bordering those kingdoms, the Teutonic Knights <note>A German medieval military order; they were the primary rivals to the Templars' influence in Northern and Eastern Europe</note> had for a long time possessed various cities and several fine commanderies <term>Comthureyen: the basic administrative divisions and estates of a military order, each managed by a Commander</term>.

<margin>
The Templars are taken into custody in Paris.
</margin>
The day for the terrible execution of this plan drew near, without the Grand Master, the Prince Dauphin, or the commanders having the slightest suspicion of it. They appeared at court and saw the King

...frequently, especially the Grand Master <note>Jacques de Molay (c. 1244–1314), the last Grand Master of the Knights Templar</note>, who until then had received so many marks of his [the King's] favor, and Pairaud, Grand Prior in France, who had served as the General Financier. The King continued to maintain his deception. Meanwhile, at the beginning of October, the Grand Master noticed that the King received and welcomed him only coldly and with a look of displeasure. Since he had nothing with which to reproach himself, this change was incomprehensible to him. The crimes of which the entire Order was accused were not unknown to him; however, since he knew they were groundless, he did not think for a moment that an investigation would be conducted in such a violent manner.

Finally, the fateful day arrived, set for October 13th. The evening before, the King had already issued his orders and made all preparations. At daybreak on the 13th, the Temple <note>The fortified headquarters of the Knights Templar in Paris</note> was occupied, and they arrested the Grand Master, the Grand Prior of France, Pairaud, the Prince-Dauphin, who was Grand Prior of Normandy, the Grand Prior of Aquitaine, and all the Commanders <term>Comthuren: the administrative officers in charge of the Order’s local estates or "commanderies"</term> and knights residing there, 140 in number. They were brought to various prisons, and most to the castle at Melun. One can easily imagine how great their astonishment and terror must have been. All of Paris fell into unrest and the greatest wonder when it saw people of such rank, from the premier families in France, who were honored by everyone and

...and were highly esteemed, and against whom no crime could be charged, being led to prison. At the arrest of all these gentlemen of the Templar Order, one could believe nothing else but that a conspiracy against the state had been in the works.

<margin>The King takes possession of the Temple, the treasures, and the estates of the Order. The same <note>Refers to the historical sources cited on the previous page (Dupuy, Gürtler, etc.).</note>.</margin>
Hardly had the Grand Master and those who were taken into custody with him arrived in their prison, when the King went to the Temple <note>The "Temple" was the fortified Paris headquarters of the Order, a massive complex that served as a bank and a fortress.</note>, which he chose as his residence, and seized all the gold and silver that was in the treasury, all the rich furnishings in the palace, and even the deeds concerning their estates. It is not reported whether he had a written report of this drawn up, nor whether he had an inventory of the household goods and the documents taken. This much is certain: this step did him no honor, and it revealed that he was the sole author of this violent proceeding. He did even more; since he regarded the Templars as already condemned, he sought to show that he was intentionally taking up residence in the Temple by having the money of the Royal Treasury and the archives of the realm brought there.

In the entire province of Isle de France <note>The region surrounding Paris, the heart of the French royal domain.</note>, the royal commissioners located everywhere took possession of the commanderies <term>Commanderies: The local estates and administrative centers where the Templars managed their lands and collected revenue.</term>, secured all household goods and assets, and claimed the revenues for themselves, so that they could only be paid to them. They did, however, create inventories of the movable property—

<margin>
All Knights Templar throughout France are arrested.
Dupuis <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar whose work on the trial of the Templars is a primary historical source.</note>.
</margin>
Throughout the entire realm, the royal governors and officials opened their sealed packets <note>To ensure the element of surprise, King Philip IV sent out secret orders to be opened simultaneously across the country.</note> during the night from the 12th to the 13th of October; they found within them the orders against the Knights Templar and immediately took measures to execute them, calling upon the aid of everyone at their command. On the morning of the 13th, the houses where the knights were located were surrounded; they were all taken into custody and led to prison. They offered no resistance whatsoever, as they had not the slightest suspicion of their misfortune and lived separately from one another.

The exact number of those taken into custody is not known; however, it must have been very large, both because of the vastness of the realm and because France was the state that provided the Order with the most knights. Twenty-eight are recorded in Normandy, namely thirteen at Caen, ten at Pont-de-l'Arche, and five at Bayeux; three at Troyes; twelve in Languedoc, namely six at Carcassonne and just as many at Beaucaire; seven at Cahors and eleven at Bigorre. From the other provinces, there are no detailed records; however, it is not to be doubted—

...doubt <note>The OCR "feln" is the end of the German word "zweifeln" (to doubt), continuing from the previous page.</note> that their number was very great, especially in Burgundy, where the Dukes had always been favorable to them.

<margin>All their property is seized.</margin>
On the following day, the same formalities were observed according to the model of what had occurred in Isle de France <note>The region surrounding Paris, the historic heart of the French royal domain.</note>, and in accordance with the Royal command. Everything was taken into possession, and the Order was stripped of all its property. Commissioners were appointed everywhere; in the bailiwick <note>The administrative district under the jurisdiction of a "Vogt" or bailiff.</note> of Caen, this task was given to Hugo du Chatel and to Gautier of Boisgilon, two gentlemen who styled themselves knights; under them, Wilhelm of Fontenoy and Robert de la Planque of Tornebus were appointed as overseers.

<margin>Names of the one hundred and forty Knights Templar taken into custody in Paris.</margin>
It is only fitting to leave to posterity the names of the one hundred and forty Knights Templar taken into custody at Paris. From these, one can learn the names of the great families of France at that time, and those descendants who still remain today need not blush at the association. They could have no better proof of their ancient lineage. There were few illustrious families in which there were no Knights Templar. One found the families of Montmorency, Hanget, Longueval, Raineval, du Plessis, and Hondelot among them; nevertheless, none of these specific names are found among the one hundred and forty seized in Paris, whether because they were not present in Paris at the time or had been exempted from the general order.

All these knights were held in various prisons in Paris and the surrounding area, particularly in Melun. They were not all separated from one another, as there were not enough prisons available for 140 prisoners; however, for each location, certain gentlemen were appointed who were given the task of guarding them so that none could escape. It has been noted that Hugo de la Celle and William of Marsilly held this office, which brought very little honor <note>Guarding prisoners was often seen as beneath the dignity of high-ranking knights, reflecting the humiliating nature of the Templars' downfall.</note>. They had subordinates who were nevertheless also men of standing, as they are called knights in the records. These were Philip Coquerel, Girard Robert, William of Bretigny, John of Boisesmont, Imbert of Saint Jara, and John Pitard. Their salaries were fixed.

<margin>
The Confessor, Inquisitor of the Faith. Dupuy <note>Original: "Düpüis." This likely refers to Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar who wrote an influential history of the Templar trial.</note>.
</margin>

Over all of these was placed the King’s confessor, William of Paris, a Dominican and Inquisitor of the Faith, who acted as their Inspector General. This was a learned man who possessed the King's complete trust, but who was also so blindly devoted to him that the prince's will was law to him. Everything had been arranged between them—that is, between the King and the confessor—to whom one can also add Nogaret <note>Guillaume de Nogaret, King Philip IV's chief minister and a master of legal maneuver, who was instrumental in the arrests.</note>, one of the most distinguished ministers, who [shared] the intentions

<margin>1307. Mezeray <note>François Eudes de Mézeray (1610–1683) was a prominent French historian. This margin cites his historical accounts as a source.</note>.</margin>
...sought just as much to advance the King’s interests. The Royal Confessor <note>Referring to William of Paris, mentioned on the previous page.</note> had traveled to Melun, where the majority of the prisoners were held. Here he applied the greatest effort to ensure they were well-guarded; he visited them frequently, conversed with them, gave them to understand what kind of statements they ought to make, and sought, at least in appearance, to reassure them in order to make the King’s intentions understandable to them, as well as the price for which they might regain their freedom. In his capacity as Inquisitor, he made use of their responses to provide testimony on the occasion, which, by virtue of his office, was bound to be all the more powerful.

<margin>Preparations for the interrogation. Dupuy <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651) was a French scholar whose history of the Templar trial serves as a primary source for this manuscript.</note>.</margin>
All these measures were intended to organize the interrogation into which the King wished to draw the prisoners, and to align it with the thoughts of this master, who remained fully convinced of the crimes of the Knights Templar and the ruin of the Order *). As the time for this formality approached, he made all these crimes known and had a petition presented to him by the citizens of Paris, in the name of the French nation, in which the atrocities of the Templars were loathed and he was asked to proceed against them with the utmost severity.

*) Or at least he gave the appearance of being convinced. The interrogation was a mere formality to give the appearance of legality to the abominable proceedings against the Templars. <note>Original: "Tempel-ren," a truncated version of "Tempelherren" due to the line break.</note> Note by the translator.

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding King Philip IV of France’s campaign against the Knights Templar. While the previous page described the psychological manipulation of the prisoners by the Royal Confessor, William of Paris, this page details the specific, scandalous charges leveled against them to turn public opinion and the formal organization of the inquisitorial trials across France in late 1307.</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>

-> — ) 0 ( — 59 <-

<margin>1307.</margin>
The (alleged) monstrous crimes of these knights were made public: their denial of *Jesus Christ*, their contempt for his cross—upon which they supposedly spat three times—and the permission of sodomy <note>In the Middle Ages, "sodomy" was a broad term for non-procreative sexual acts; these charges were standard tools used by the Inquisition to demonize groups and justify their dissolution.</note>. It was claimed that their vow of chastity was restricted only to avoiding contact with women, and that they prepared for these crimes through shameful kisses. Finally, they were accused of idolatry, asserting that they worshipped an idol with a gilded head <note>This refers to the infamous "Baphomet," a mysterious idol the Templars were accused of worshipping, though modern historians find little evidence it actually existed outside of forced confessions.</note> and girded themselves with a thin cord that had touched this idol, which they regarded as an amulet.

The Royal Confessor <note>William of Paris, who was both the King's confessor and the Grand Inquisitor of France.</note> was given the task of interrogating the one hundred and forty Templars taken into custody in *Paris*. He claimed that this duty fell to him as the Inquisitor of the Faith and as a delegate of the Pope. In this capacity, he appointed commissioners for the various provinces of the kingdom to simultaneously interrogate the knights imprisoned there. To make these proceedings appear more authentic <note>By "authentic," the author suggests the King wanted the trials to have the appearance of legal and religious legitimacy to satisfy the public and the Church.</note>, the King ordered all bailiffs and governors to attend the sessions alongside the local nobility.

We do not possess the records for all these interrogations; however, from those that have been preserved, one can easily judge the nature of the others. The records that remain are the interrogations from *Paris*, *Caen*, *Pont-de-l'Arche*, *Cahors*, and *Carcassonne*. The interrogation in *Paris* began at the end of *October*,

<meta>The text continues on the next page with the word "and" (und), likely describing the specific testimonies or the expansion of the trials.</meta>

<keywords>Knights Templar, Philip IV, William of Paris, Inquisition, heresy, Baphomet, 1307, trial records, Paris</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the account of the legal proceedings against the Knights Templar in 1307. Interrogation in
Paris.
Dupuy.
Gürtler.
<note>Reference to historians Pierre Dupuy and Nicolaus Gürtler, whose 17th-century works are primary sources for the trial records.</note>
</margin> This interrogation, which was conducted in Paris and the surrounding area, was the most important, and without a doubt the one upon which the fate of the Order had to depend. This was because one hundred and forty witnesses were to be heard and confronted with the most prominent Templars: the Grand Master <note>Jacques de Molay, the last Grand Master of the Knights Templar.</note>, the Grand Prior of France—who was also called the Grand Commander—the Grand Priors of Normandy and Aquitaine, and the other knights, all of whom came from the most noble families in France. The Inquisitor of the Faith <note>William of Paris, the Dominican monk serving as the Chief Inquisitor.</note> traveled, by all appearances, first to Melun, where the greatest number of prisoners were held. All of them had been informed that the King desired them to confess to the crimes of which the Order was accused. He was accompanied by those gentlemen whom the King had appointed to attend the interrogation. He first read to them the articles upon which he wished to question them and took an oath from them that they would all tell the truth <note>An absurd and irrational procedure! How could one hope that people, who were accused of the most abominable crimes—denial and contempt of the Savior of the world, <term>sodomy</term> <gloss>In this context, a broad medieval term for non-procreative sexual acts used to demonize the accused.</gloss>, and idolatry—would be moved by an oath to tell the truth? Note by the original translator.</note>; and after this, he brought them into the interrogation one by one.

<margin>1307.</margin>
<margin>The Torture.</margin>
<margin>Gürtler. <note>Nicolaus Gürtler, a 17th-century scholar and author of a history of the Templars.</note></margin>
<margin>The Spirit of the Laws. <note>original: "l'Esprit des Loix," the 1751 work by Montesquieu which criticized legal abuses and the use of torture.</note></margin>
<margin>Vertot. <note>René-Aubert Vertot, an 18th-century French historian known for his chronicles of the Knights of Malta and the Templars.</note></margin>
To this formality was added a terrible spectacle, namely the instruments of torture, which were to be used against those who would not speak the truth, or rather, who refused to willingly confess to the crimes of which they were accused. Since almost all of them denied these charges, they were put to the rack *) <note>A torture device consisting of a rectangular frame with rollers at both ends; the victim's wrists and ankles were bound to the rollers and stretched, often to the point of joint dislocation.</note> which was executed upon them in such a violent and forceful manner that the limbs of many were dislocated, so that they raised a dreadful cry, and the surrounding areas echoed with their howling and lamentation. The most steadfast endured this torture for a long time and confessed nothing, so that they were brought back to their prisons completely broken; several died there while professing their innocence and the purity of their faith. Those who were not as courageous finally yielded to the pain after suffering for a long time and confessed a portion of that with which they were charged. Those

---

*) The testimony of two villains expelled from the <term>Order</term> <gloss>The Poor Fellow-Soldiers of Christ and of the Temple of Solomon, known as the Knights Templar.</gloss>, and of another villain who had forfeited his life <note>Referring to a criminal facing execution who was granted a reprieve in exchange for false testimony.</note>, were thus considered sufficient, without any other evidence, to force men—venerable by their status and virtues and held in the highest regard by everyone—through the most cruel tortures to confess to such crimes of which they had in no way made themselves suspect throughout their lives. Only a <term>Dominican monk</term> <gloss>A member of the Order of Preachers; they were the primary administrators of the Medieval Inquisition.</gloss> and Inquisitor of the Faith, and a cruel, greedy King filled with hatred, could proceed in such a way. — Note by the Translator.
