<page-num>62</page-num>

<header>— ) o ( —</header>

<margin>1307.</margin> Those, however, who possessed the least **resolve** did not wait so long as to be brought to the **rack**; they testified to whatever they were asked, primarily because they were led to understand that they must do so to please the King <note>King Philip IV of France, who orchestrated the downfall of the Templars to seize their assets and cancel his debts.</note>, and they were assured that they would remain unpunished and regain their **freedom**. Meanwhile, it is not established that they all testified against the truth; for it could also be that the refusal to confess these crimes was based on the shame of such a confession and the fear of punishment *).

Such was the nature of this **interrogation**, and these were the methods of persuasion and exhortation employed by a priest, a cleric, or an **Inquisitor of the Faith** to bring the accused to a **confession** of the truth. Such a **proceeding** was as unheard of as it was hateful, and it rendered their testimonies extremely suspicious. Indeed, is there any example of starting by putting the accused to the **rack** when there is no **proof** against them, subjecting them to a punishment that is only appropriate for a proven, or at least half-proven, crime **)?

---

*) The author could have spared this remark. Given the unheard-of procedure, one can always assume that everyone testified against the truth; for they blamed the entire Order, which might perhaps have been true of only some members. — *Note by the Translator.*

**) A half-proof is no proof at all, and the doctrine of half-proofs, quarter-proofs, and so forth <note>original: "Lehre von halben, von viertel Beweiſen." This refers to the medieval legal system of "legal proofs" (probatio semiplena), where judges mathematically added up fragments of evidence to justify the use of torture.</note>

<meta>This page continues the legal and moral critique of the interrogations of the Knights Templar initiated on page 62. It transitions from describing the specific conditions of the 1307 trials to a broader historical and comparative analysis of torture in judicial systems (Athens, Rome, and England), before returning to the specific testimonies of the 140 knights.</meta>

<page-num>63</page-num>

<header>— ( . ) ( —</header>

<margin>1307.</margin>
**Torture**, even when applied following a judicial verdict, is always a dubious path, and brings both the false and the true to light, as the intensity of the **torment** overcomes human weakness. In Athens, it was used only for the crime of **treason** <note>original: "beleidigten Majestät," referring to *crimen laesae maiestatis* or injured majesty.</note>, and when it was a matter of discovering **accomplices**, it was used only thirty days after their conviction.

In Rome, there was no **preparatory torture** *); birth, rank, and military positions always protected against it, except in the case of the sole crime against the state; and even now, this cruel procedure is not in use in England, where the throne of **liberty** resides.

The testimony of the one hundred and forty **Knights Templar** after this terrible preparation—both of those who actually endured the torture and those who avoided it through a voluntary confession to escape the torment that they saw some of their

---

<note>This sentence fragment completes the thought from the previous page regarding legal doctrines.</note> ...which should be entirely banished from the textbooks of the **legal scholars**. <note>Note by the Translator.</note>

*) Among free people, it was certainly not common; however, the testimonies of servants <note>original: "Knechte," referring to the enslaved or unfree class in Roman law.</note> were not considered valid unless they were confirmed by torture. <note>Note by the Translator.</note>

<summary>This page critiques the use of torture as a judicial tool, comparing its restricted use in ancient Athens and Rome and its absence in England to the brutal treatment of 140 Knights Templar. The author and translator argue that torture is unreliable because physical pain overpowers human resolve, leading to false confessions.</summary>
<keywords>torture, Knights Templar, legal history, Athens, Rome, England, treason, judicial procedures, 1307, human weakness</keywords>

<margin>The denial of Jesus Christ and of the cross. Dupuy. <note>original: "Düpuy," referring to Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar whose work *Histoire de l'Ordre Militaire des Templiers* is a primary source for the trial records.</note></margin>
Among the one hundred and forty **Knights Templar** brought for interrogation, there were one hundred and twenty-six who confessed that, upon their admission into the Order, they were made to deny **Jesus Christ** and spit upon the cross three times; they admitted that they had done so. These are Nos. 1, 2, 3, — 5 through 23 inclusive; 27 through 57; 59 through 63; 67, 69 through 77; 79 through 97; 99 through 114; and 116 through 140.

It must be noted that among these one hundred and twenty-six knights, there were sixteen who declared that they performed this denial and spat upon the cross only because of the violent proceedings and the coercion used against them. These are Nos. 1, 2, 7, 17, 52, 53, 63, 64, 68, 77, 81, 89, 103, 112, 126, 139.

The 64th, **Pierre de Villars**, testified that he only denied [Christ] after he had been locked in a prison for a day and a night.

The 68th, **Matthieu du Quesnoy**, said they had placed him on bread and water for three days.

The 77th, **Constantin de Viciac**, testified that, in order to obtain his consent, they had forcibly dragged him through the entire room.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the charges of idolatry brought against the Knights Templar in 1307. The previous page ended with a mention of the idol, and this page opens with a physical description of the alleged object of worship.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>
<header>) 0 (</header>

<margin>1307.</margin>
...idol, which had two carbuncles <note>original: "Carfunkel"; deep red gemstones, historically rumored to glow in the dark like coals.</note> instead of eyes, as well as a large beard, and moreover a hideous form, four feet, two in front and two behind; that it only appeared during the General Chapters <note>The formal assembly of the high-ranking members of the Order.</note>, where all the knights had worshiped it by removing their caps and falling down before it. *)

It is not reported whether it was kept in a specific city, although various knights name Montpellier. If it were located only in this single city, then this "mystery of iniquity" <note>original: "Geheimniß der Ungerechtigkeit"; a biblical reference to 2 Thessalonians 2:7, here used to describe the alleged secret corruption of the Order.</note> would have taken place only in this one city; for General Chapters were also held in other places, and either more similar idols must have existed, or the idolatry was practiced only in France.

---

> *) How is this confession of idolatry to be reconciled with the testimony of the two expelled knights, Monfaucon and Noffodei <note>Esquin de Floyran and Noffo Dei, the two primary informers whose accusations against the Templars provided King Philip IV with the pretext for their arrest.</note>, that at their reception the knights had to acknowledge "that there is only one God, who has not died, nor can die"? Is it conceivable that people who recognize only one eternal, immortal God could be idolaters? And why was no search conducted to locate the idol? The poor knights could not have set it aside, as they were all arrested without the slightest suspicion. But perhaps the idol possessed the power to make itself invisible, or perhaps it served Philip IV’s interest to inquire as little after this idol as after the Templar who had confessed to Florian <note>Esquin de Floyran, also referred to as "Florian" in some accounts.</note> in prison. — Translator's Note.

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed analysis of the trial of the Knights Templar in 1307, specifically focusing on the interrogations in Paris regarding the alleged idol and the "small cord" ritual. It follows the discussion of the idol's physical description and the inherent contradictions in the informers' accusations.</meta>

— ) 0 ( —
E 3

<margin>[1307.</margin>
In the interrogation of the one hundred and forty Templars <note>The Knights Templar (Order of the Temple), a wealthy and powerful Catholic military order during the Crusades.</note> in Paris, one finds sixty-five who admit and confess that they worshiped the idol. These are No. 2, 12, 13, 14, 15, 21, 22, 27, 34, 36, 38, 42, 43, 46, 48, 57, 59, 60, 61, 62, 72, 73, 74, 75, 76, 79, 80, 84, 85, 86, 87, 88, 89, 90, 91, 95, 99, 100, 101, 102, 104, 105, 107, 109, 110, 115, 116, 118, 119, 120, 123, 124, 126, 130, 131, 132, 133, 134, 135, 136, 139, 140.

<margin>The small cord.</margin>
In these testimonies, a great deal of discrepancy prevails. Some never saw the idol; others did not worship it. Various individuals add that, upon their admission <note>The initiation ceremony into the Order.</note>, a small cord had been tied around their bodies which had touched the idol, and which they were ordered to wear at all times. However, far from these statements being consistent, various knights do not mention the small cord at all and say they have never heard it spoken of; among others, the eleventh witness, Theobald von Beauvremont. It is equally astonishing that seventy-five other witnesses say nothing of the idolatry, nor of the small cord; this is a contradiction that causes a great deal of difficulty <note>original: "viel zu schaffen macht"; suggests this inconsistency is a major problem for the credibility of the trial's evidence.</note>.

<margin>1307.</margin>
complete; these were the 93rd, Jean de Chateauvilars, the 98th Henry d'Hercigny, the 108th Jean de Paris, and the 117th Lambert de Coisy; who boldly testified that upon their admission nothing was proposed to them, nor said, nor done, which was not chaste and honorable. It is incomprehensible how, given the harbored intention to abolish the Order, their testimonies were incorporated into the interrogation, and how they were spared the torments of torture. Did they have enough strength to endure it and resist? Or was one tired of putting so many knights on the rack? Perhaps it was believed that there was sufficient evidence already.

Matthieu
Adhemar.
Dupuy.
</margin>
Matthieu Adhemar, the seventh witness, likewise declares that he did not see the idol, as he never attended a General Chapter <note>A formal assembly of the high-ranking members of the Order to decide on administrative and spiritual matters.</note>; however, he charges the Order with a new crime in relation to idolatry. He testified that he had the Holy Mass read three times in his commandery <note><term>commandery</term> — the administrative manor and lands managed by a Commander of the Order.</note>, and a superior had forbidden it; troubled by a prohibition that caused him a strong doubt of conscience, he made the decision to go to Rome to confess and be absolved; he persuaded seven other knights to undertake this journey with him; he names them Jean de Chalancourt, Jean de Jovigny, Anedülfe de Hardiviliers, Jean de Trochincourt, Pierre de Salsante, Renier d'Argiville, and Benoit de Somenereur. It is not found, however, that he actually made the journey.

<margin>1307. Testimony of the Grand Master and the two Grand Priors.</margin>
We must dwell a little longer on the testimonies of the Grand Master and the two Grand Priors, as much depends on them, since they were the three most prominent persons of the Order, whose testimonies were bound to make the strongest impression.

The Grand Master <note>Jacques de Molay, the 23rd and final Grand Master of the Knights Templar.</note>, who is the 26th witness of the interrogation, declares that during his admission he was made to deny Jesus Christ three times—a confession diametrically opposed to the reputation for piety he held, the speeches he had delivered concerning the crimes of which the Order was accused, and his conversations with the Pope <note>Pope Clement V, who was initially skeptical of the charges against the Order.</note>, with which the latter had been so satisfied. It is evident, therefore, that the severity of the torture <note>original: <term>Marter</term></note> forced this confession from him, and perhaps also the weakness he had in yielding to the exhortations to satisfy the King <note>Philip IV of France, known as "the Fair," who sought to seize the Order's wealth.</note> and the promises to set him free. He made no other confession, neither of sodomy nor of the shameful kisses which were, as it were, a prelude to it. He did not say that he had spat upon the cross <note>original: <term>Kreuz</term></note> three times, although this usually accompanied the denial. Apparently, there was no desire to torture him further, and they were sufficiently satisfied with what he had confessed, which was indeed abominable enough to render him punishable and to incite loathing against the entire Order.

<summary>This page analyzes the forced confession of Jacques de Molay, the Grand Master of the Templars, highlighting the contradictions between his previous reputation and his admission of denying Christ under torture.</summary>
<keywords>Grand Master, Jacques de Molay, torture, confession, King Philip IV, Pope Clement V, Knights Templar, 1307 trials</keywords>

<margin>1307.</margin>
The testimony of Hugh de Pairaud <note>original: <term>Hugo von Peyraud</term>; a powerful figure who served as the Visitor of France and was effectively the Order's financier.</note>, Grand Prior of France and the 87th witness, is much more detailed; if it was not, as it appears, inspired by the truth itself, then he must have either been thrown into a great state of terror by the torture <note>original: <term>Marter</term></note> of the preceding knights, or he sought to earn the King's favor <note>Referring to King Philip IV of France.</note> through answers that were so remarkably favorable to this lord's purpose. Pairaud was, moreover, already quite old, held in very high esteem within the Order, and had for a long time acted with vigor in the most important matters.

He says, therefore, that upon his admission he denied Jesus Christ three times, and spat three times upon the cross; that he had given and received shameful kisses <note>A common accusation involving ritualized kissing on the mouth, navel, or base of the spine during initiation.</note>; that since the Grand Priory had been conferred upon him, he had admitted various knights in this capacity and had urged them to do the same things *); that he permitted them sodomy <note>original: <term>Sodomiterey</term>; in the medieval legal context, this referred broadly to "unnatural" sexual acts.</note>, as it had been permitted to him; that this was a statute of the Order. He added—

*) Why were the names of the knights admitted by him not asked for? Why were they not summoned and likewise interrogated? Why were they not confronted with him? In this way, one could have seen whether their statements agreed with his, and thus brought out the truth. But this might perhaps not have been conducive to the King's intention to exterminate the Order. The 86th witness, Guillaume de Hautmenil, after having confessed to all the crimes charged against the Order, added that he would have left the same had he not feared reproaches from his family—who had gone to such great expense to have him received as a knight—and if his fear that this step might be interpreted as a lack of courage <note>original: <term>Herzhaftigkeit</term>; in the chivalric context, this refers to the "heart" or bravery expected of a knight.</note> had not been even greater. <margin>1307.</margin> ...to subject himself to the denials, which were performed in memory of the threefold denial of Peter <note>A reference to the biblical account where the Apostle Peter denies knowing Jesus three times before the cock crows.</note>.

<margin>1307.</margin>

His dual role gave the King significant influence over the religious trial.</note> extended so far. He had several persons, who were appointed by the King, and three notaries as assessors. This procedure was carried out in Paris, in Melun, and in all the surrounding regions. <margin>Mezerai <note>François Eudes de Mézeray (1610–1683), a prominent French historian known for his comprehensive history of France.</note>.</margin> In addition to the four articles appearing in the statement of the two apostate <note>original: <term>abtrünnigen</term>; members who had left the Order and turned against it, providing the initial accusations for the King.</note> Templars, several other articles had also been drafted upon which the Inquisitor was to question them; they are, however, so ridiculous that one is almost ashamed to mention them.

<margin>Gaguin <note>Robert Gaguin (1433–1501), a French Renaissance humanist and chronicler.</note>.</margin>
They were accused of burning the bodies of deceased knights and making those who were being admitted swallow their ashes; that the knights had the children, whom they had fathered with a girl, roasted, and smeared their idol <note>A reference to the "Baphomet," the mysterious head the Templars were alleged to worship.</note> with the fat, without having these children baptized first; <margin>Paradin <note>Guillaume Paradin (1510–1590), a French writer and historian who documented the customs and history of his time.</note>.</margin> that they would not enter the room of a woman in childbed <note>A woman who has recently given birth.</note>, and if they happened to enter by chance, they would immediately back out; that they gathered in a dark cellar where the idol was located and brought sacrifices to it; that they brought women inside, then extinguished the lights, whereupon men and women mingled indiscriminately—just as the early Christians were once accused of doing, and as all new sects have been accused.

These actions were in contradiction to their alleged abhorrence of the female sex, and the opposite crimes with which they were accused <note>A reference to the charges of sodomy, which the author notes are logically inconsistent with the charges of fathering and sacrificing children mentioned above.</note>

<meta>The catchword "Ver:" at the bottom of the previous page connects to "Verhöre" (interrogations) or "Verfahren" (proceedings) in the following text.</meta>

1307, gave exactly the opposite result; however, once a society or a community has become the object of hatred, the accusations against it are invariably magnified. This often serves more to provide an excuse for their persecution than to actually prove them guilty. Within the transcripts of the interrogation, one finds no traces of these foolish <note>original: <term>läppischen</term>; meaning trifling, silly, or absurd.</note> accusations.

They were also accused of having stolen from the Royal Treasury and of having entered into a secret understanding with the Saracens <note>A term used in the Middle Ages for Muslims, particularly those in the Levant and North Africa.</note> against Saint Louis <note>King Louis IX of France (reigned 1226–1270), who led the Seventh and Eighth Crusades.</note> during his crusades. Yet, nothing of this is found in the official records; and since the aforementioned crusade took place in the year 1248—some sixty years earlier—no knight living at the time of the trial could have participated in this crime. In truth, the Order was accused of this because of the various interests they were forced to manage with both Christian and non-Christian <note>original: <term>ungläubigen</term>; literally "infidel," referring here to the diplomatic realities of the Crusader States.</note> princes.

The Inquisitor <note>Guillaume de Paris, the King's confessor.</note> had sub-delegated commissioners to his side to conduct these interrogations, even though it is generally against the rules of law for a delegate to sub-delegate <note>A reference to the legal principle *delegatus non potest delegare*, meaning one who is delegated a power cannot pass that power to another.</note>; however, he disregarded this rule, seeing himself supported by the King and undoubtedly merely fulfilling Philip’s commands. <margin>1307.</margin>
The interrogations followed the same formalities as those held in Paris; from which it can be concluded that the preparations for torture—and undoubtedly the actual torment of the defiant—took place there as well. Royal officials attended these proceedings along with the witnesses who had been appointed to be heard by the commissioners. We possess the records of only eight of these interrogations: namely those of Caen, Pont-de-l’Arche, Cahors, Carcassonne, Beaucaire, Troyes, Bayeux, and Bigorre. There is no doubt that the interrogations in the other provinces followed the same form.

<margin>Caen.</margin>
It was Dominican monks to whom the Inquisitor <note>The Grand Inquisitor of France, Guillaume de Paris, who was also the King's confessor.</note> had granted his authority to conduct the questioning, and who performed the interrogation in the presence of the knights Hugo von Chatel and Enguerrand de Villiers, whom the King had appointed to attend.

In this city, thirteen Templars had been taken into custody. They were brought out from the prisons to be interrogated by these Dominican monks, who promised the defendants the mercy of the Church, while at the same time the King's deputies assured them of the remission of temporal <note>Refers to secular or physical punishments, such as imprisonment or execution, as opposed to spiritual penance.</note> punishments *).

---

*) Unscrupulous fiscal agents <note>original: <term>Fiscale</term>; royal legal officers or prosecutors acting on behalf of the treasury.</note>, who lure nearly convicted thieves and murderers into confessions through such fraudulent promises and forbidden suggestions, are loathed,

<margin>1307.</margin> The preparations for torture, which were set before their eyes, brought the first twelve who were interrogated to the resolution to confess everything except for idolatry <note>original: <term>Abgötterey</term>; the specific charge that the Templars worshipped a head or an idol known as Baphomet.</note>, of which they declared that they knew nothing, and they further assured the court that they had not seen the idol. They admitted, however, that each of them had been given a small cord <note>Templars were accused of wearing a small cord or "girdle" around their waists that had supposedly touched their idol; this was a key piece of the prosecution's evidence of heresy.</note>, but that the use they were supposed to make of it had never been explained to them.

The 13th knight refused to confess anything. He was immediately placed upon the rack <note>original: <term>Folterbank</term></note>, and in the midst of the torment, the promise of forgiveness and full pardon was renewed. As he was undoubtedly unable to endure the severity of the torture, he followed the example of his brothers and made the same confession.

We have no further news of these interrogations.

---

and they may even be removed from their office; but far more detestable are the clergy who, through such promises, lead innocent—or at least non-suspicious—people into misfortune and to the most shameful death. Yet—

> What evil has happened that a priest did not do? <note>This cynical rhetorical question reflects the anti-clerical sentiment often found in Enlightenment-era histories of the Templar trials.</note>

It is a pity that the names of these abominable Dominicans <note>The Dominican Order often served as the primary agents of the Inquisition.</note> have not been preserved, so that they might be branded with eternal shame. **Translator’s Note.**

<meta>This page continues the historical account of the 1307 interrogations of the Knights Templar across various French provinces, following the previous discussion regarding the trials in Caen and the coercive methods used by inquisitors.</meta>

<margin>
1307.
At Pont de l'Arche.
Dupüy <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651) was a French scholar and king's librarian known for publishing primary documents related to the trial of the Templars.</note>.
</margin>
Even less is known about the proceedings at Pont de l'Arche. Ten Knights Templar were found there. They were interrogated in the presence of Pierre de Hangert, the <term>Amtmann</term> <note>A high-ranking administrative official or bailiff representing the crown.</note> of Rouen, and several other noblemen. No doubt by the same means <note>Likely referring to the threat or application of torture mentioned on the previous page.</note>, they rendered confessions identical to those at Caen; they spoke very obscurely regarding the point of idolatry, but they did admit to the circumstance involving the small cord <note>Templars were often accused of wearing a "girdle" or cord that had been consecrated by touching a heretical idol.</note>.

<margin>
At Cahors.
Dupüy.
</margin>
The names of the seven Knights Templar who were interrogated at Cahors in the presence of the knight Jean d'Areillan and two notaries are known. They were named Renaud and Pierre de Teyac (two brothers), Bernard de Cazal, Etienne Sommelin, Gui Cocha, Bernard de Velas, and Guillaume Arnaud. They all confessed that they had denied Jesus Christ and that they had given and received illicit kisses, albeit with varying degrees of incriminating detail. They admitted that sodomy was permitted among them; Arnaud even declared that the superior who had received him into the Order had abused him. This loathsome testimony is all the more compelling because violence could not bring him to do it <note>The main text suggests Arnaud's confession was voluntary and thus more credible, a claim the footnote below immediately disputes.</note> *).

At Carcassonne.

In Carcassonne, six Knights Templar were interrogated; it is necessary to cite the circumstances of the first two statements, as it seems that these, as abominable as they are, were dictated by the truth.

<margin>
Jean de Cassagne.
Dupuy <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), the French scholar whose records are being cited here.</note>.
</margin>
The first, Jean de Cassagne, Commander of Nogarede, testified that when he presented himself to be received as a knight in the chapter held at Pamiers, two knights were sent to question him. They asked if he wished to enter the Order. He had answered yes—

---

night, all at once and entirely alone, to have stolen so many items that a four-horse wagon would have been required to move them. Regardless of this, the torture would have continued if someone attending the ordeal out of curiosity had not reminded the judges that it was impossible for one person to carry away so many things at once; it was then that they finally saw that the poor victim <note>The original uses "Patientinn," a term often used historically for someone undergoing a medical or judicial "procedure."</note> had been driven insane by the pain. But thank God! These barbaric times are over. In the Austrian states, and in many other German lands, torture has been abolished; also in Hamburg, the translator's home city, no example of it has been known for a long time, and even where it is not explicitly abolished, it has fallen out of use through the <term>tacito consensu</term> <note>Latin: "by silent consent."</note> of the authorities. Only in France, in "gentle" France, does this cruel habit still persist. Perhaps it was reserved for Louis XVI, this true father of his people, to bring about a happy change in this matter, as in so many other things. —Note by the Translator.

answered. They had then departed to deliver his answer, and two others had come to him and said that he desired something very important, the fulfillment of which would be difficult for him, because the statutes of the Order—of which he saw only the exterior—were not easy to observe. When this speech did not frighten him, he was allowed to enter a hall where the Superior <note>Likely referring to the Preceptor or the presiding officer of the chapter.</note> was found in the company of ten other knights.

He was then made to swear that he wished to live in the Order without having anything as his own property, that he would preserve chastity, and that he would believe in a single God, the Creator, who had not died and also would not die. <note>This particular oath was highly controversial during the trials, as it was interpreted by inquisitors as a denial of Christ’s humanity and his death on the cross.</note>

Immediately, all the knights fell to their knees; a crucifix was raised high; at this sight, they worshipped the idol three times, for which the crucifix served as a sign of how they denied the same; and as often as they worshipped the idol, they spat upon the crucifix. <note>The "denial of the cross" and spitting on the crucifix were central charges brought against the Templars by the Inquisition to prove heresy.</note> The Superior gave the initiate a belt made of thread and permitted him to engage in sodomy <note>Homosexual acts; these accusations were frequently used in the Middle Ages to dehumanize and criminalize groups targeted by the Church.</note> only with the other knights. He then led him into a nearby room, where he put the robes of the Order on him and gave him instruction on how he should behave in church, in war, and at the table, during which he strictly enjoined him to constantly wear the thread belt. Cassagne added further that on the same day he had seen another knight received in the same manner; and he had observed these same ceremonies at various other initiations as well. He declared that he had confessed all these abominations in the Jubilee year of 1300. <note>There is a chronological tension here: the marginal date is 1307 (the year of the mass arrests), but Cassagne claims to have confessed these acts in 1300, suggesting the practices—or the accusations—predated the fall of the Order.</note>

<margin>
1307.
Testimony of 
Monpezat. 
Dupuy. <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651) was a French scholar who published the primary documents of the Templar trial in the 17th century.</note>
</margin>
Gaufferan de Monpezat confesses that, during his reception into the Order, the Superior showed him a bearded idol <term>Götzenbild: an "idol," often referred to in the trial records as Baphomet, though the description varies between witnesses</term>, which had the shape of a marionette, along with a crucifix. He made him worship the idol and spit upon the crucifix three times, telling him that this was a custom of the Order. Following this, he, Monpezat, gave the Superior all the shameful kisses <note>The "osculum infame" or ritual kisses on the mouth, navel, and base of the spine mentioned in the charges.</note>, after which the Superior permitted him to engage in sodomy with his fellow brothers in order to avoid the company of women—which was harmful to a good reputation—and to be able to withstand the extraordinary heat in Palestine. This Superior also gave him a belt, which he took from a box, and commanded him to wear it constantly.

The four other knights, Raimond Rúbbé, Guillaume Vos, Arnaud Sabatier, and Pierre de Mossie, testified to almost the same things, except for a few minor circumstances. Only one among them added that the Superior, while showing the idol, uttered these Arabic words: *Sarrazin y Alla* <note>original: "Sarrazin y Alla"</note>, which mean as much as "The Saracen to God."

Beaucaire. 
Dupuy.

The Royal Deputy was named Odoard de Molinier. The knights and serving brothers confessed everything, and above all the denial of Jesus Christ, of whom the Superiors

<margin>1307.</margin>...spoke of him as a deceiver. They further confessed that they had worshipped the idol once in a provincial chapter <note>A regional assembly of the Order's high-ranking members.</note>, and one of them stated that this was the custom of the Order.

The priest declared that the Superior who had received him into the Order had forbidden him from consecrating the host <note>The bread used in the Eucharist; "consecration" is the ritual act of turning the bread and wine into the body and blood of Christ.</note> when he celebrated the holy Mass, and had made him swear an oath to that effect. However, he did not always observe this, as he consecrated it in his heart and recited the words of institution <note>The specific biblical words spoken by Jesus at the Last Supper—"This is my body"—which are essential to the sacrament.</note> very softly. <margin>Interrogation in Bigorre. Dupuy. <note>Referring to the 17th-century scholar Pierre Dupuy, who compiled the primary legal records of the trial.</note></margin>Eleven knights taken into custody in the County of Bigorre, who were questioned before the bailiff <term>Landvogt: a royal official serving as a territorial governor and judge</term> of this province and before Bernard Agassa—both of whom were knights and deputies of the King—confessed that the Superior had forced them to spit upon the cross, to renounce it, and even to kick it away with their feet, though they claimed they had not wished to do so. For the rest, they confessed to all the crimes with which they were charged, except for sodomy; only a single individual admitted that it had been permitted to him, and that he had likewise permitted it to another knight. This interrogation was signed by a notary, a witness, and the Templar knight de Monpezat.

<margin>
1307.
Interrogation at Bayeux.
Dupuy <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar whose works are a primary source for the legal history of the Templar trials.</note>.
</margin>
The Inquisitor traveled to Bayeux in November, following the interrogations in Paris, and questioned five Knights Templar who had been detained there.

Their testimonies largely corresponded with those given in Paris. One of them, Raoul de Gisy <note>original: "Radülfe de Gisy"</note>, added that although he held the rank of Prior within the Order, he had never seen any idolatry practiced within it, and he did not know if the small cord given to him had touched any cult image <note>Accusers claimed the Templars wore cords that had been "consecrated" by being wrapped around the neck of an idol.</note>. He then fell to his knees and begged for forgiveness in the presence of the entire assembly. The interrogation was signed by two notaries.

<margin>
Interrogation at Troyes.
The same <note>Referring to the source by Dupuy.</note>.
</margin>
From Bayeux, the Inquisitor proceeded to Troyes, where he recorded the testimonies of several Knights Templar in the presence of the bailiff <term>Amtmann: a high-ranking local official performing judicial and administrative duties</term> and two noblemen. It is stated in these records that two of them confessed to all the facts about which they were questioned—except for idolatry—without any coercion. They even admitted that permission for sodomy <term>Sodomiterey: in this context, the historical term for same-sex acts, which were included in the list of heresy charges against the Order</term> had been granted, though they declared they had never personally engaged in it. Their testimony was read back to them two days later, and they made no changes to it.

When they were exhorted to reflect upon their actions, they began to weep, fell to their knees, and begged for forgiveness.

<margin>1307.</margin> In addition to the crimes which all these knights confessed in the various interrogations, they also admitted to the Order's desire to enrich and expand itself by any means. In the interrogation at Beaucaire, they confessed that within this province alone, they had acquired landed property for 11,000 livres <term>Livre: the standard currency of account in medieval France; 11,000 was a massive fortune at the time</term> over the span of forty years—an enormous sum for those times.

<margin>Interrogation at Metz and in the three bishoprics. Dupuy <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), the French scholar and librarian whose collection of trial documents is a primary historical source.</note>.</margin> From Troyes, the Inquisitor proceeded to the three bishoprics \*), and questioned the knights who had been taken into custody there. These were Germans. By all appearances, no force was used there. They did not confess; rather, they declared that nothing but what was good and holy had occurred during their reception into the Order. The Inquisitor was obliged to report this to the King; this likely did not please him much, as he held them all to be guilty.

<margin>Examination of the interrogation.</margin> Such is the nature of the interrogations that have survived to our time. It is to be presumed that the interrogations in the other bailiwicks <term>Amtsvogteyen: administrative districts overseen by a bailiff who held judicial and executive power</term> of France—where the same procedure was followed and, most notably, where the method of torture had been employed—were of almost the same character; which would then suggest \*\*)

**) Not even to presume. For a legal presumption to be valid, there must at least be some grounds present, <note>The German translator is providing a skeptical legal commentary here, arguing that the "confessions" obtained via torture in other districts should not be considered valid evidence of guilt.</note>

<sig>F 5</sig>

<margin>1307.</margin>
...that this famous Order consisted of villains who were at once <term>Deists</term> <note>A Deist believes in a creator based on reason and nature but rejects organized religion and divine revelation; the author notes this is logically inconsistent with being an "idolater."</note>, sodomites, and idolaters—though these qualities are, in a sense, incompatible with one another, especially the first and the last. Furthermore, since <term>Deism</term> is founded upon the laws of nature, it censures and condemns all crimes; it cannot coexist with idolatry at all, and it abhors the unnatural crime of which the <term>Knights Templar</term> were accused, and which they supposedly made no difficulty in confessing.

One should not, therefore, be surprised that an Order already notorious for its debauchery and pride became loathsome to the King once he believed it to be convicted of these hideous crimes, and which he was determined to abolish *). Nor should one be surprised that all of <term>France</term>, once these crimes were made public, joined in the King's opinion, and that the <term>Knights Templar</term> became objects of public detestation.

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal and moral critique of the trial of the Knights Templar. While the previous page discussed the illogical nature of the specific charges (such as being both Deists and idolaters), this page enumerates the specific procedural irregularities and the unreliable nature of the witnesses used by King Philip IV of France in 1307.</meta>

->— ) 0 ( —<-

<margin>1307.</margin>
If, however, one examines the circumstances of these interrogations more closely, one cannot help but doubt the truth and sincerity of most of the testimonies.

<margin>
Arguments in favor of the innocence of the <term>Knights Templar</term>.
</margin>
Firstly, has a judicial proceeding ever been more irregular? When suspicion is cast upon a great <term>Order</term> <note>The Poor Fellow-Soldiers of Christ and of the Temple of Solomon, commonly known as the Knights Templar.</note>, whose origin is so holy and which has rendered such important services to <term>Christendom</term>, one begins by resorting to force—condemning all the knights, arresting them without any legal formality, and casting them all into dark dungeons.

Secondly, there was no proof of their crimes to justify such shameful treatment other than the hearsay of a man condemned to death, and the testimony of two apostates from the Order. These men had been punished by the law and had forfeited their own lives; however, having been informed of the King's <note>King Philip IV of France, who was deeply in debt to the Templars and sought to seize their wealth.</note> intentions, they lodged an accusation to save their own necks. This accusation lacks all probability and should have been disregarded, especially since their own proven crimes and the manifest danger they faced rendered them legally unfit to serve as witnesses and made their testimony contemptible under any law.

Thirdly, advantage was taken of the consternation and sudden terror into which all the knights were plunged when they found themselves bound and fettered, trembling for their lives and convinced that the intent was to exterminate them all.

<meta>catchword: Four:</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the legal irregularities of the 1307 trials against the Knights Templar, specifically criticizing the lack of due process and the reliance on the testimony of two criminals who traded accusations for their lives.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, 1307 trials, King Philip IV, judicial irregularities, forced confessions, Christendom, legal defense</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal critique of the trial of the Knights Templar, specifically focusing on the use of torture to extract confessions and the inconsistencies found in the resulting testimonies. It follows the previous page's discussion of the irregular arrest and the questionable nature of the original accusers.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>->— ) 0 ( —<-</header>

<margin>1307.</margin>
Fourthly, at the moment of interrogation, the instruments of torture were laid out before their eyes; they were threatened with being subjected to them if they did not confess to the crimes with which they were charged. Indeed, even more was done: they were actually placed upon the rack, and through the intensity of the agony, confessions were wrung from them—confessions that overwhelmed them with shame, but which must be given no credence. No one’s testimony should be accepted when, by testifying against themselves, they stand to lose their lives. What a way to treat men of high standing, unaccustomed to such pain! They were urged to declare themselves guilty to please the <term>King</term> <note>King Philip IV of France, who orchestrated the downfall of the Order.</note>, with the promise that they would go unpunished. They were even persuaded that after making a confession—the consequences of which they did not foresee—they would be set free and only subjected to fines that would be the responsibility of the entire <term>Order</term>.

And finally, what is one to think of the so many alterations, and indeed the many contradictions, with which the testimonies of the knights are filled? A few, though only a small number, refuse to confess anything, or their testimonies actually defend the knights. Others—the majority, in fact—confess to the <term>denial</term> <note>The specific charge that new members were forced to deny Christ and spit on the cross during their initiation.</note>, without mentioning anything about <term>sodomy</term> or <term>idolatry</term> <note>Standard charges of the era used to justify the total dissolution of a religious or military group and the seizure of its assets.</note>; some have heard nothing of such things at all. Almost every testimony sounds different. If these practices were part of the <term>Order's statutes</term>, how could the initiation ceremonies not have been uniform? Have they perhaps

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal and historical examination of the trial of the Knights Templar in 1307. It addresses the argument that the uniformity of the confessions might suggest guilt, while simultaneously offering a rebuttal based on the conditions of the imprisonment and the influence of the Inquisitors.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>->— ) 0 ( —<-</header>

1307. Are there examples showing that in an <term>Order</term> <note>A religious or military community bound by a common rule, such as the Knights Templar.</note>, as in any spiritual community, the same ceremonies are not observed for everyone who enters it? And are the rules and statutes not explained to all of them in the same way? According to these interrogations, it is therefore evident that there was neither a general rule nor a statute that introduced these crimes, prescribed them, or was known to all the knights; and that all those who made these general confessions could have done so merely with the intention of avoiding <term>torture</term> <note>The German "Marter" refers specifically to the physical agony of the rack or other interrogation devices.</note>.

<margin>
Conjectures against the Knights Templar.
</margin>
On the other hand, however, one cannot hide two things; the first is that such a large number of testimonies and uniform confessions *) should arouse a great prejudice against the innocence of the Knights Templar. What? Should so many lords and noblemen, the <term>Grand Master</term> <note>Jacques de Molay, the last head of the Order.</note> himself, the Grand Priors, the Commanders, the—

---

*) <note>original: "Anm. des Uebers."</note> These uniform confessions are easily explained. The worthy <term>Inquisitor</term> <note>The official appointed by the Church to investigate heresy; in this case, working closely with King Philip the Fair.</note> had indeed informed the knights before the interrogation what they should confess in order to please the King; before the seizure of the knights, the crimes with which they were burdened had already become known, and they had even complained to the <term>Pope</term> <note>Pope Clement V, who was under significant pressure from the French monarchy.</note> about it; the knights were not each imprisoned in isolation, but several were kept together; they could therefore have reached an agreement with one another simply to escape the situation: and in this way, is it really so surprising that many of them agreed with one another in their statements? —Translator’s Note.

<summary>This page weighs the evidence against the Templars, noting that while the uniformity of confessions looks suspicious, it was likely the result of coaching by Inquisitors and the knights' ability to coordinate stories to avoid further torture.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, trial of 1307, confessions, Inquisitor, Grand Master, Jacques de Molay, legal defense, torture</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the trial of the Knights Templar, specifically weighing the likelihood of the knights' confessions being true versus the possibility that they were coerced or motivated by cowardice.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>->— ) 0 ( —<-</header>

<margin>1307.</margin>
—those who saw death so closely in battles, who even often defied it, could have had so little heart and been so base \*) to blame themselves and their <term>Order</term> <note>The Poor Fellow-Soldiers of Christ and of the Temple of Solomon, commonly known as the Knights Templar.</note> for shameful deeds and abominations, simply to avoid a punishment of short duration, which surely could not have extended to the death penalty! Out of contempt for religion, they would have spoken against their honor and against the truth—something which tender persons of the female sex, raised in luxury, would hardly have done.

The second thing to be noted here is that very many knights, in their testimonies, speak of certain circumstances that were purely personal to them, giving these statements an almost physical certainty through which they are, as it were, proven. What could have compelled them to report these shameful acts about which they were not asked, which they could have kept silent, and which further aggravated their crime by showering them with disgrace and shame? Will it be claimed that they wished to make themselves dishonorable out of abominable complaisance toward the King and the <term>Commissioners</term> <note>The royal and papal officials appointed to lead the investigation and trial of the Templars.</note>, to dishonor the Order, and to invent deeds,
<meta>of which</meta>

---

Man's heart is a stubborn and despondent thing; how many a man is a hero in the field and yet cannot see a <term>bloodletting</term> <note>A common medical practice (venesection) involving the withdrawal of blood; here used to illustrate how physical squeamishness differs from military bravery.</note> without fainting. <summary>This page examines the psychological contradictions of the Templar trials, questioning why battle-hardened knights would confess to shameful crimes they might not have committed. It argues that the specific, personal details in their testimonies suggest a level of authenticity that complicates the theory of forced confession.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the validity of the Templars' confessions, concluding that the Order was likely neither entirely corrupt nor entirely innocent, but rather characterized by regional inconsistencies in practice.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
<header>->— ) 0 ( —<-</header>

<margin>1307.</margin>
whose correctness one could have investigated? This is against all probability and does not allow one to doubt the reality and sincerity of their confessions \*), however useless and abominable they may be; for they also cite times, witnesses, and circumstances that are only too convincing.

<margin>Result of the investigation.</margin>
What will be the conclusion from so many contrasts? That the entire <term>Order</term> <note>The Poor Fellow-Soldiers of Christ and of the Temple of Solomon, known as the Knights Templar.</note> was corrupt? There are far too many proofs to the contrary. That it was not corrupt at all? There are just as many proofs that demonstrate the corruption of some knights. One must therefore concede that there was no uniformity in the Order; that in the various <term>Chapters</term> <note>Local administrative branches or governing assemblies of the Order.</note> in the provinces, conflicting statutes, customs, and practices took place which cannot be attributed to the entire Order. One will see in the following, from the reports in the other European states where the <term>Order of the Temple</term> was established and likewise possessed great estates, that just as much diversity reigned there, and that in some of these kingdoms various knights were punishable, but most were innocent.

---

\*) <note>original: "Anmerk. des Uebersetzers."</note> It has already been mentioned above what kind of mad things delinquents have often confessed under torture. The knights were just as much human beings as common criminals, and the torment could therefore lead them to equally mad and contradictory statements. Translator's Note.

<meta>catchword: Almost</meta>

<summary>This page concludes that the Knights Templar were neither entirely guilty nor entirely innocent, suggesting instead that corruption was localized and practices varied by region. The author emphasizes that while some knights were surely guilty of the crimes confessed, the Order lacked the uniformity required to condemn the entire organization.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the discussion of the investigation into the Templars' confessions, shifting from the specific evidence of corruption to the political and economic motivations behind their continent-wide arrest in 1307.</meta>

<page-num>95</page-num>

<header>->— ) 0 ( —<-</header>

<margin>
1307. All Templars in Europe are taken into custody. <note>The text cites historical accounts by Pierre Dupuy, Nicolaus Gürtler, and Claude Fleury.</note>
</margin>

At almost the same time that King <term>Philip the Fair</term> <note>Philip IV of France (1268–1314), who was instrumental in the downfall of the Order.</note> had all the Templars in France arrested, they were all seized in nearly the same manner in other states. This followed the letters which this sovereign had sent to their rulers and the reports he had given them regarding their crimes—crimes for which he claimed to have received certain proof. The princes gave credence to Philip’s letters out of great reverence for the King and for the Crown of France, which at that time enjoyed such great respect in Europe that it set the tone for the other powers. However, they were also already inclined toward this action due to the haughty pride of the knights, who did not conduct themselves with the discretion owed to absolute rulers and had offended almost all of them. A spirit of self-interest may likewise have contributed; these princes saw clearly that it was the intent of the King and the <term>Pope</term> <note>Pope Clement V, who was under significant pressure from the French crown.</note> to abolish an <term>Order</term> *) that was far too arrogant and wealthy, possessing immeasurable estates everywhere; and everyone flattered themselves with the hope of receiving a share of the spoils.

---

*) The wealth of the knights was, without doubt, their greatest crime. Had they not been rich, they might have been truly guilty of even more crimes in those corrupt times than they were accused of, and no one would have disturbed them. <note>original: "Anmerk. des Uebersetzers."</note> Translator's Note.

<margin>1307.</margin>
Thus, the knights were taken into custody in every state—though not all in the year 1307 as happened in France, as distances did not allow for it, and each prince had to take certain measures to achieve this end. Rather, it happened gradually throughout this and the following years. Moving beyond chronological order for the sake of consistency in the subject matter, we wish to report how this proceeded in each state.

<margin>
Sicily.
<note>The text references the works of historians Pierre Dupuy, Nicolaus Gürtler, and Claude Fleury.</note>
</margin>
Sicily was the first state to follow France's example, and where all the Knights Templar were taken into custody. Charles II of Anjou was king there, and he lived in the greatest harmony and perfect understanding with King Philip the Fair, whose cousin he was.

This kingdom had been established by Charles I of Anjou, a brother of <term>St. Louis</term> <note>King Louis IX of France (1214–1270), the only French king to be canonized.</note>, who had taken it from <term>Manfred of Swabia</term> <note>The last Hohenstaufen King of Sicily (1232–1266).</note>, and had been <term>enfeoffed</term> <note>A legal process where a lord or the Pope granted land or a kingdom to a vassal in exchange for loyalty and service.</note> with it by Pope Urban IV. This conquest encompassed the Kingdom of Naples and the island of Sicily; Charles I further united with it the counties of Provence, Forcalquier, and Piedmont, which he possessed through his wife, Beatrice of Aragon, who was their heiress.

Charles II was one of the greatest and best kings in Europe; and as great as his desire was to be pleasing to the King of France, it is still doubted whether he [acted solely] on his

The vengeance of the husbands was the origin of the **Sicilian Vespers** <note>A successful rebellion on the island of Sicily in 1282 against the rule of the French-born King Charles I.</note>, in which all the French were murdered, after which these people submitted to the rule of the King of Aragon, who took possession of the island of Sicily.

Through this unfortunate outcome, Charles became a mortal enemy of the <term>Knights Templar</term> <note>A powerful medieval Catholic military order.</note>; he seized all the <term>commanderies</term> <note>original: "Comthureyen." The administrative centers and estates that provided the Order's income.</note> they held in the Kingdom of <term>Neapolis</term> <note>Naples</note>, from which they were expelled. This was considered consistent with justice, for it was unheard of that an order, which owed its very foundation to the generosity of all Christian princes, should dare—out of base self-interest—to renounce a neutrality that was, so to speak, mandated by divine right, and to wage war against one of its own benefactors.

This was the state of affairs when Charles received a letter from King <term>Philip the Fair</term> <note>King Philip IV of France (reigned 1285–1314), who was the primary architect behind the destruction of the Templars.</note>, along with all the instructions and dossiers regarding the crimes of the Knights Templar, as well as the plans this prince had devised against them and an urgent invitation to follow his example. Without hesitation, King Charles joined him; since few Templars remained in his Italian territories, he issued his orders only in the counties of **Provence**, **Forcalquier**, and **Piedmont**, following the model of what had already taken place in France. They were all taken into custody on the 24th of January. The interrogations began almost at the same time, and there is no doubt that the same legal formalities were observed and the same violence <note>A reference to the widespread use of torture to extract confessions from the Templars.</note> was applied. It is said that they all confessed to the crimes of which they were accused. Since these interrogation records are no longer available, one cannot confirm the truth of this; however,

<margin>In Italy. <note>original: "Dupuis." Refers to Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar who wrote an influential history of the Templar trial.</note></margin>
In the rest of Italy, the <term>Knights Templar</term> <note>A wealthy and powerful Catholic military order suppressed in the early 14th century.</note> did not possess any significant estates, except in the <term>Papal States</term> <note>original: "Kirchenstaate," territories in central Italy under the direct sovereign rule of the Pope.</note>, in <term>Tuscany</term>, and in the <term>Milanese</term> <note>original: "Mayländischen," the region surrounding the city of Milan.</note>. All the knights there were taken into custody in November. However, the interrogation was not conducted until February and March before Easter, once the <term>Pope</term> <note>Pope Clement V (reigned 1305–1314), who was under significant pressure from the French King Philip IV.</note> had issued the order for it. Here, the knights were interrogated by the bishops, and the instruments of torture were likewise employed to force them to confess the truth. In **Ravenna**, the archbishop conducted the legal proceedings, and it is asserted that they confessed to all the crimes. In **Bologna**, several made the same confession; however, very many others denied them with great steadfastness.

In Tuscany and the Milanese, the archbishops of **Florence** and **Pisa**, together with the papal commissioners, prepared the trial. The interrogations they conducted had the same result as in Ravenna. They drafted written reports in the form of judicial inquiries, citing the testimony of various outside witnesses who accused the Knights Templar of various crimes, which these witnesses knew either from their own experience or from common rumor.

<margin>1307.</margin>
### Navarre
Since <term>Philip the Fair’s</term> <note>King Philip IV of France (r. 1285–1314), the primary instigator of the trials against the Templars.</note> eldest son, <term>Louis</term> <note>Louis X of France, who was King of Navarre through his mother’s inheritance before becoming King of France.</note>, was King in **Navarre** on account of his mother, **Joan**, one should not doubt that Philip’s orders were carried out in Navarre with just as much severity as in France itself. However, it appears there were few <term>Knights Templar</term> <note>Members of the military-religious order founded to protect pilgrims in the Holy Land.</note> in Navarre—a poor kingdom with narrow borders—whose kings were not in a position to grant this Order great estates.

### Castile, Aragon, and Portugal
In **Castile**, **Aragon**, and **Portugal**, matters were quite different, especially in **Castile**, which was then a very wealthy and populated kingdom. **Don Ferdinand IV**, King of Castile; **Don James II**, King of Aragon; and **Don Denis**, King of Portugal, fully understood the intentions of the King of France and the advantages they themselves could draw from the abolition of the Templars. They loved the Templars just as little as that sovereign did, as the **Templar Order** was virtually independent of the rulers in all states and often opposed them through its pride and wealth.

Nevertheless, they did not rush; they wanted to see how such a great undertaking would play out in the rest of Europe—a behavior suited to these nations, which combine prudence with slowness. In the meantime, they did not fail to make their preparations to proceed against the Templars when the opportunity should be favorable.

<meta>catchword: In</meta>

<summary>This page describes the varied responses to the Templar suppression in the Iberian kingdoms, noting that while Navarre followed French orders strictly, the rulers of Castile, Aragon, and Portugal acted with calculated deliberation to see how the situation evolved elsewhere in Europe.</summary>

In Cyprus.
<note original="Dupuy, Chevreau, Nangis, Fleuri">Pierre Dupuy, Urbain Chevreau, Guillaume de Nangis, and Claude Fleury: these are prominent French historians and chroniclers whose works served as primary sources for the history of the Templar trials and the Crusades.</note>

In **Cyprus**, the difficulties were greatest. After the tragic end of the siege of **Acre** <note>The Fall of Acre in 1291 was the loss of the last major Crusader stronghold in the Holy Land to the Mamluks, forcing the military orders to retreat to Cyprus.</note>, the **Grand Master** had relocated the headquarters of the Order there; they possessed various places on the island, including **Ninove** <note>This likely refers to Limassol (Lemesos), which was the primary Templar port and headquarters in Cyprus; 'Ninove' may be a regional variant or an archaic transcription.</note>, a coastal city some miles from **Nicosia**, the capital of the kingdom. Ninove was the residence of the Grand Master; he maintained a magnificent court there and kept various ships equipped for war in the harbor, with which the knights cruised to all the trading hubs of the **Levant** <note>A term for the Eastern Mediterranean lands.</note>, attacked the Sultan’s fleets, and never returned without rich plunder. The **Templars** were thus so powerful on this island that they served as a counterweight to the power and prestige of the King of Cyprus, who feared them and wished to have nothing to do with them.

The **Lusignan** family had ruled in Cyprus since 1191. They were originally of French descent and had always been in the greatest harmony with the French crown. One should therefore not doubt that King Philip’s letter <note>Philip IV of France, who orchestrated the arrest of the Templars and sought support from other Christian monarchs.</note> was received there with joy, and that they resolved to show themselves compliant with it, especially since the fulfillment of this plan aligned with the interests of the state.

**Hugh IV**, a son of King **Henry II**, was the King of Cyprus at that time; but because he could not rule himself due to his minority, **Amaury**, Prince of **Tyre**, the first prince of the blood and Regent of the realm,

<margin>1307.</margin>
...of the kingdom, he held the power in his hands. **Amaury** <note>Amaury de Lusignan, Lord of Tyre, who acted as regent of Cyprus after usurping power from his brother, King Henry II.</note> was a good lord, but of limited intellect, and important matters often threw him into confusion. He had frequently had quarrels with the **Grand Master Gaudin** <note>Thibaud Gaudin, Grand Master from 1291 to 1292.</note>, who had undertaken various actions against the authority of the Regent; and it is not known whether **Molay** <note>Jacques de Molay, the last Grand Master of the Templars.</note>, Gaudin's successor, followed in his footsteps. For there exists a letter from the Regent to **Pope Clement V**, written shortly before the election of this Pope, which contains very sharp complaints against the **Order of the Templars**; because of this, no favorable sentiments toward them were instilled in this Pope.

The Regent rejoiced at the letter from the **King of France** <note>Philip IV, also known as Philip the Fair, who initiated the arrests of the Templars in France.</note>, especially after he had received a letter from the Pope, which gave him news of everything that had occurred in the **West** <note>original: "Occidente"</note>, and issued a **bull** <note>A formal proclamation or decree issued by a Pope.</note> to him to have all Templars taken into custody on one and the same day. However, the Regent was in a quandary regarding the execution of this order. The proceedings in **Europe** had already become known. All the knights were on their guard and had almost all gone to **Ninove** <note>As noted previously, this refers to the Templar stronghold in Limassol, Cyprus.</note>, where they had taken up arms and strongly fortified themselves. It was therefore a necessity for the Regent to postpone the execution of the bull until he had taken the appropriate measures to carry the matter out successfully.

<margin>1307.</margin> All **Knights Templar** <term>Templar: A member of the Poor Fellow-Soldiers of Christ and of the Temple of Solomon, a wealthy and powerful Catholic military order.</term> were thus in custody in almost all European realms by the end of this year. The **Pope** <note>Clement V, who reigned from 1305 to 1314.</note> contributed to this, although not right at the beginning; for a dispute arose between him and the **King of France** <note>Philip IV, also known as Philip the Fair.</note> that would have been almost favorable to the persecuted Order. On this point we must now offer an explanation, as we have—setting aside strict chronological order <note>original: "Zeitordnung"</note> so as not to break the thread of the narrative—only related the manner in which the Templars were taken into custody across all states. We now return to the point in time when the Pope received news of the interrogations conducted in France and the confiscation of the Templars' properties.

The Pope’s dissatisfaction with the proceedings initiated in France.
Pierre Dupuy <note>original: "Düpuis" — A 17th-century French scholar who wrote an influential history of the Templar trial.</note>.
Claude Fleury <note>original: "Fleuri" — A prominent French ecclesiastical historian.</note>.

The **Pope** <note>Clement V.</note> very soon received news of everything that had occurred in France. He was not surprised that all the **Knights Templar** <term>Knights Templar: A wealthy Catholic military order that the King of France sought to dismantle.</term> in France had been seized on the same day; it is probable that the King had informed him of this intention: but when the manner of the interrogation, the violence <note>Referring to the use of torture to extract confessions.</note> that had been employed to achieve it, and especially the confiscation of all their movable and immovable property reached his ears, as well as the fact that administrators had been appointed in the King's name to collect the income from the real estate, he became indignant and flew into a rage.

He viewed all these proceedings as so many infringements upon his authority: and he believed that they originated from the **Inquisitor** <note>Guillaume de Paris, the Grand Inquisitor of France and confessor to King Philip.</note> and the bish... <note>The text breaks off here mid-word; it likely intended to say "bishops."</note>

He stated that this was not the obedience that Christian princes owed to the **Successor of Peter** <note>A traditional title for the Pope, claiming direct lineage from the Apostle Peter.</note>; the Pope was saddened and astonished by it; yet he hoped His Majesty would soon reflect, rectify everything that had been undertaken against good order, and give him full satisfaction.

Furthermore, one could not understand by what right he had undertaken this **suspension**, because the prelates shared the **pastoral functions** with him and were his assistants in church governance. The **bull** <term>Bull: A formal proclamation or edict issued by the Pope.</term> was all the less regular since the **bishops**, each in his own **diocese** <term>Diocese (original: Kirchensprengel): The specific geographic district under a bishop's jurisdiction.</term>, could **organize** <term>Organize (original: instruiren): A legal term meaning to gather evidence and prepare a case for trial.</term> the **trial** far more easily, quickly, and with greater insight. It was therefore a manifest injustice to take this **instruction** away from them in order to put strangers in their place—men without experience and who had no acquaintance with the inhabitants of the country. <margin>1307.</margin>
...that the Pope despised religion and the cause of Jesus Christ; that the Pope should be answerable to God, indeed that he could even be held accountable before men for this reason, because he was subject to the laws of his predecessors <note>original: "Vorfahren"; literally "ancestors," but referring here to previous Popes and the established traditions of the Church.</note>, and because one could proceed against him, especially in matters of faith.

He concluded by representing to him that, through the resolution he intended to take—to investigate the trial of the Templars himself or through delegated judges—he was favoring them and intended to ensure they went unpunished; this resolution would lead to delays that would never end, and would cause incidents contrary to the justice and necessity of a swift dispatch; that, moreover, the King in this great matter acted neither as a plaintiff nor as an accuser; but that he merely fulfilled the duty of a Christian prince, filled with zeal for religion, a prince who was a servant of God and His knight, and must give Him a strict account of everything that occurred in his realm against the faith, against morals, and good order.

<meta>This page continues the account of the conflict between Pope Clement V and King Philip IV of France regarding the Knights Templar. Following the King’s forceful protest, the Pope now weighs his dangerous position—residing within French territory and fearing the same violent fate as his predecessor, Boniface VIII.</meta>

<header>-( 0 )-</header>

<margin>1307.</margin>maintain; however, he had to face the most powerful King in Europe, an enterprising and bold prince. He remembered the obligation he owed him for the papal dignity <note>Philip IV was instrumental in securing the election of Clement V, the first Pope of the "Avignon Papacy."</note>, and the promises he had finally made to him. He considered that he found himself in the midst of this King's realm and was in his power; that he could neither leave France nor go to Rome without exposing himself to a thousand dangers, and subjecting himself to the tumult and riot of the Romans, who had in a sense thrown off the yoke of his predecessors, who were busy trying to turn Rome into a republic, and had frequently driven the Popes out of their city. 

However, what made the greatest impression on him was the fate of Boniface VIII, who had succumbed in his conflict with Philip, and which had cost him both his throne and his life. He already imagined he saw a new Nogaret <note>Guillaume de Nogaret, Philip’s advisor who led the "Outrage of Anagni," where Pope Boniface was physically assaulted and arrested.</note>, a new Colonna <note>Sciarra Colonna, a member of a powerful Roman family and an ally of Philip, who famously struck Pope Boniface.</note>, dedicating themselves to the passion of this violent master and subjecting him to an undignified encounter, just as Boniface had experienced at their hands, even in the heart of the Papal States <term>Kirchenstaat: The Papal States; territories in central Italy over which the Pope exercised temporal, secular rule.</term>.

These considerations gained the upper hand over what he owed to his own fame and the honor of the papal throne. Filled with terror, he yielded in everything, and was now intent on nothing else but to spare this hot-tempered prince, to please him, and while essentially giving in to him, to strive to shroud his weakness with a veil and maintain the outward appearance of his authority.

<header>) o (</header>
<sig>H 2</sig>

<margin>1307. He revokes his first Bulls. <note>The margin cites Pierre Dupuy and Claude Fleury, prominent 17th-century historians who documented the Templar trials.</note></margin>
As he thus consulted nothing but his fear, and an interest that concerned him more closely than the interest of the papal dignity, he seemed not to notice anything insulting or defiant in the King's presentation; he revoked his first bulls <note>A "Bulla" is an official papal edict, named after the lead seal (bulla) attached to it.</note>, and through a new bull which he had issued, he lifted the suspension he had previously ordered; he permitted all bishops to personally oversee the trial of the Templars within their own ecclesiastical districts <term>Kirchensprengel: An administrative district or diocese under a bishop’s jurisdiction.</term> even until the final judgment, under the condition that said judgment should be confirmed by a provincial council; and to save a portion of his honor, he reserved for himself the judgment in the trials of the Grand Master and the high officials who had been taken into custody in France. These were the Grand Priors of France, of Normandy, and of Aquitaine.

The entire realm, and especially those who took up the cause of the Knights Templar, fell into the greatest astonishment at this sudden change by the Holy Father. The letters he had received from the Inquisitor of the Faith and from all the bishops in France, which had been arranged in concert with the King, could serve the Pope as a pretext for these new bulls. They had written to him that everything undertaken against the Templars had been absolutely necessary to forestall their evil intentions, and that furthermore, these knights had erred against the faith and consequently fell under the jurisdiction of the Inqui-

<margin>1307.</margin>
...inquisitors <note>The text begins mid-word, continuing from the previous page's mention of the Inquisitor of the Faith.</note> and the bishops. Regarding this latter crime, it may not have lacked all probability; but the first was a great untruth; for the Order had never been more peaceful, nor less inclined to cause the slightest unrest in the kingdom.

The Pope, who now followed only the impressions of his fear, was not satisfied with having revoked his first bull <note>original: "Bulle." An official papal edict.</note>; he also wrote to the legates <note>A papal legate is a personal representative of the Pope acting on his behalf in a foreign court.</note> who were with the King, instructing them to accommodate themselves to the King's will, to satisfy him in everything concerning the Knights Templar, and in doing so, to protect the papal authority as much as they possibly could. By nullifying the previous bull—which had authorized the provincial councils <term>Provinzial : Concilia: Regional meetings of bishops and high-ranking clergy to decide on matters of church law and discipline.</term> to confirm the judicial sentences of the bishops—the Pope thus restricted their power over these sentences. He reserved the judgment over the general state of the Order solely for himself, setting it aside for the King and the papal throne.

<margin>
The King reconciles with the Pope again. Dupuy. <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651) was a French scholar whose works provided the primary historical documentation for the trial of the Templars.</note>
</margin>
The King received the Pope's new arrangements with great joy; for despite everything, he did not wish to remain at odds with him; he saw only too well how necessary the Pope was for the execution of his plan. And although he had emerged from the conflict with the late Pope Boniface <note>Pope Boniface VIII (r. 1294–1303), who had been a fierce rival of King Philip IV; their conflict led to the King's men attempting to kidnap the Pope at Anagni.</note> to his own advantage, this strife had nevertheless caused him so much trouble, provoked so much resentment, and exposed him to so many dangers, that he was glad to find himself

<meta>This page continues the description of the reconciliation between King Philip IV of France and Pope Clement V. Having previously been at odds over the legal jurisdiction of the Knights Templar, the two leaders have now aligned their interests to dismantle the Order.</meta>

<header>— ) 0 ( — 117</header>

<margin>1307.</margin>
to see himself freed from a similar danger. He immediately wrote a letter to the Pope in a completely different style of writing; he thanked him for his bull <note>original: "Bulle." An official papal decree.</note>, assured him that he had received his legates with all honors, and protested to him that it had never been his intention to infringe upon the papal jurisdiction. In doing so, he promised him that all the property of the Knights Templar would be reserved for the purpose for which His Holiness had dedicated it, and would be managed by the same officials who administered his own domainal estates <term>Domanial-Güter: Lands and properties owned directly by the crown rather than by feudal lords.</term>, so that neither confusion nor embezzlement could take place.

<margin>
Administrators of the property of the Knights Templar. Dupuy. <note>Referring to Pierre Dupuy’s historical records of the trial.</note>
</margin>
As both powers were thus united with one another and proceeded in harmony against the Knights Templar, no further help remained for the Order. The King had indeed received the legates with every mark of preference they could possibly desire. On their part, they behaved according to the Pope's latest instructions, following the wishes and intentions of the King. He surrendered all prisoners and all their property to them; but, to tell the truth, this was only for the sake of appearances <note>original: "zum Scheine." Philip maintained practical control while appearing to defer to the Church.</note>. For the King still kept them in his power, since the legates had declared by means of an authentic legal act that—because it was difficult to safely remove the prisoners from the kingdom—they should be kept there by Royal officials in the name and under the authority of the Pope and his officers.

The system of laws and legal principles made and enforced by the hierarchical authorities of the Church.</note>.
