However, this was consistent neither with the intentions nor the advantages of the Templars' enemies, as the knights might retract everything they had confessed, thereby thwarting the aim of the King, who remained determined to exterminate the Order.

Great embarrassment already existed because of the retractions that various knights had made regarding their testimonies, both in France and in <term>Provence</term> <note>A region in southeastern France that was, at the time, a sovereign territory outside the direct control of the French crown.</note>. <margin>1308.</margin>
royal administrators managed the estates and had them in actual possession. Meanwhile, the King—so as not to make the Pope indignant—wrote a letter to him, in which he explained that his inclination was for all these goods to remain together, so that he might provide an account of them in the same condition as they were found at the time all the knights were taken into custody.

<margin>
Return of the <term>legates</term> <note>Diplomatic representatives of the Pope.</note> to Poitiers.
<term>Dupuy</term> <note>Refers to the historian Pierre Dupuy, whose works are a primary source for the trial records.</note>.

After the legates had completed their mission to the satisfaction of the Pope and the King, who had now so perfectly reconciled with one another, they returned to the Pope at Poitiers <note>The city in France where Pope Clement V was residing at the time, as the papacy had moved from Rome to France.</note>. They provided him with information on everything that had occurred and delivered to him the records of the interrogations, which were now adopted as the primary foundation of the <term>proceedings</term> <note>original: "Processes." The formal legal trial against the Order.</note>. They added that the retraction of some <term>Templars</term> <note>original: "Tempelherren."</note> deserved no belief; that since the entire Order was in a state of ruin, they were uniform in their conduct and had made themselves subject to the same crimes through horrific vows; in a word, that even if an innocent man might perhaps be found among them—which was nonetheless not probable—the Order as a whole was nevertheless an object of loathing and had to be abolished. This was a consequence of what had been decided in the assembly held in Paris regarding the altered testimony of certain Templars.

<margin>
1308.
Parlement <note>In this context, an assembly of the three estates (nobility, clergy, and commoners) called by the King to gain consensus for his actions.</note> at Tours.
Dupuy <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar who published historical documents regarding the trial of the Templars.</note>.
Gurtelin <note>Likely Nicolaus Gürtler, a scholar known for his history of the Templar Order.</note>.

To accelerate this great <term>legal process</term> <note>original: "Processes." Referring to the ongoing trial against the Order.</note>, the King summoned a Parlement at Tours in the month of June and invited nearly all the princes, bishops, and great lords of the realm to it, indicating to them that they should appear either in person or through legal proxies. He made it known that he himself would be present, and that this assembly would have the matter of the <term>Knights Templar</term> <note>original: "Tempelherren."</note> as its object. Nothing could have been more detrimental to the Order, because the King’s sentiments were not unknown; and since he was greatly feared, no one dared to be so bold as to oppose him. He traveled to Tours around <term>Pentecost</term> <note>original: "Pfingsten." A Christian feast day occurring fifty days after Easter; a common time for royal business.</note> and found there a very large multitude of both spiritual and secular lords who had obeyed his commands, as well as about four hundred proxies for those who had been unable to come.

The King spoke there with all the fire and heat inspired by the opinion he held regarding the crimes of the Templars. He delivered a brief report on the interrogations by which they were accused; he spoke at length about the many atrocities they were charged with and which they had confessed; in such a manner that the entire assembly—without hearing them, and indeed without even conducting an investigation into their crimes—declared them deserving of death.

The King
departs
</margin>
Gratified by this result, the King left Tours and traveled to Poitiers to see the <term>Pope</term> <note>Pope Clement V, the first of the Avignon popes, who was under heavy influence from the French King.</note>,

Bolstered by this political victory, the King travels to Poitiers to pressure Pope Clement V.</summary>

Accordingly, the final measures against the **Knights Templar** were taken at Poitiers, and it was agreed between these two lords that all Templars should be held in custody under royal authority, at the request of the Pope and the bishops and in their names; that the <term>proceß</term> <note>The legal trial or proceedings.</note> against the Templars should be conducted by the bishops and archbishops in their dioceses, and even by the **Inquisitor of the Faith** <note>original: "Glaubens-Inquisitor." Referring to the office of the Inquisition, which normally operated independently of secular kings.</note> together with them, and carried out until the final judgment, even though this was against the usual rules. However, this was done with the consent of the Pope in order to accommodate the King’s will. It was further agreed that the King should only punish the Templars with the involvement of the Pope; that in the event it was deemed beneficial to abolish the Order, all its property should be used for the reconquest of the **Promised Land** <note>original: "gelobten Landes." Referring to the Holy Land (the Levant), the original focus of the Crusades.</note>; and that to this end, the Pope and the King [would use] their...

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal and ecclesiastical proceedings against the Knights Templar in 1308. It follows the agreement between King Philip IV of France and Pope Clement V regarding the custody of the knights and the distribution of their assets, leading into the formal summons for the Council of Vienne.</meta>

->- ) 0 ( -<-

<margin>1308.</margin>
...they should issue their orders for the execution of this article; indeed, His Majesty intended to have the **papal commissioners** <note>Officials appointed by the Pope to oversee specific administrative or legal tasks.</note> receive back the movable and immovable property of the **Order** <note>The Poor Fellow-Soldiers of Christ and of the Temple of Solomon, commonly known as the Knights Templar.</note> that remained in the hands of the King’s subjects.

The Pope, for his part, renewed the prohibition against granting refuge to the knights through a <term>bull</term> <note>A formal public decree or charter issued by a Pope.</note> and added the penalty of **excommunication** <note>original: "Kirchenbann." The formal exclusion of a person from the sacramental life of the Church.</note>. However, even as he granted the bishops the power to judge the individual knights, he excluded the **Grand Master** and the **Grand Priors**, for whom he reserved the right of judicial inquiry to himself. This agreement was signed, and it was now easy to foresee what the eventual fate of the Order would be.

<margin>Convocation of the general Council of Vienne.</margin>
To this end, the Pope issued a bull convening a general **Council at Vienne** <note>Vienne is a city in southeastern France; this council (1311–1312) ultimately resulted in the dissolution of the Templar Order.</note> in the <term>Dauphiné</term> <note>original: "Delphinat." A former province in southeastern France.</note> for the month of October in the year 1310. He directed all archbishops, bishops, abbots, priors, and deans of Christendom to attend, while also requesting the King’s presence. By means of a second bull, he summoned the entire **Order of the Templars** to appear at the Council to defend themselves. He further commanded the **Cardinal-Bishop of Palestrina** <note>original: "Präneste," the Latin name for the city of Palestrina in Italy.</note>, to whom the case of the Templars imprisoned in France had been entrusted, to present the Grand Master and all the Grand Priors of the Order—whose judgment the Pope had reserved for himself—before the Council.

<summary>This page details the formal legal steps taken by Pope Clement V in 1308 to consolidate the trial of the Templars, including the reservation of high-ranking leaders for papal judgment and the convocation of the Council of Vienne.</summary>

<keywords>Pope Clement V, King Philip IV, Knights Templar, Council of Vienne, excommunication, Grand Master, Cardinal-Bishop of Palestrina, Dauphiné, papal bull</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the legal proceedings against the Knights Templar in 1308. Having previously discussed the King's agreement with the Pope and the summons for the Council of Vienne, the text now focuses on the Pope’s personal interrogation of a select group of knights at Poitiers.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>
<header>->— ) 0 ( —<-</header>

<margin>1308. The Pope interrogates the Knights Templar at Poitiers. Dupuy. Fleury. <note>Refers to the historical works of Pierre Dupuy and Claude Fleury, who documented the trial of the Templars.</note></margin>
Before the bishops proceeded further in their legal actions, the Pope desired that the **Knights Templar** who had been taken into custody and interrogated in Paris should be brought before him. An order was therefore issued to bring seventy-eight of them to Poitiers; however, only seventy-two arrived, as the remaining six fell ill at Chinon <note>The Castle of Chinon was a fortress where several high-ranking Templars, including the Grand Master Jacques de Molay, were imprisoned.</note>. The Pope had the seventy-two knights enter the <term>Consistory</term> <note>original: "Consistorium." A formal meeting of the Pope and his college of Cardinals for the transaction of Church business.</note>, where, in the presence of all the Cardinals, the testimonies from the interrogations conducted in Paris were read aloud. No one raised any objection to these statements, nor did they during a second reading of the testimonies which the Pope conducted with them a few days later. Consequently, the Pope could not doubt the truth of the crimes with which they were charged.

It is strange that out of one hundred and forty Templars who had been interrogated in Paris, only seventy-two were brought to Poitiers. It is probable that the sixty-eight who were not brought were those who had retracted their previous confessions, and whom it was not deemed useful to present to the Pope. These seventy-two whom he examined—still gripped by fear and terror, and intimidated by the threats that had been issued against those who had retracted their statements—maintained silence during the reading of their testimonies and thereby confirmed them.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>In 1308, Pope Clement V personally interrogated seventy-two Knights Templar at Poitiers, where they confirmed their previous confessions under duress and fear of retribution.</summary>
<keywords>Pope Clement V, Poitiers, Knights Templar, confessions, interrogation, Consistory, Chinon, Pierre Dupuy, Claude Fleury</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the Templar trials in 1308, specifically detailing why certain high-ranking leaders were unable to travel to Poitiers and the subsequent legal proceedings conducted while they were held at the Castle of Chinon.</meta>

<page-num>128</page-num>
<header>->— ) 0 ( —<-</header>

<margin>
1308.
Interrogation at Chinon.
Dupuy.
Fleury.
Vertot.
<note>These refer to Pierre Dupuy, Claude Fleury, and René-Aubert Vertot, prominent historians who documented the trial of the Templars.</note>
</margin>

The six Knights Templar <note>original: "Tempelherren"</note> who were unable to come to Poitiers were the Grand Master <note>Jacques de Molay</note>, the Visitor of France, the Grand Commander of Cyprus, the Grand Priors of Normandy and Aquitaine, and the Prior of Poitou. They were all ill and so enfeebled that they could not sit upon a horse. Their poor state of health was of long standing; for it had been caused by the torments they suffered on the rack <note>original: "Folterbank"</note> during the interrogations in Paris. They were brought to the castle at Chinon <note>A massive royal fortress in the Loire Valley where the leaders were held to keep them separate from the rank-and-file knights.</note>.

After the Pope had received word that they could not travel to Poitiers, he appointed the two Cardinal Legates who had been in Paris and Cardinal Landulf of St. Ange to interrogate them. They interrogated these gentlemen in the castle, or rather, they merely had the statements read aloud to them which they had already made in Paris; this took place over three different days. On August 17th, they interrogated the Grand Master, the Prince Dauphin <note>Likely referring to Hugh de Pairaud, who was the Visitor of France and a member of the family of the Counts of Forez/Dauphiné.</note>, the Grand Prior of Normandy, the Visitor of France, and the Prior of Poitou. They all four <note>While five names are listed, the original text specifies "all four," reflecting a possible discrepancy in the historical source or the merging of the titles of the Prince Dauphin and the Visitor.</note> confessed that they had denied Jesus Christ and spat upon the cross. Peiraud, the Grand Prior of Aquitaine, likewise confirmed his Paris testimony on the 19th and 20th. The Grand Commander of Cyprus did the same; indeed, to make his testimony even more certain, he even asked the commissioners to interrogate a serving brother who confessed to the same crimes.

<meta>catchword: These</meta>

<summary>This page describes the interrogation of the high-ranking leaders of the Knights Templar at the Castle of Chinon in 1308, noting that they were too physically broken by torture to travel to the Pope at Poitiers.</summary>
<keywords>Jacques de Molay, Chinon, torture, Grand Master, confessions, Cardinal Legates, 1308, Hugh de Pairaud</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the 1308 trial of the Knights Templar at the Castle of Chinon, following the previous page's description of the leaders' poor health and the beginning of their interrogation by the three Cardinal legates.</meta>

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>— ) o ( — 129</header>
<sig>I</sig>

<margin>1308.</margin>
These seven knights <note>While the previous page mentioned six leaders, historical accounts of the Chinon proceedings often fluctuate in the total number of high-ranking officials present.</note> hereupon desired to be reconciled with the Church, which was granted to them; they were given absolution <note>A formal ecclesiastical act of releasing someone from the guilt or penalty of sin.</note> as the Pope had permitted, and the three Cardinals reported to the King what had occurred, whereby they asked him to proceed with leniency toward the guilty and to likewise forgive them.

Although one finds this report in the writings of those times, it is not free from all suspicion. The continuation of this history will show that the Grand Master <note>Jacques de Molay</note> and the Prince-Dauphin <note>Hugh de Pairaud, the Visitor of France.</note> contradicted these facts, and that a contradiction even stands out in what the former was made to say—that he had spat three times upon the holy cross—even though this circumstance had not been mentioned in the interrogation in Paris, and it is not probable that he would have wanted to further aggravate the accusations against himself. A copy of this type of repeated interrogation was sent to the King, and the Cardinals returned to Poitiers to report on their mission to the Pope. All Templars questioned at Poitiers and Chinon were brought back to prison in Paris. The report further adds that the Grand Master and the others fell to their knees after the interrogation and, shedding a flood of tears, asked for forgiveness.

<meta>catchword: After:</meta>

Having received reports from his legates regarding the interrogations at Chinon, Pope Clement V now moves to formalize the proceedings against the Order across all of Christendom, despite legal concerns regarding the previous year's local inquisitions.</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>
<header>- ) 0 ( -</header>

<margin>
1308.
Bulle to proceed against the Order.
Dupuy.
Fleury. <note>References to historians Pierre Dupuy and Claude Fleury, who documented these events.</note>
</margin>
After the Pope had received the written report of the three Cardinals, and had himself been a witness to the confessions which the seventy-two knights had made in full consistory <note>A formal meeting of the College of Cardinals presided over by the Pope.</note> where they had been interrogated, he considered himself convinced of all the crimes of which they were accused; he regarded this Order as corrupted, and was thereby confirmed in his resolution to abolish it. Since, however, he did not consider the proceedings conducted the previous year by the Inquisitor and the bishops—whom he had not authorized—to be legally valid <note>The Pope was concerned with "due process" under canon law, as the initial arrests and trials in 1307 were largely directed by the French King rather than the Papacy.</note>; he decided to launch new investigations under his own authority, which, as he desired, should also remove even the slightest doubts and irrefutably demonstrate all the crimes of the Templars.

In this frame of mind, on the 11th of August, he issued two new bulls <note>Formal papal decrees, named for the "bulla" or lead seal attached to them.</note>. In the first, he ordained that investigations should be conducted against all Templars in all Christian states; namely in France, in England, in the province of Wales, in Scotland, in Ireland, in Germany, in Bohemia, in Poland, in Spain, in the Kingdom of Arles, in Provence, in Dauphiné <note>original: "Delphinat"</note>, in Savoy, in Cyprus, in Italy, in Sicily, in the Kingdom of Naples <note>original: "Neapolis"</note>, in Hungary, in Greece, in Sardinia, and in Corsica. He recounts in this same document everything that had been undertaken against them; that the King of France had given him the first news of the corruption of the Order, and indeed without any selfish motive, since this lord [offered] him the administration of their goods

<meta>catchword: over:</meta>

<summary>Pope Clement V, convinced of the Templars' guilt but wary of the legal flaws in earlier trials, issues new papal bulls to launch a standardized, church-wide investigation across Europe and the Mediterranean.</summary>
<keywords>Clement V, Papal Bulls, Knights Templar, Inquisition, Philip IV of France, European Kingdoms, Canon Law</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of Pope Clement V's formal actions against the Knights Templar in 1308. It details the contents of two papal bulls which expanded the investigation from France to the rest of the Christian world and established the legal framework for the trials.</meta>

<page-num>131</page-num>
<header>- ) 0 ( -</header>

<margin>1308.</margin>
had abandoned [them]; that the ruin of the Order was certain, as he himself had heard seventy-two knights in the <term>Consistory</term> <note>A formal meeting of the Pope and his cardinals.</note> who had confessed their horrific crimes; that one of the most prominent members of the Order had made a secret confession to him; that three Cardinals at Chinon <note>The Castle of Chinon, where the Grand Master Jacques de Molay and other leaders were being held and interrogated.</note> had questioned the Grand Master and six heads of the Order, who had all confessed to the crimes; that one could therefore not doubt the total corruption of the Order, and that he had summoned a general <term>Council to Vienne</term> <note>The Council of Vienne (1311–1312) was the forum where the Order would eventually be abolished.</note> to remedy such an abominable disorder. Afterward, he listed in detail all the horrors of which the Order of the Temple was said to be guilty, and about which the knights were to be questioned.

By virtue of the second bull, which concerns only France and in which he repeats almost the same points, he appointed commissioners—mostly Archbishops and Bishops of France—to go into all the provinces to conduct investigations against the Templars, to seek them out, and to interrogate them. It was not difficult to find them, because they were all sitting in prison. These commissioners were empowered to hold provincial councils and to judge the guilty, with the exception of the Grand Master and the five Grand Priors and <term>Commanders</term> <note>High-ranking regional officers within the Templar hierarchy.</note>, over whom the Pope reserved the right to pass judgment himself.

These bulls were distributed with great diligence. The proceedings against the Templars began anew, and in all

<page-num>132</page-num>

<margin>1308.</margin> In France, the commissioners conducted their investigations. In the <term>ecclesiastical province of Sens</term> <note>At this time, Paris was under the religious jurisdiction of the Archbishop of Sens, making this district the focal point of the trials.</note> they had the most work to do, as the one hundred and forty Templars taken into custody in Paris fell under the Archbishop's district. The provincial councils had the authority to judge them and even to request the assistance of the <term>secular arm</term> <note>The "secular arm" refers to civil government or royal power; since the Church could not legally shed blood, they handed "guilty" parties to the state for punishment.</note>.

On the 22nd of November, the Pope wrote a private letter to Robert, Duke of Calabria—the son and presumptive heir of Charles II, King of Sicily—to urge him to follow the example of the King of France. He was encouraged to arrest all Templars found in the states of his father, the King, who had accepted him as co-regent. In Provence, which was part of his territories, this had already occurred. This letter, therefore, could only have been directed at the Kingdom of Naples, where—due to the bitterness of the Neapolitan court against the entire Order—very few knights were to be found.

<margin>
Draft for
a Royal
Order.
Vertot. <note>Abbé de Vertot (1655–1735) was a historian known for his works on the Knights of Malta and the Templars.</note>
</margin> King <term>Philip the Fair</term> <note>Philip IV of France, the king responsible for the downfall of the Templars.</note> had returned to Paris, very satisfied to see the proceedings against the Templars taking such a favorable course. In the nearly certain assurance that an Order so hated by him would be eradicated, he began to look ahead. This imminent abolition provided the opportunity for a plan devised by this lord’s ministers, which would have been very advantageous for him. They had this plan—

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of King Philip the Fair's political maneuvers in 1308 during the suppression of the Knights Templar. The previous page ended with the King returning to Paris, satisfied with the progress of the legal proceedings against the Order.</meta>

<page-num>133</page-num>
<header>— ) 0 ( —</header>

<margin>1308.</margin>
A draft was drawn up to make their intentions known to his court, and to secure a significant provision for Prince Philip, the King's second son. The intent was to unite all military orders in Europe and consolidate them into a single one, which would have carried the name of the "Royal Order." This order was to have its headquarters in Cyprus, and the King of Cyprus, Hugh IV <note>Hugh IV of the House of Lusignan. At this time, the crown of Cyprus was a vital base for Crusader operations after the fall of Acre.</note>, who was unmarried because he was still a minor, was to be appointed its Grand Master. In addition to all the assets of the various military orders, the plan was to grant the new order the Kingdom of Cyprus and King Hugh's rights to the crown of Jerusalem. There was no doubt that this young king would approve the plan, as it would have made him a powerful monarch. To this end, however, this prince would have had to take a vow, after which he would be appointed Grand Master of the Royal Order; he would then have been persuaded to adopt Prince Philip as his son and name him his successor.

Nothing could have been more flattering for Philip the Fair, who would have thus become the father of two kings and raised the glory of the nation to its highest peak; however, as ambitious as this lord was, being both prudent and insightful, he considered this undertaking to be unfeasible. He recognized the obstacles presented by all the military orders, by the various rulers in whose states they held <term>commanderies</term> <note>original: "Comthureyen." A commandery was the smallest administrative unit of a military order, consisting of an estate or manor that produced revenue to support the knights' missions.</note>, and by the Pope—

<sig>I 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: —pope</meta>

<summary>This page details a 1308 proposal to consolidate all military orders into a single "Royal Order" based in Cyprus, intended to benefit King Philip the Fair's second son. Despite the potential for increased prestige, the King ultimately deemed the plan's logistical and political hurdles—including opposition from other monarchs and the Pope—too great to overcome.</summary>

<margin>1308.</margin>
...the Pope <note>The OCR fragment "stes" completes "Pabstes," referring to Pope Clement V.</note> himself, with whom he did not wish to fall into a dispute, and whom the Grand Master of the Order of the Temple had discouraged from a similar intention with such sound reasons that the Pope had completely withdrawn from the idea. This proposal was therefore regarded as almost impossible. The King reserved the right, however, to sound out the Pope regarding this matter.

<margin>Envoy of the King of England.</margin>
Around this time, the Pope received an envoy from King Edward II <note>Edward II of England, who was the son-in-law of Philip the Fair, having married Philip's daughter Isabella.</note>, who delivered a letter from this lord. This letter was not at all pleasant for the Pope, given the preconceived opinion he held against the Templars and the alliances he had entered into with the King of France. In it, Edward informed the Pope that he had heard with astonishment that the Knights Templar had been taken into custody and that rigorous investigations had been launched against them. He reminded the Holy Father <note>original: "heil. Vater"</note> that he should not give ear to all the slanders <note>original: "Verläumdungen"</note> that might be spread against them, and assured him that nothing was occurring on their part within his own states that would give cause for suspicion of a crime; on the contrary, he asserted they were models of piety, of blameless character, and filled with zeal for the welfare of religion and the defense of the faith. The King of France, to whom the Pope communicated this letter, did not give it much credit and did not pursue the measures <note>original: "Maaßregeln"</note> he had taken with any less vigor because of it.

<margin>
1308.
Investigations.
Dupuy <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651) was a French scholar and librarian to the King; his work "Histoire de l'ordre militaire des Templiers" is a foundational source for these trials.</note>.
</margin>
So, a beginning was made with the investigations, and by this means all the statements of the Knights Templar <note>original: "Tempelherren"</note> were recorded in public acts <note>official legal records</note>, wherein it was mentioned that those whom the Pope had interrogated had taken the oath by placing their hands upon the Holy Book of Gospels. A great many knights confirmed their first statements and thus confessed, before the bishops or commissioners, to the crimes of which they had made a confession.

<margin>
Patents for the defense of the Order.
Dupuy.
</margin>
On the 25th and 26th of November, two Royal Patents <note>formal legal documents or decrees issued by a monarch</note> appeared; in the first, it was commanded that the knights who had been taken into custody in the provinces, and who were to be interrogated there by the commissioners, should be brought to Paris. In the second, it was reported that various knights had requested to defend the Order; that the King permitted them to do so, and accordingly commanded that all those who wished to undertake this should be brought under secure guard before the commissioners in Paris; to this end, the administrators were to advance the costs for bringing them there, and also to ensure that the knights, when the matter was over, were returned to their prisons.

<margin>
1309.
Various procedures.
Dupuy.
Fleury <note>Claude Fleury (1640–1723) was a prominent French ecclesiastical historian.</note>.
</margin>
The three cardinals, to whom the task of conducting the new interrogation had been assigned, arrived in Paris at the beginning of August; on the 8th, they summoned the entire Order, partly to interrogate the knights and partly to hear those who wished to undertake their defense.

<meta>This page continues the account of the legal proceedings against the Knights Templar in 1309, moving from the initial arrests and royal decrees to the formal scheduling of hearings and the introduction of increasingly sensational accusations against the Order.</meta>

<page-num>236</page-num>

->— ) 0 ( —<-

<margin>1309.</margin>
The deadline was set for the 12th of November in the bishop’s hall; court officials were dispatched to Reims, Rouen, Tours, Lyon, Bourges, Narbonne, and Auch to deliver the same citation <note>A formal legal summons to appear before a court.</note>, which was supported by the orders issued by the King. At the same time, various provincial councils <note>Regional meetings of church leaders.</note> were scheduled, to which the judicial investigations were to be sent so that the bishops who comprised them could pass judgment. The most famous of these councils were those held at Paris and at Reims.

<margin>
New Accusations.
Dupuy <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651) was a French scholar and librarian; his "History of the Templars" is a primary source for these accounts.</note>.
Mezerai <note>François Eudes de Mézeray (1610–1683), a noted French historian whose works often critiqued the monarchy and church.</note>.
</margin>
The commissioners had been provided with new articles <note>Formal points of accusation or specific questions for interrogation.</note> upon which they were to question the knights; articles which, following the crimes already contained in the first interrogations, were quite useless, and whose absurdity betrayed a blind passion to make the Order of the Temple hateful and loathsome.

The most prominent of these was, first, that the Grand Master, after a knight's admission and at his own convenience, would have them deny Jesus Christ <note>original: "Jesum Christum"</note>, and sometimes even all the saints in Paradise, both male and female; an accusation that was already included in the Deism <note>A belief in a creator-god that does not intervene in the universe; here used as a charge of heresy to suggest the knights rejected Christian dogma.</note> they were accused of professing, and in the denial they had supposedly performed upon their admission. Moreover, how useless it was to have them deny the Holy Virgin and all the saints after they had already been made to deny Jesus Christ.

<meta>catchword: Two-</meta>

<summary>This page describes the formal organization of the 1309 trials against the Templars across France and critiques the redundancy and perceived absurdity of the new, increasingly extreme charges of heresy brought against the knights.</summary>

<keywords>1309, Knights Templar, Provincial Councils, Grand Master, heresy accusations, Deism, Pierre Dupuy, legal proceedings</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of formal accusations brought against the Knights Templar during the 1309 proceedings, specifically detailing the "new articles" mentioned on the previous page. It highlights the logical inconsistencies and lack of evidence for the more sensational charges.</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>->— ) 0 ( —<-</header>
<sig>I 5</sig>

<margin>1309.</margin>
**Second**, that the Grand Master and the superiors <term>superiors: high-ranking officers within the Order’s hierarchy</term> had the right to absolve them of all their sins. This presupposed that they confessed, and that despite rejecting the Christian religion, they nevertheless accepted its doctrine that is associated with the greatest difficulties <note>The author points out the contradiction: if the Templars truly rejected Christianity, it makes little sense that they would still practice the rigorous and "difficult" ritual of confession and penance.</note>.

**Third**, that the Grand Master had admitted to these two articles in the presence of various witnesses of the first rank; a confession which it was claimed he had made before he was even taken into custody; however, no proof of this could be provided.

**Fourth**, to the criminal kisses <note>original: "strafbaren Küssen." This refers to the *osculum infame* or "shameful kiss," a common accusation in medieval heresy and witchcraft trials, alleging that initiates had to kiss their superiors on the mouth, navel, or base of the spine.</note> of which they were accused, an even more shameful circumstance was added.

**Fifth**, that the superiors had those put to death who refused to submit to the laws prescribed for them. Not a single knight had testified to such a serious crime, even though they had spoken extensively enough about the acts of violence committed against them <note>The text suggests that while knights complained of mistreatment during their arrest or interrogation, none supported the claim that the Order murdered its own members for internal disobedience.</note>.

**Sixth**, that their initiation <term>initiation: the formal "reception" or ceremony where a man was admitted as a brother into the Order</term> occurred only at night, and with all the doors of the church closed. The contrary was demonstrated by various testimonies, which proved that many of these initiations did not take place in churches.

<summary>This page details five specific accusations against the Templars, including the illegal absolution of sins and secret nighttime initiations, while noting the lack of evidence or logical consistency behind these claims.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the accusations against the Templars, moving from the specific charges of the 1309 proceedings to the historical spread of these allegations from France to other European kingdoms, specifically Aragon.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>->— ) 0 ( —<-</header>

<margin>1309.</margin>
**Seventh**, that many knights had left the Order because of the disorders occurring within it. The large number of knights of which the Order consisted proved the contrary <note>The author suggests that if the Order were truly in such internal disarray, its membership would not have remained so high.</note>.

In fact, one does not find that the commissioners received any proof of these new accusations.

<margin>
Judicial proceedings in other states.
1307.
Dupuy.
In Aragon.
</margin>
It is not known whether these final accusations were also sent to other European states. The first ones were more than sufficient to proceed against the Templars. It was only on December 3, 1307, that the King of Aragon received a letter from the King of France, in which he informed him of everything he had done and exhorted him to follow his example. Accompanying this writing was also a letter from a famous Parisian Doctor of Theology and a Dominican <term>Dominican: a member of the Order of Preachers, known for their focus on education and their prominent role in the Inquisition</term>. He confirmed the crimes of the knights, as if he were completely convinced of them. Don James <note>original: "Don Jayme (Jakob)," referring to King James II of Aragon, called 'the Just'</note> immediately assembled all the grandees of his realm, and on their advice, he charged the Archbishops of Saragossa and Valencia, along with the General Inquisitor *), to conduct judicial inquiries against the Templars. This commission was issued on December 3, and the proceedings began in January.

->Die<-

---

*) He was named Johannes Lotgerius and belonged to the Order of Preachers <note>original: "Prediger:Orden," another name for the Dominicans</note>. Note by the translator.

<summary>This page describes how the King of France attempted to export his persecution of the Templars to Aragon in late 1307, leading King James II to initiate a formal investigation involving the Inquisition and high-ranking archbishops.</summary>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the suppression of the Knights Templar in Spain. Following the commissions established in late 1307, the narrative now shifts to the year 1308, detailing the Templars' armed resistance in the regions of Aragon and Valencia against the King and the Inquisition.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>->— ) 0 ( —<-</header>

<margin>1308.</margin>
The knights, who had received word of these orders and were not unaware of how their fellow brothers had been treated in France, gathered together on their side to face the storm that threatened them. They resolved to resist the consequences of these investigations, even by force of arms. They possessed various cities, castles, and fortresses in the two kingdoms of Aragon <note>original: "Arragonien"</note> and Valencia <note>original: "Valentia"</note>, as well as in the Principality of Catalonia <note>original: "Catalonien"</note>; they retreated into these and entrenched themselves there. The bishops immediately notified the King of this and begged him to issue an order that they be taken into custody and all their property confiscated. He had the orders for this drawn up immediately.

Because of this, the Grand Inquisitor <term>Grand Inquisitor: the high-ranking official overseeing the Inquisition's proceedings; here, the Dominican Johannes Lotgerius</term> issued his commands to all the major cities, requesting that they assist him with armed force to execute the King’s orders. This caused the matter to be drawn out, as it was necessary to gather a sufficient number of troops to force the knights into obedience, for they could not safely be attacked except by a superior power.

In the meantime, to turn the public against them, the Inquisitor summoned them all to the Dominican monastery in Valencia to be interrogated there concerning their faith. The King, for his part, called a meeting of all the bishops of his realm in this city, in order to

The meeting was held on the 6th of January, and they waited a long time to see if the knights would obey; however, it was learned that they were instead preparing themselves to mount a defense, and that they could only be subdued by force. <margin>In Castile and Portugal. 1308. Dupuy <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651) was a French scholar whose history of the Templars' trial is a primary source for this text.</note>.</margin>
Don Ferdinand <note>Ferdinand IV of Castile</note>, King of Castile, proceeded with greater vigor. Scarcely had he received the letter from the King of France <note>Philip IV, "the Fair," who initiated the arrests of the Templars</note> than he immediately followed that lord’s method. He had all the Templars taken into custody on a single day, ordered all their estates confiscated, and appointed all the bishops to manage them, to collect the revenues from them, and to provide an accounting to those who would be appointed for that purpose. At the same time, he appointed the bishops of Toledo and Compostela to proceed against the Order, and the same procedure was observed in this lord’s states as was followed in France. In Portugal, King Don Denis <note>Denis I, known as the "Farmer King"</note> followed the conduct of the King of Castile in his own behavior.

<margin>In England. The same <note>referring to the author, Pierre Dupuy</note>.</margin>

In the other regions of Italy, they proceeded with extreme severity. That which had occurred in France had incensed the prelates and the people in the Papal States <note>original: "Kirchenstaate"</note>, in Tuscany, and in the Milanese against the Order.

<margin>In Germany.</margin>
The Pope had issued his bulls <note>Official papal edicts or decrees, named after the *bulla* or lead seal used to authenticate them.</note> to Germany to the Archbishops of Mainz, Trier, and Magdeburg, as well as to the Bishops of Constance and Strasbourg, so that each in his diocese <note>original: "Sprengel"</note> and in the other regions of the Empire might have the Templars <note>original: "Tempelherren"</note> seized and proceed against them legally. He had directed the bulls to the King of the Romans <note>The title used by the German king before being formally crowned Holy Roman Emperor; at this time, Henry VII.</note>, to the Archduke of Austria, as well as to the other rulers and prelates, to have the Templars taken into custody to put them on trial. However, there was no great haste to assist the delegate, and the investigations that were conducted were by no means accompanied by the acts of violence that had been perpetrated in other states.

In France—to return again to this kingdom, where more detailed news of all the proceedings is available—the highways were full of knights who had been taken prisoner in the provinces. They were brought to Paris before the three Cardinals and other commissioners who had the task of interrogating them. These prisoners were placed in various prisons where, because they were present in such great numbers, it must have been very uncomfortable for them. The interrogation was to be conducted in the hall of the episcopal palace, and according to the reports of the commissioners, judgment was thereafter to be passed upon them in the Councils <note>These were provincial church synods or assemblies called to decide the fate of individual members.</note> that were held in almost every province. The most prominent were, as already mentioned, the Councils at Paris and at Reims, over which the Archbishops of Reims and Sens presided. He answered: the Order was dependent on the Roman Church and stood under its authority; the Popes had confirmed it and had granted it various privileges; they had the right to investigate whether it had deviated from its original foundation <term>original: "Einrichtung"; the Rule of the Order which dictated their religious and military life</term>, and to initiate legal proceedings against the Order; they could even decree that certain knights should undertake its defense; however, to want to entrust this to him alone, to organize this defense immediately, this was a proposal that put him in the greatest astonishment; for a matter of such importance, a much longer time was required; one has the example of Emperor Frederick I <note>Frederick I (Barbarossa). At the same time, the statement was read to the Grand Master that he had given in Paris before the Inquisitor <note>Guillaume de Paris, the King's confessor and Chief Inquisitor of France.</note> and had repeated at Chinon <note>A castle where high-ranking Templars were interrogated by representatives of the Pope in 1308.</note> before the three Cardinals. In this astonishment, he made the sign of the cross twice and said with a proud tone filled with indignation that if anyone other than the Pope's deputies, who held such high dignities, had dared to have such a statement recorded, he would well know what he would then have to say in response <note>This was a veiled threat; in the medieval code of honor, such a lie would typically be answered with a challenge to a judicial duel.</note>.

<margin>Astonishment and response of the Grand Master. <note>original: "Dupuy. Vertot."; referring to the historical works of Pierre Dupuy and René-Aubert Vertot.</note></margin>
The astonishment of the Grand Master, an honest man of genuine character, raised a great suspicion against the truthfulness of the interrogation in Paris and everything that had transpired at Chinon. Since he could neither read nor write—which was the case for nearly all people of rank at that time, especially those in the <term>military class <gloss>original: "Soldatenstande"; the social caste of knights and professional warriors</gloss></term>—this could give reason to think that the scribe who recorded the statements had drafted them as they had been [told to him] by others...

For with what audacity <note>original: "Stirn," literally "forehead," a German idiom for "gall" or "brazenness."</note> could this gentleman deny facts that he had confessed twice, and for which he had suffered a two-year imprisonment? Terrified by the tone in which the Grand Master had spoken—indeed, even believing that he had challenged them—they replied to him that they were not the kind of people to accept a challenge <note>In the medieval context, de Molay's indignant tone was perceived as a formal challenge to a judicial duel, which the church-appointed commissioners refused.</note>.

The Grand Master replied that this was not the meaning of his words; yet he embittered them even more by adding that he had meant to say that, regarding those who could be capable of such a crime—that is, of attributing such a statement to others—it would be good if the custom of the Saracens <note>A term used in the Middle Ages for Muslims.</note> and Tartars <note>A term referring to the Mongol and Turkic peoples of the East.</note> were introduced, who first cut off the heads of such great villains and then slice open their bellies.

<margin>Counter-response of the commissioners. Dupuy. <note>Refers to the historian Pierre Dupuy.</note></margin>
The commissioners, who interpreted this answer as referring to the punishment that the guilty knights deserved, replied that the Church—which held an abhorrence for the shedding of blood—contented itself with passing judgment concerning heresy <note>A belief or opinion contrary to orthodox religious doctrine.</note>, and thereafter handed the stubborn over to the secular arm <note>The "secular arm" refers to the civil legal system and government. By handing a prisoner over, the Church technically avoided "shedding blood" while knowing the civil authorities would execute the person.</note>.

When the matter of the defense of the Order <term>Order (the Knights Templar)</term> was raised again, the Grand Master requested that he might be permitted to discuss it with the Knight of Placian <note>Guillaume de Plaisians, a prominent legal advisor to King Philip IV of France. Despite being described here as a "friend," he was instrumental in the King's efforts to suppress the Templars.</note>, his friend. He was allowed to come, and the two went aside to speak with one another in private, so that they could not be heard. The Grand Master revealed to the Knight his resolution to undertake the defense of the Order. Placian pointed out to him the difficulties involved and the circumstances in which they found themselves, whereby he urged him not to act recklessly, nor to draw upon himself the blame for an unsuccessful outcome, nor to needlessly throw himself into ruin.

At Placian's suggestion, the Grand Master returned to the commissioners and requested a delay to consider the matter; this was granted to him until the 28th.

On the 27th, a proclamation was issued to invite the knights to present themselves for the defense of the Order. Since they had not yet made their arrangements, no one appeared.

<margin>The Grand Master demands to be brought to the Pope. Dupuy <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French historian and royal librarian who compiled the primary records of the Templar trial.</note>.</margin>

He said, therefore, that his conscience compelled him to point out to them that he knew of no religious order in which the service owed to God was conducted with more dignity and propriety; none, if one excepted the cathedral churches, where divine worship was accompanied by greater majesty, or where Christian charity was practiced in such rich measure. The Archbishop replied to him that all these actions were very praiseworthy, but were useless without faith, which is the foundation of the Catholic religion and which alone can make such deeds meritorious. Upon this, the Grand Master, who admitted this principle, delivered his confession of faith in a manner very much in accordance with all the teachings of the Catholic religion; whereby he assured them that every knight was instructed in it and practiced it.

<margin>
The Chancellor joins the discussion.
Dupuy.
<note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651) was a French scholar who published a collection of documents regarding the trial of the Templars, which serves as a primary source here.</note>
</margin>
During this discussion, the Chancellor, Guillaume de Nogaret, the King’s chief minister, had arrived. He turned to the Grand Master and said to him quite vehemently that the entire Order of the Templars <term>Tempelherren-Orden (The Knights Templar)</term> lay in ruin, and that it was recorded in the *Chronicle of the Abbey of St. Denis* that in the time of Saladin, the Sultan of Egypt, the Grand Master of this Order and the most prominent Commanders <term>Comthuren (the heads of local administrative cells of the Order)</term> had paid him homage. He added that the Sultan had been so well informed of the corruption of the Order that, upon the news that a great misfortune had befallen them, he had said aloud that it had happened to them as a punishment for their crimes, because all the knights had violated their law, denied their faith, and were one and all sodomites <note>This was a standard legal accusation in the Middle Ages used to strip an organization of its moral standing and legal protections.</note>.

The Grand Master seemed greatly astonished by these reproaches, and answered very modestly,

<margin>1309.</margin>

Guillaume de Beaujeu was the Grand Master of the Templars from 1273 to 1291; he died during the Siege of Acre.</note> an alliance had existed between the Order and the Sultan, which had caused offense to many knights; but that their grumbling had ceased when they learned that various places belonging to the Order lay in the middle of this prince's territories, or on the borders, and that these places, because they could not be defended, would have been lost, which had made the treaty with this infidel prince a matter of unavoidable necessity. Here the conversation ended; and the Grand Master, as he departed, asked the commissioners <term>Commissarien (the papal investigators appointed to examine the Order's conduct)</term> to obtain permission for him to hear Mass in his chapel and to have divine service held there. They praised his piety and promised to intercede for him and to have this favor granted.

<margin>Arrival of the knights for the investigation and for the defense of the Order. Dupuy. <note>Referring to the historian Pierre Dupuy.</note></margin>
This was, as it were, only the prelude to the defense of the Order. They undoubtedly wished to know the sentiments and the will of the Grand Master, primarily because he had retracted his first statement. His answers gave sufficient indication that he believed it had been foisted upon him <note>The Grand Master is suggesting that his previous "confession" was either forged or improperly recorded by the royal officials.</note>; but the contrary became clear from what followed.

Meanwhile, they continued to conduct investigations in the councils <term>Concilien (regional church assemblies)</term> of Paris and Reims, and the commissioners who were appointed for that purpose to hear the knights who [defended] the Order...

<meta>This page continues the account of the papal commission's investigation into the Knights Templar in Paris during late 1309 and early 1310. It follows the Grand Master Jacques de Molay's request for religious services and the arrival of knights from across France to testify.</meta>

->— )o( —<-

<margin>1309.</margin>
Those who were tasked with defending the Order <note>The Knights Templar</note> were also commissioned to interrogate its members. They were brought in from every province of France where they had been taken into custody, including several who had not yet been questioned. It took considerable time to transport them to Paris. Together with those whom the Pope had interrogated at Poitiers <note>Pope Clement V held court at Poitiers between 1307 and 1308 to oversee the initial stages of the Templar investigation.</note>, they numbered two hundred and thirty individuals; furthermore, outside witnesses who did not belong to the Order were also summoned. This resulted in a tedious legal procedure, and the commissioners <term>Commissioners (the papal investigators appointed to examine the Order's conduct)</term> spent from the month of September 1309 until the month of June 1310 conducting it.

<margin>Assembly for the defense of the Order. Dupuy. <note>Refers to Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar whose history of the Templar trial was a primary source for later writers.</note></margin>
When they had all arrived, the commissioners inquired as to those who wished to undertake the defense of the Order. Seventy-eight of them came forward, among whom none were from the hundred and forty who had previously been interrogated in Paris. This was not because many of the latter had not retracted their statements; rather, they were far too terrified by threats of the stake <note>original: "Drohungen mit dem Feuer." Under the legal practices of the time, a prisoner who retracted a confession was often declared a "relapsed heretic," a crime punished by being burned at the stake.</note> and too uncertain of their fate to join the ranks of the defenders.

It appears that these seventy-eight had not been accused of the crimes with which the Order was charged, and consequently had not incriminated it, as they came to Paris specifically to be interrogated and to defend the Order. After their names had been recorded, they were led...

It highlights the fear among the knights, noting that only 78 dared to defend the Order, while others were silenced by the threat of being burned as relapsed heretics.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the legal proceedings against the Knights Templar in 1309. <margin>1309.</margin>
each specifically wanted to defend the Order, and for this purpose be brought into the Council <note>original: "Concilium"; referring to the ecclesiastical assembly called to decide the fate of the Order.</note>, and there speak before the Pope and the Fathers of the Council.

The President replied that the Council was not yet ready to assemble; that he would then issue the orders he deemed appropriate regarding what concerned them; that in the meantime the Pope and the King had consented to their being admitted to defend the Order; that they must make use of this permission; that everything they presented would be faithfully recorded and sent to the Pope; and that if they did not obey these orders, they might perhaps no longer be accepted to present their arguments.

This threat brought them to a resolution; they appointed the priests of the Order, Pierre de Boulogne and Raynal de Prines; the <term>Commanders</term> <note>original: "Comthuren"; the heads of the Order's local estates or "commanderies."</note> Guillaume de Chambonet, de Bertro, Bertrand de Lartige, and Guillaume Fox; and four knights, Jean de Montroyal, Matthieu des Essars, Jean de Saint Leonard, and Guillaume de Guirisac; however, Boulogne was given the commission to act as the primary speaker after he had consulted with the others on the matter.

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal defense of the Knights Templar in 1309. Following the appointment of the ten representatives (procurators) mentioned on the previous page, Pierre de Boulogne begins his formal defense before the commission.</meta>

->— ) 0 ( —<-

the ten procurators <note>original: "Procuratoren"; legal representatives or agents authorized to act on behalf of the Order in court.</note> of the Order were also brought in.

Boulogne began his speech with a formal protest, stating that none of the arguments he was about to present should in any way prejudice the right belonging to the Order to appear only before a General Council <note>original: "General-Concilio"; a formal assembly of the whole Church, usually summoned by the Pope to decide on matters of faith or discipline.</note> in the presence of the Pope, their primary superior. He stated that he wished to justify the Order before the Pope and before this solemn assembly, and to convincingly demonstrate that its faith, its conduct, and its good reputation were untainted. He asserted that the procurators would prove this through indisputable acts and records <note>original: "Urkunden"; legal deeds or historical documents.</note>. Furthermore, he argued that as a preliminary point, it would be an act of injustice to deny all the knights the return of their freedom and the use of their property so that they might attend the Council. This would allow them to appear in a state and with the dignity befitting their birth and rank, and to use their own assets for their maintenance as well as for travel expenses and the necessary costs of gathering and producing all their records and documents, as well as all witnesses who were to serve in their defense. He then stated that, in the name of the entire Order, he denied everything that had been alleged to its detriment and declared it null and void:

That everything of which the Order was accused was abominable, shameful, and horrifying:

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal defense of the Knights Templar during the trials of 1309. Pierre de Boulogne, acting as the Order's representative, is presenting a formal rebuttal to the accusations brought against the knights before the Papal Commission in Paris.</meta>

->— ) 0 ( —<-

<margin>1309.</margin>
That all the articles <note>In this legal context, "articles" refers to the specific list of charges or accusations brought against the Order.</note> upon which the knights had been interrogated were shameful, false, slanderous, abominable, unbelievable, and even ridiculous; that it was incomprehensible how any credit could have been given to them; that they were as godless as they were contrary to all probability; and that they had been invented and devised only by their mortal enemies—by apostates <note>original: "Abtrünnigen"; those who had abandoned their vows or been expelled from the Order.</note> who had been cast out of the Order for their crimes, and by villains who were worse than heretics and infidels. These individuals, seeking only to avoid their own deserved punishment, intended to defame and bring to ruin a pure, holy, and spiritual Order that was without blemish, and thereby to sow the seeds of discord within the Church of Christ:

That he conceded that the many statements and confessions made by the knights during the interrogations in Paris and other cities created a negative prejudice <note>original: "Vorurtheil"; here meaning a legal bias or a preconceived judgment formed before the full trial.</note> against the Order; but that one must not be misled by this deceptive appearance; that one must investigate and research how these statements were made; that they were all false and extracted through force, as it was publicly known that the knights had yielded to the intensity of the torment <note>original: "Marter"; refers to physical agony or martyrdom.</note>; that they should not be blamed, nor should these shameful confessions—made under the intensity of cruel torture <note>original: "Folter"; the systematic use of physical pain to extract evidence.</note>—be held too heavily against them; that although there was a large number who had not been placed on the rack <note>original: "Folterbank"; a torture device designed to stretch the victim's limbs.</note>—

He urges the commission to look past these forced statements to see the Order's true, unblemished nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal defense of the Knights Templar during the 1309 Papal Commission. Pierre de Boulogne is arguing that the confessions obtained from the knights are legally invalid because they were extracted through the psychological and physical terror of torture.</meta>

->— )( —<-

<margin>1309.</margin>
laid <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "confessions made under [the pressure] laid [before them]."</note> before them; but that they were nonetheless no less excusable for it, because fear had produced the same effect upon them as the torture actually employed upon others, and that they had been unwilling to submit to the latter after having seen their fellow brothers with broken, dislocated limbs and in a wretched state; that they had, to be sure, in this instance displayed signs of a weakness and timidity unbecoming of their rank <note>original: "Stande"; referring to their status as noble knights and sworn monks.</note>, as they ought to have preferred a glorious martyrdom to the abomination of betraying the truth; but, since only
<margin><unclear>retracts again</unclear></margin>
few such noble and magnanimous souls were to be found who were capable of sacrificing their lives, the faint-heartedness of these knights in their bonds <note>original: "Banden"; referring to their imprisonment and chains.</note>, and their fear of death, gave no legal weight to the statements made under these circumstances.

That it was not unknown to him that there were several knights who had confessed to abominable things without having endured torture, or even without having been threatened with it; but that it was nonetheless certain, and the proof of it easy, that these were people without conscience who had yielded to seduction, who had taken money, to whom grand promises had been made, and whose statements—which were invalid under all laws—must be disregarded; but that there were also noble and undaunted hearts to be found who had enough courage to maintain the innocence of the Order, and that after the investigation had taken place, a great number of knights [retracted] their...

<meta>This page continues Pierre de Boulogne’s legal defense of the Knights Templar. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing those "noble and undaunted hearts" who had endured hardship but ultimately remained loyal to the Order.</meta>

<margin>1309.</margin>
...dured the pressure, but then <note>This completes the thought from the previous page: "who had en[dured...]"</note> retracted their statements, and had thereby exposed themselves to the greatest danger; a retraction which, since it occurred voluntarily and was linked with such peril, undoubtedly nullified their previous [forced] confessions. He spoke out vehemently regarding the character of the apostate informants <note>original: "abtrünnige Denuncianten"; refers to former members or accusers who betrayed the Order.</note>, who were loathed and despised by the nation; he insisted that they should be taken into custody to be formally questioned and confronted with the knights, for whom it would not be difficult to put them to shame and destroy their shameful accusation.

<margin>Manifesto of the Order. Düpuy. <note>Refers to Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar who wrote extensively on the trial of the Templars.</note></margin>
Boulogne then read a manifesto that had been drafted for the defense of the Order; its content was nearly identical to his speech. However, since some matters are presented differently within it, it is useful to cite them. He read it with a fire and an unction <note>original: "Salbung"; a term suggesting a deeply sincere, spiritual, and persuasive eloquence.</note> which revealed his grief as much as his conviction of the Order’s innocence. This manifesto stated that a holy Order was being attacked—one founded on Christian and brotherly love; that at its founding, the sole intention had been to defend Christians against the infidels, especially in the Promised Land <note>The Crusader states in the Levant.</note>, even at the cost of the knights' own blood; that the Order had placed itself under the protection of the Blessed Virgin, and that it had been confirmed by the Popes, who, in consideration of its services, had granted it great privileges; and that this Order, through the grace of Jesus Christ and through the assistance of this divine Protectress...

<margin>1309.</margin> ...that the truth would reveal itself before the Pope, their only natural judge; that none among them could withdraw from his jurisdiction, and that the Grand Master <note>Jacques de Molay, the last Grand Master of the Knights Templar.</note> himself could not exempt himself from it; that he, like the others, must submit to it, and that he would go there to give an account of his statement to him, if he had been timid enough to make a false one. He spoke at length about the holiness and regularity of the Order, maintaining that all priests within it consecrated the body and blood of Christ <note>A reference to the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note> according to the custom of the Catholic Church. He cited the example of the Knight of Sens, who had left the Order due to a doubt of conscience to enter a stricter one, but had returned to the Order because he realized that he could only find salvation within it. Finally, he expressed indignation at the ridiculousness of the accusation brought against them—that they denied Jesus Christ—and cited as proof of this false allegation the incident of the twenty-four knights who had been taken prisoner by the Sultan during the last war against the infidels <note>original: "Ungläubigen"; referring to the Muslim forces during the Crusades.</note>; "he offered them," he added, "life, freedom, and the highest positions of honor if they would become Muslims <note>original: "Mahometaner".</note>. They rejected it with indignation and all suffered as martyrs. Had they denied Jesus Christ upon their admission, what would it have mattered to them then to deny him once more? Would they have been foolish enough to perish in such a miserable way if they [had] already <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

When none of the other procurators <term>Procurators: legal representatives or advocates appointed to manage a case</term> stepped forward to speak, the Archbishop of Narbonne <note>Gilles Aycelin de Montagu, an influential advisor to King Philip IV of France.</note> answered—after he had first consulted with his colleagues—that the procurators had asserted two points in their speeches which they could not prove: first, that the Order had been beyond reproach before the proceedings initiated against it, because the opposite was evident from the Pope’s own Bull <term>Papal Bull: a formal public decree or charter issued by a Pope</term>, in which he stated that the ill repute of the Order had reached his ears *); and second, that the Pope alone had the right to pass judgment on the accusations brought against the Order; that even if one assumed His Holiness had this right, he could nevertheless delegate it to his representatives, and that they all had full authority from the Pope; but that, since this was a matter of heresy and crimes against the...

---

*) The Archbishop of Narbonne showed himself here to be the worst kind of legal trickster <term>original: "Chicaneur"; one who uses petty legal technicalities to harass or deceive</term>. Did the Papal Bull appear before the proceedings against the Knights Templar, or after they had already been forced to confess through torture? And did the Pope not know what "evil" he knew of the Order solely from the King? How then could one refer to the Papal Bull and cite it as proof against the Order's blamelessness prior to the proceedings? Translator's note.

1309. [if matters of] faith <note>Continuing from the previous page: "since this was a matter of heresy and crimes against the faith..."</note> were the subject of discussion, the judgment regarding it, independent of the authority of the Pope, would legally belong to the bishops.

As for the demand they had made that they might be restored to freedom and the possession of their property, they should not address the commissioners regarding this, as the commissioners had not taken the Knights into custody, nor had they ordered their property to be confiscated; rather, everything was in the hands of the Pope, who could do as he wished in the matter, and who had entrusted this administration to the Cardinal of Palestrina <note>Bérenger Frédol (c. 1250–1323), a high-ranking canon lawyer and Cardinal-Bishop of Palestrina (Latin: *Praeneste*).</note>, to whom they could turn for that reason:

That, as far as they <term>the commissioners</term> were concerned, their only duty was to examine them according to the legal form, to question them as well as the other Knights, and to faithfully incorporate all the answers and arguments of the Knights into their reports.

Thus ended the first conference for the defense of the Order. The Templars were led back to prison, where Bologna <note>Peter of Bologna, a priest and the Order's most prominent legal advocate, known for his spirited defense against the charges of heresy.</note> and his fellow brothers drafted a new manifesto to confirm what he had said and to add further arguments.

<margin>1309.</margin>
[Peter of] Bologna and the other procurators <term>legal representatives</term> of the Order appeared there to be present for the oath that the summoned witnesses were to take. These witnesses were of two kinds: Knights and outsiders <note>German: *Fremde*. In this legal context, these were witnesses who were not members of the Templar Order and thus considered more objective.</note>. The latter maintained that they had knowledge of the disorders within the Order, and their testimony was to be of even greater weight since they were not an interested party. The former group had been brought by the Provost <note>A high-ranking administrative or law enforcement official.</note> of Poitiers and by Jean de Janville, under whose supervision they had been kept. They were permitted to give testimony for both the prosecution and the acquittal; however, it appears it was well known that they intended to accuse the Order. There were nineteen of them, whose names we wish to list here; though they have been unknown until now, they may still be found within their families.

Among them were Jean de Seminiac, from the diocese <note>German: *Kirchensprengel*. This refers to the administrative district under a bishop's jurisdiction.</note> of Sens, and Jean de Falage, both priests of the Order, who appeared in secular clothing as proof that they intended to accuse the Order. Furthermore, Jean de Javeni and Jean de Chevre-Coeur, from the diocese of Beauvais, the latter of whom wore the Order’s habit and cloak; Jean de Taillefer, Hugues de Bury, from the diocese of Langres; Geofroi Thanton, from the diocese of Tours, and Jean Langlois, from the diocese of Loudun. There were enough other witnesses present who were harmful to them. A large number of those whom the commissioners interrogated confessed to being guilty. They stated that they had appeared before the councils <term>Concilien</term> of Paris and Reims, were reconciled with the Church there, had voluntarily laid aside the Order’s cloak <term>Ordens-Mantel</term>, and had allowed themselves to be shorn <note>To have one's hair or beard cut, a symbolic act indicating they were no longer members of the fighting order.</note>, as if they were no longer Knights Templar. <margin>1310.</margin>
1. That no example of a similar procedure <term>Procedur</term> <note>The formal legal trial or judicial process.</note> had ever been seen, which had been conducted with such great haste in a matter of such importance, and against such a venerable and famous Order <term>Orden</term>;

2. That in this procedure, none of the formalities prescribed by the laws <term>Geſetzen</term> had been observed;

3. That hatred, rage, injustice, and violence alone had presided over the matter;

4. That without proof and without investigation, they had begun by having all the knights <term>Ritter</term> taken into custody <term>Verhaft</term> on one and the same day; that they had been dragged into dark dungeons <term>Kerker</term> and had been judged guilty before they had even been accused or questioned;

5. That at the same time, they had seized all of their movable goods <term>Mobilien</term> <note>Personal property such as furniture, money, and equipment, distinct from real estate.</note> and estates <term>Güter</term>, thereby violating the law <term>Geſetz</term> which forbids taking possession of such things while the defendant is sitting in prison <term>Gefängniſſe</term>;

6. That the witnesses <note>original: "Zeugen"</note> had not been left their freedom during the interrogations <term>Verhöre</term> that were conducted; instead—in order to bring them to a confession of whatever was beneficial to the rage of their enemies and the harbored intention to ruin and abolish the Order—they had begun by putting them on the rack <term>Folter</term> <note>German: *Folter*, referring to judicial torture used to extract confessions.</note> and cruelly agonizing and martyring them, so that many

7. <meta>This page continues the list of grievances and legal demands presented by Pierre de Boulogne, a priest and defender of the Knights Templar, during the papal commission's hearings in Paris in 1310. It concludes the tenth point regarding the manipulation of witnesses and lists four specific procedural demands for the defense.</meta>

<header>->— ) 0 ( —<-</header>

<margin>1310.</margin>...ters had been given to them, stating that they would strive in vain to defend an already condemned Order <term>Orden</term> <note>The Knights Templar, a wealthy and powerful Catholic military order founded to protect pilgrims to the Holy Land.</note>, and provided the assurance that, if they confessed to the things of which they were accused, they would be granted life, freedom, and a handsome allowance <term>Gehalt</term> <note>A pension or salary intended to ensure the former knights could maintain their social status after the Order's dissolution.</note>, in order to live honorably and comfortably outside the Order.

Following this, Boulogne presented various demands to the commissioners <term>Commissarien</term> <note>The papal commission appointed to investigate the Order in France.</note> in this manifesto, all of which aimed at the justification <term>Rechtfertigung</term> of the Order.

The first was that all these forced statements—which could not be detrimental to the Order’s blamelessness—should be declared null and void based on his solemn protestation of their obvious falsehood.

The second, that he be provided with the indictment and records of the investigations, so that he might dismantle them through legal refutation.

The third, that those knights who had testified nothing against the Order should be separated from the base, weak, and slanderous witnesses, who inspired terror in them and might thereby make them share in their own fear and frailty.

The fourth, that new legal inquiries should be conducted in secret, as was proper, and kept confidential until they were submitted to the Pope <term>Pabste</term> <note>Pope Clement V, who was under significant pressure from King Philip IV of France to suppress the Templars.</note>.

<margin>1310.</margin>
The fifth [demand was] that during these investigations, they should examine the jailers <term>Kerkermeister</term> and those who were present at the final words of the knights who died in prison from wounds they had received during torture <term>Folter</term> <note>The use of torture to extract confessions was a central and controversial element of the Templar trials.</note>. Furthermore, they should examine those who had testified according to the will of the commissioners <term>Commissarien</term>, had been reconciled with the Church, and had died a natural death. The purpose was to learn the declarations made by both groups in order to judge their true sentiments, which no one seeks to hide while dying.

The sixth, that those knights who had refused to testify—of whom there was a fairly large number—should also be admitted to the number of witnesses, and indeed compelled to do so, so that they might not maintain a criminal silence; that they should be required to take an oath <term>Eyd</term> to tell the truth, and to confess freely and naturally what occurred during their reception into the Order <term>Orden</term> and everything that was customarily practiced therein.

The seventh, that they should summon and question the Knight of Balincourt, a Parisian, who could easily be heard, to answer the question of whether it was not true that, inspired by an all-too-fervent zeal, he had left the Order to join the Carthusian Order <term>Kartheuser-Orden</term> <note>The Carthusians are a strictly cloistered monastic order known for their extreme austerity and silence; Balincourt's desire to join them suggests he sought a higher level of piety than he (initially) felt the Templars offered.</note>, which he had considered stricter and in which he believed he could more readily achieve salvation; and whether he had not, after realizing he had been mistaken, requested...
