<margin>1310.</margin> [desired] to re-enter the Order; whether he had not indeed re-entered it and submitted himself to the penance <term>Buße</term> <note>In the medieval context, penance was a formal process of atonement for sins or breaking monastic vows.</note> imposed upon the inconstant? This penance consists of appearing in the full Chapter <term>Kapitel</term> accompanied by several of one’s friends, falling to one’s knees before the Superior <term>Superior</term>, who then sentences the individual to eat on the floor in the Refectory <term>Refectorio</term> <note>The communal dining hall of a monastery or convent.</note> for a year and a day. During this time, he must fast on bread and water every Saturday, and attend the great Conventual Mass <term>Conventual-Messe</term> every following Sunday to receive the discipline <term>Züchtigung</term> <note>A ritualized public scourging or lashing performed as part of a formal ecclesiastical penance.</note> there. He added,

That Balincourt had submitted to all these conditions, and that only after such a long delay had they readmitted him and returned the habit <term>Ordens-Kleidung</term> of the Order to him. “He is still alive,” Boulogne cried out, “let him be brought here and questioned; he will confirm these facts and provide a report on how one conducts oneself in the Order and how one lives within it.”

> “What, my lords,” he continued, “can it truly occur to the minds of reasonable people that so many men of noble birth <term>vornehmer Abkunft</term> would have chosen a station and entered an Order in which they were suddenly made to change their religion? An Order they entered to be saved, yet to which they supposedly consented to their own eternal damnation? What, did not one of them resist? Did they all persist? They all committed abominable crimes; did no one say anything against it, did no one

<summary>Boulogne cites the case of the Knight of Balincourt, who endured severe public penance to rejoin the Templars, as proof of the Order's integrity. He argues it is illogical to believe noblemen would knowingly enter an Order that forced them into spiritual ruin.</summary>

<margin>1310.</margin>
...has he regretted it? Has the secret remained concealed for two centuries? These are unbelievable things that are not only false, but also ridiculous, absurd, and tasteless; they prove irrefutably the fury of their enemies and the unjust and abominable plan to destroy such a venerable Order. They demonstrate the falsehood of all the statements made by the knights whom force and fear have seduced into testifying to imaginary things and absurd circumstances—matters about which they were not even asked—because they believed they would thereby establish their fortune and earn the favor of the Prince <note>Refers to King Philip IV ("the Fair") of France, who initiated the arrests of the Templars to seize their wealth and cancel his debts.</note>.„ —

The Commissioners <term>Commissarien</term> <note>These were the members of the Papal Commission appointed by Pope Clement V to investigate the Order as a whole, rather than individual members.</note> answered not a word to these many reasons, and perhaps they would have been embarrassed by them. They contented themselves with accepting the Manifesto <term>Manifest</term>, and ordered four copies to be made of it, whereupon they sent the Procurators <term>Procuratoren</term> <note>Legal representatives or defenders acting on behalf of the Order.</note> of the Order back to their prisons.

He argued that this would be against all form and rule, because the Lords Commissioners had already instituted legal proceedings against them, and were still doing so, and they could not at the same time

The Commissioners did not know at first what they should answer to this demand, and the Procurators <term>Procuratoren</term> <note>Legal representatives for the defense.</note> of the knights departed without any objection being raised; in the afternoon, however, they were called back to have their defense heard further. They were told that this would be the last time, and that Pierre de Berac, who had been appointed as their guard, had orders to return them to the prisons of their respective provinces. Upon this, Boulogne renewed his appeal to the Pope, lodged all the protests which the situation of the knights required, and insisted on the demand that they be permitted to engage a legal counsel <term>Consulenten</term> to pursue their appeal, and that they be provided with the necessary funds to travel to the Pope, into whose protection they

While these proceedings were taking place, the two councils at Paris and Reims held their sessions and conducted investigations against the Templars. The King <note>Philip IV of France, known as "the Fair."</note>, being still fully convinced of the crimes of the Templars and never losing sight of their dissolution, had summoned a Parliament to Pontoise to support him; he regularly attended this assembly and received reports there on everything that occurred in both councils. He had the great lords of his realm with him, all of whom, being dependent on his will, did not hesitate to prove themselves in accordance with it. He resided in a neighboring abbey.

The two councils <note>The provincial church councils of Paris and Reims.</note> had not hurried so much, because they had a much larger number of witnesses to examine; they also remained assembled for a long time.

There are no written records remaining, neither of the investigation nor of the verdict that followed; however, since they proceeded according to the prescribed form, without violence and without putting the knights to the torture, one may suspect that this council ended in the same manner as the one in Salamanca.

<margin>1310.</margin> [The Bishops of] Avila, Ciudad Rodrigo, Astorga, Tuy, and Lugo conducted the investigations. Various witnesses were heard, and all the knights were found innocent of the crimes of which they were accused. This verdict was, however, not final; the decision was referred to the Pope <note>Pope Clement V, who was under significant pressure from King Philip IV of France to dissolve the Order.</note>. Although the Castilian knights had been recognized as innocent, they were nonetheless to share the same fate as the rest of the Order, as would be determined by a general council <term>Concilio</term> <note>The Council of Vienne, which met between 1311 and 1312 to decide the Order's future.</note>. Their estates therefore remained seized <note>original: "sequestrirt"</note>, and an accounting of them was rendered to the bishops.

Upon the first rumor that the knights were to be taken into custody, they retreated into these places, improved the fortifications, and put themselves in a state of defense. At the same time, they wrote to the Pope to complain about the persecution; but all were deaf to their cries. The King sent troops to take them into custody and to seize their commanderies <term>Comthureyen</term> <note>The basic administrative unit of the Knights Templar's landholdings, managed by a Commander.</note>. <margin>1310. 1311.</margin>
[...taken up arms] <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of Templars defending their fortresses.</note>; however, they were attacked in a no less dangerous way. Besides the proceedings that the Archbishops of Florence and Pisa instituted against them, the Archbishop of Ravenna had also assembled a council <term>Concilium</term> <note>The Council of Ravenna (1310) is notable in Templar history for its relatively fair treatment of the accused.</note> at the seat of his archdiocese; he conducted investigations against the knights there and brought the accusations before the full assembly of the council. <meta>This page continues the account of the trials against the Knights Templar. The previous page ended with the Pope's orders to the Archbishops of Germany to begin investigations and prepare for the trials of the knights within their jurisdictions.</meta>

<margin>1310.</margin>
[...to conduct the trial] <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page regarding the investigations in Germany.</note>, so that the Knights Templar <term>Tempelherren</term> had no cause to complain, and had as much time for their defense as they could ever desire.

<margin>
In Cyprus.
Dupuy <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar whose history of the Templar trial was a major source for this text.</note>.
</margin>
In Cyprus, force had to be used to carry out the Pope’s orders. All the Knights Templar had gathered at Nimove <note>Limassol, a major port city in Cyprus where the Templars were headquartered after the fall of Acre.</note>, at which place they always had access to the open sea; but when they saw that they were about to be besieged there, and that the whole kingdom was in arms against them—and moreover, as they relied upon their own innocence—they changed their minds. When it was least expected, they traveled to Nicosia, with the Marshal, the Preceptor, the Draper <term>Drapier</term> <note>The high official responsible for the Order's clothing and equipment.</note>, and the Treasurer of the Order at their head. Ten knights followed them, and they submitted to whatever would be decreed by the Pope, their superior. The Regent of Cyprus had their weapons and horses taken from them, ordered them all into custody, and brought them to a decent prison, after which he sent word of this to the Pope. It is not reported that any proceedings were initiated against them at that time; since they no longer had their freedom, they likely had to resign themselves to the same fate as the knights in the other kingdoms.

<margin>
New Appeal of the Templars.
Dupuy.
Vertot <note>René-Aubert Vertot (1655–1735), another historian known for writing about the military orders.</note>.
</margin>
In France, they proceeded much more swiftly. The councils of Sens and Reims, which were held in Paris and Senlis so that they might consult with one another due to their proximity, hurried to bring the proceedings to an end—

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the legal proceedings against the Knights Templar following the arrests and initial investigations mentioned on the previous page. It details the interrogations conducted before ecclesiastical councils and the difficulties the knights faced in their defense.</meta>

<page-num>181</page-num>
<header>— ) 0 ( —</header>

<margin>1310.</margin>
[...to bring them] <note>The sentence begins on the previous page, likely referring to bringing the knights to trial.</note> to justice. These proceedings lasted a year and a half, and three hundred and thirty-one Knights Templar <term>Tempelherren</term> were interrogated. The basis for their interrogation was always the first statement they had made before the Inquisitor or his delegates. They were not permitted to renounce those earlier testimonies. In vain did fifty-nine of them declare, by way of retraction, that they had only given their testimony under duress or out of fear of torture <note>The use of torture was a standard part of the inquisitorial process to extract confessions, which the knights later tried to rescind once they felt safer or were before a different body.</note>, and that everything they had said was false; they were confronted neither with their accusers nor with one another, even though several had accused their own fellow brothers. The Fathers at the councils, in justifying their conduct, appealed to the orders of the Pope <term>Pabst</term> <note>original: "Pabstes"</note> and to the full powers he had granted them.

In the meantime, the procurators <note>Legal representatives or advocates assigned to the Order.</note> for the defense of the Order feared that the commissioners, before whom they had presented their defense, might not have made the Fathers of the two councils aware of the appeal they had lodged with the Pope; therefore, they found ways to get the appeal into the hands of the councils' own procurators. This, however, was of no benefit to the defendants.

<margin>the Pope, to pass judgment upon them. Dupuy <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar and lead historian on the trial of the Templars.</note>.</margin>
In this matter, these councils proved themselves to be in accordance with the Papal Bulls <term>Bullen</term> <note>Official decrees issued by the Pope.</note> and the will of the Pope. The Kings of France, England, Castile, and Aragon had reported to him on everything that had occurred within their respective states; they pressed him to bring this matter to a conclusion,

<summary>This page describes the judicial process against 331 Knights Templar in 1310, highlighting the denial of legal rights, the dismissal of retractions made under torture, and the unified pressure from European monarchs on the Pope to end the trial.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the second book of the history, continuing the description of the legal maneuvers orchestrated by Pope Clement V against the Knights Templar in 1310. It transitions from the local interrogations to the preparations for the ecumenical Council of Vienne.</meta>

<page-num>182</page-num>
<header>— ) 0 ( —</header>

<margin>1310.</margin>
...[defense] <note>The sentence continues from the previous page, likely completing a thought about the inability of the Order's legal representatives to secure a fair hearing.</note> and to allow a judgment to be pronounced upon the Knights Templar <term>Tempelherren</term>. Following this, the Pope <term>Pabst</term> <note>original: "Pabst"</note> issued various bulls <term>Bullen</term> <note>A formal proclamation or edict issued by the Pope, named after the "bulla" or lead seal attached to the document.</note>, by means of which he permitted the Fathers of the two councils <note>These were local ecclesiastical councils acting under the Pope's authority to process the mass of accused knights.</note> to speak as his representatives in delivering a final verdict over the knights. In their sentence, they were to include not only the Order as a whole, but every individual Knight Templar specifically. They were authorized to compel witnesses to testify through all means and methods, and even to call upon the secular arm <term>weltlichen Arm</term> <note>This refers to the civil authorities and the power of the state, which the Church used to carry out physical punishments, such as executions, that the clergy were canonically forbidden from performing themselves.</note> for assistance. He made an exception for the Grand Master <term>Großmeister</term> and the Grand Priors <term>Großprioren</term>, over whom he reserved the right of inquiry and the final verdict for himself; his intention was to judge them at the Council of Vienne <note>original: "Concilio zu Vienne"</note>, which was to begin in the following year. Through another bull, he invited all prelates, doctors, and clergy to present themselves there.

# History
### of the Abolition
# of the Order of the Knights Templar.
## Book Three.

<margin>
1311.
End of the
Councils
of Sens
and Reims.
Dupuy.
Vertot.
<note>Pierre Dupuy and René-Aubert Vertot were influential historians; Dupuy published a collection of documents on the trial in 1654, and Vertot wrote a famous history of the Knights of Malta in 1726.</note>
</margin>
The proceedings initiated against the Order of the Knights Templar <term>Tempelherren-Orden</term> were already deep into their fourth year, and throughout this entire time, the knights were forced to languish in prisons until finally the Councils <term>Concilien</term> <note>Ecclesiastical meetings of church leaders convened to decide on matters of doctrine or discipline; in this case, acting as a court.</note> of Sens and Reims, which were to decide their fate, concluded their sessions. The Council of Sens had been held in Paris, as previously mentioned, and the Council of Reims in Senlis, so that the proximity of these two cities might make it easier for the Fathers of both councils to act in agreement during the proceedings and in the verdict that was to be handed down. All the investigations had been gathered there, consisting of four hundred and five witness testimonies, including seventy-two statements that the Pope <term>Pabst</term> <note>original: "Pabst"</note> had recorded in Poitiers in the year 1308. The commissioners, to whom the three

1311. manifestos brought by the procurators <term>Procuratoren</term> <note>Legal representatives or proxies appointed to handle the Order's defense.</note> of the Order for its defense had been handed over, they had delivered them to the Council of Sens, together with the act of the lodged appeal to the Pope <note>original: "Pabst"</note>, which these procurators had made known to the Council. Everything was investigated, and the verdict was passed that no heed should be paid to it <note>The council essentially dismissed the Order's legal appeals out of hand.</note>. This opinion even reached the Pope, who approved it, and it was decided to proceed further to the final judgment.

It seemed only fair that one could not refrain from confronting both the foreign witnesses and the knights with one another; because many did not only testify about their own actions but also accused other knights, who could have refuted these testimonies and proven their falsehood; however, the tediousness of this procedure and the great number of witnesses caused this to be rejected. It was believed that the crimes were sufficiently proven, and no confrontation took place. They were weary of having spent 18 months on this trial.

Regarding the first classes, no difficulty was found. Those of the third class, however, were pierced by the most vivid grief. For whether their statements had been true or false, they could not have been more severely punished than to spend their lives between four walls, without company, without comfort, with poor food, and without hope. To people of spirit, death seems easier, and they become envious of the fate of animals, which at least have their freedom. Their fate seemed harder to them than that which threatened the hardened heretics <term>verhärteten Ketzer</term> <note>Those who had retracted their confessions and were thus sentenced to death.</note>.

For these latter men—after the priestly orders had been stripped from those whose status <term>Character</term> <note>In canon law, this refers to the "sacramental character" or the indelible mark of holy orders; a priest had to be formally "degraded" or defrocked before he could be handed over to secular authorities for execution.</note> subjected them to this shameful formality—the field adjoining the Abbey of St. Anthony was designated for the execution of the sentence. There, pyres <term>Scheiterhaufen</term> were erected at some distance from the abbey’s mill. The field was large and well-suited for erecting several pyres and for accommodating the vast number of spectators who were expected to be present.

execution.

The knight did not recant. He was burned alive and his <note>The text ends mid-sentence.</note>

<margin>1311.</margin>
...his punishment did not frighten those who were condemned to the same fate.

<margin>Second execution. *Dupuy. Turcelin. Vertot. Fleury.*</margin>
Eight days later, the second execution took place on the same spot in the Field of St. Antoine <note>The *Champ de Saint-Antoine*, located near the abbey of the same name in Paris.</note>, the most terrifying spectacle that had ever been seen in France. Fifteen to twenty pyres had been lit; they did not burn with bright flames but looked like so many fiery beds meant to burn the guilty slowly, filled only with glowing coals. An unbelievable number of people from Paris and the surrounding area—from towns and villages for several miles around—had traveled there upon receiving news of this execution. Fifty-four knights, from among those who had retracted their first statements <note>The knights had initially confessed under torture but later "relapsed" by declaring their order innocent, an act which, under the Inquisition's law, led to being burned at the stake.</note>, were brought there on carts. They were made to dismount so they could be thrown into these pans of coal and burned by a slow fire.

Before they proceeded, a proposal was made to them: to renounce their retraction and confirm their original testimony. They were urged most intensely to do so, and to persuade them, it was suggested that they were sacrificing themselves in vain for an order that had already been abolished. At the same time, they were shown a royal patent <term>königliches Patent</term> <note>A formal legal document or decree issued by King Philip the Fair.</note> that granted a general amnesty to all the guilty and promised them freedom and a pension, so that they might spend the rest of their lives in comfort and peace. Equally un...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the mass execution of fifty-four Knights Templar in Paris in 1311. The previous page ended mid-sentence describing a royal patent offered to the knights, which would have granted them amnesty if they stood by their original confessions of guilt.</meta>

-> — ) 0 ( — <-

<margin>1311.</margin>
...[amnesty; yet, no more] moved by these advantages than by the fear of the punishment they had before their eyes, they refused it; they declared that they were innocent of all the crimes of which they were accused, and that they had been forced into their confessions only through violence and the severity of torture <term>Martern</term> <note>A term for judicial torture, which was a standard part of legal proceedings at the time to extract "truthful" confessions.</note>.

Since they were all men of rank, being relatives and friends of the most prominent and respected persons at court and in the city, they were surrounded by a large number of their kin and friends who exhorted them to obey the King's command and not to perish so miserably through the cruelest of all death penalties. Tears, pleas, and entreaties—everything was used, but in vain. They constantly maintained that they were innocent, and that through their retraction <note>By "retracting" their earlier confessions, the knights were legally considered "relapsed heretics," a status that carried a mandatory death sentence by fire.</note> they had borne witness to the truth.

One had to proceed, then, to the execution of this cruel sentence. The fifty-four knights were thrown, bound, into these pans of coal <note>These were large braziers or pits filled with glowing coals, intended to prolong the execution through slow, intense heat rather than a quick-burning flame.</note>. Instead of the laments, wailing, and cries that one expected to hear from them, they did not utter even a single sigh. They displayed an admirable steadfastness, calling upon the name of God, praising Him <gloss>benedeyeten</gloss> <note>From the Latin *benedicere*, meaning to bless or praise.</note>, and taking Him as witness to their innocence. In this manner they died, consumed by a slow fire.

<margin>
Unrest and
Astonishment
</margin>
This spectacle filled the entire crowd that attended it with astonishment and ter—

<summary>This page describes the final moments of fifty-four Knights Templar who, despite the pleas of their families and the threat of a slow death, refused to reaffirm their forced confessions and died claiming innocence. --- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the public's reaction to the burning of fifty-four Knights Templar in Paris. The previous page established that the knights chose a gruesome death by fire rather than reaffirming their forced confessions to receive the King's amnesty.</meta>

-> — ) 0 ( — <-

<margin>The same. 1311.</margin>
Terror, compassion, and horror seized all minds. Everyone was struck with loathing. People lamented and admired so many men of rank <term>Leute vom Stande</term> <note>This refers to the knights' noble status, which usually granted them certain legal protections or at least a more "honorable" form of execution than burning.</note> who were sacrificed in such a cruel manner. One could not believe men of such rank and merit were guilty, who in the flower of their youth voluntarily perished, choosing a terrible death over life and the conditions that had been offered to them. If they were guilty, was it not the height of madness to die for such a cause? By doing so, they only increased their crimes as they appeared before the Highest Judge <note>A reference to God as the ultimate arbiter of truth and justice.</note> to be burdened with even more terrible punishments. Yet, if they did not believe in this inexorable Judge, what advantage would they have in renouncing their lives?

The entire populace was consequently thrown into intense unrest, emotion, and agitation. It was the spectators who shed tears and lamented the fate of the dying. Almost everyone considered them innocent, despite the public rumors that ascribed such abhorrent vices to them.

Others, however, who considered them convicted by virtue of such a great number of testimonies, viewed this steadfastness as a brutish stubbornness <term>Halsstarrigkeit</term> <note>A theological term implying a sinful refusal to submit to the "truth" or repent before the Church.</note>, as madness, and as a just judgment of God, who had blinded them and allowed them to die in final impenitence.

<margin>
1311.
Third execution.
Dupuy.
Türcelin.
</margin>
Now there were still ten Templars left to be executed. They had been held back, perhaps in the hope that they would be terrified by the news of the death of their brothers and would repent; perhaps also to move a Royal Almoner <term>Almosenier</term> <note>An official in charge of distributing charity, usually a priest or high-ranking member of the royal household.</note> who was among this number. It was a vain hope. He was led with four others on the day before Ascension Day to the same field of the Abbey where a pyre had been built. The same offer was made to them; they despised it and died with equal steadfastness.

The Knights of Villars and of Cugé did not follow the example of these sixty-five martyrs of the Order. They renounced their retraction <note>By renouncing their "retraction," they effectively went back to their initial confessions of guilt, thereby satisfying the King's legal requirements for mercy.</note> and saved their lives. They said afterwards that life was too precious to be squandered in such a foolish way, adding that when they had seen their fifty-four brothers being driven on carts to St. Anthony’s Field to be burned, they were seized with such terror that they would have said anything that was not true; indeed, had it been required, they would have confessed even more to escape such a punishment.

<margin>
Exhumation of the Knight de Tur.
Dupuy.
Türcelin.
</margin>
The astonishment and terror of the Parisians increased further when they saw that, not satisfied with punishing the knights who were still alive, they even went after the dead. The Knight Jean de Tür <note>Jean de Tours, who served as the Treasurer of the Temple and Grand Prior of France.</note> had already passed away several years prior. He had earned a great name for himself in the Order, had been the Grand Prior of France, and had in the Pal:

<margin>1311.</margin>
palace of the Temple, where he lived, and had built the tower that was its most beautiful ornament. <note>This refers to the Great Tower of the Temple in Paris, a massive fortress-like structure that stood until the early 19th century and is famous for being the prison of Louis XVI during the French Revolution.</note> Some of the knights who had given testimony had accused him in their statements of being guilty of the same crimes. This was sufficient to put his memory on trial, to declare him convicted of these crimes, and to condemn him to the fire. This meant violating the laws of the state, which command that death shall bring an end to all criminal proceedings if a verdict has not yet been rendered; even more so when the proceedings have not even begun. But so great at that time was the rage ignited against an Order that was considered abominable.

This Commander <term>Comthur</term> <note>A title for a high-ranking administrative official in a military order, in charge of a "commandery" or local estate.</note> was therefore condemned to the fire according to the judgment that had been passed against him. His half-rotted corpse was dug up, and his bones were burned to ash.

<margin>
Proof of the informants.
Mezeray.
<note>original: "Mezerin." Likely a reference to François Eudes de Mézeray, a prominent 17th-century French historian.</note>
</margin>
The two apostates who had betrayed the Order and were the primary cause of this tragedy triumphed and walked proudly with heads held high through Paris, enjoying the King's favors and rejoicing over the consequences of their accusation. There is every reason to believe that they were detested by all honest people, especially after everyone had been made uncertain by the protestations of those executed and by their fearlessness in the face of death.

<meta>This page continues the account of the persecution of the Knights Templar in 1311. Following the trials in Paris, the narrative shifts to show how the influence of the French Court extended into Provence and Piedmont, resulting in further executions and King Philip IV's strategic plans to seize the Order's wealth.</meta>

-> — ) o ( — <-

<margin>1311. Executions in Provence. Vertot. <note>original: "Vertot." Referring to Abbé René-Aubert de Vertot, a popular 18th-century historian who wrote extensively on the history of the Knights of Malta and the Templars.</note></margin>
Since the states of the King of Naples <note>At this time, Robert the Wise of the House of Anjou was King of Naples and also the Count of Provence, which explains why French influence was so strong there.</note> followed all the maxims and impressions received from the French Court, the officials of this prince in Provence and Piedmont acted accordingly as soon as they received news of the verdicts from the Councils of Paris and Reims. They pressured all the knights who had retracted their confessions to renounce those retractions and confirm their original statements; the knights refused and were all burned alive. Neither their numbers nor their names have been preserved for us.

<margin>Proposal for a royal order. Dupuy. <note>original: "Düpuy." Referring to Pierre Dupuy, a French scholar and librarian to the King, known for his 1654 work on the trial of the Templars.</note></margin>
The verdict of the Councils, which had released the Templars from their vows and obligations, gave a clear enough indication of the underlying intention to abolish their Order. Because of this, the King <note>Philip IV "the Fair" of France.</note> returned once again to his plan of founding a new royal and military order to which all of the Templars' property could be transferred. Perhaps he did not yet despair of uniting all other military orders with this new one and having his second son, Prince Philip, elected as its Grand Master. With this goal in mind, he had already written a long letter to the Pope on May 12th, in which he argued that since the crimes of the Templars were so monstrous and so legally proven, His Holiness and the Council could not help but exterminate them, abolish their Order, and found a new military order to which all their assets and privileges must be granted. He did add, however, that these assets could also be given to an older order of the same purpose...

<summary>This page describes the spread of Templar executions into Provence and Piedmont and details King Philip the Fair’s political scheme to dissolve the Order and seize its assets for a new royal order or his own family.</summary>
<keywords>1311, Provence, Piedmont, King of Naples, King Philip the Fair, Pope Clement V, Grand Master, executions, property seizure, Vertot, Dupuy</keywords>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the suppression of the Knights Templar in 1311. It details the negotiations between King Philip IV of France and Pope Clement V concerning the disposal of the Order’s vast wealth and the legal maneuvers used to funnel assets toward the Papal seat in Avignon.</meta>

-> — ) 0 ( — <-

<margin>1311.</margin>
...to assign [these assets to the new order], and he promised to carry out the resolutions of the <term>Council<note>Likely referring to the Council of Vienne (1311–1312), which was convened specifically to decide the fate of the Templars.</note></term> regarding this matter within his realm, though with the reservation of his own rights and those of the French prelates and lords.

The Pope <note>Clement V, who was under significant pressure from the French crown.</note> offered no response to the demand to exterminate the Templars. He knew that the provincial councils, through their verdicts, had already seen to that. He did not approve the founding of a new order, and he left the question of uniting these properties with an ancient, already established order to the decision of the general council.

<margin>Papal Bull regarding the income from the Templars' estates. Dupuy. <note>original: "Düpüy." Pierre Dupuy was a 17th-century scholar who documented the trial of the Templars.</note></margin>
Although the Pope and the King presented an appearance of taking no part in the disposal of these assets and of seeking no profit from them, they nevertheless did not fail to remain watchful over the use of the movable goods and the income. Accordingly, as early as May 12th, while residing in <term>Avignon<note>The city in modern-day France that served as the seat of the Popes from 1309 to 1376, a period often called the "Babylonian Captivity of the Church."</note></term>, the Pope had issued a <term>bull<note>An official papal decree or public letter.</note></term> charging the Archbishop of Rouen and the Bishops of Poitiers and Mende to demand an accounting from all administrators of the Templars' income, and to move the net surplus from the kingdom under royal protection. Through this, Avignon was clearly enough indicated as the intended destination, making it known that the Pope, with royal approval, intended to dispose of these revenues.

<margin>
1311.
In Italy.
Council at Ravenna
Fleury <note>Refers to Claude Fleury (1640–1723), a French priest and ecclesiastical historian whose multi-volume "Histoire ecclésiastique" is likely the source for this account.</note>.
</margin>
After the news of the verdicts from the councils at Sens and Reims had reached the neighboring states, the proceedings against the <term>Knights Templar<note>A wealthy and powerful Catholic military order during the Crusades, which faced sudden dissolution and trials for heresy starting in 1307.</note></term> were accelerated. Judicial proceedings were initiated against them in various provinces of Italy. A council was held at Ravenna, presided over by the Archbishop of that city, with the assistance of eight bishops, his <term>suffragans<note>Bishops who are subordinate to a metropolitan archbishop within a specific ecclesiastical province.</note></term>, and three inquisitors of the faith.

Twelve Templars had been taken into custody, brought there, and questioned. Since they confessed to none of the crimes of which they were accused, it was ordered—at the request of the inquisitors, men consumed by prejudice who always seek to find criminals—that they be placed upon the <term>rack<note>The original German uses "Folterbank," referring to the torture device used to stretch the body to extract confessions.</note></term>. Five were indeed placed upon it and confessed. The seven others were questioned without being tortured. They answered with the greatest steadfastness that all the accusations brought against them were nothing but falsehoods. The five others who had confessed were brought before them, but they did not change their testimony because of it.

After the matter had been taken into consideration by the Council, it was decided that the seven Templars should be questioned once more, but without being placed on the rack, or using any other form of violence to coerce their statements. They continued with
<meta>catchword: with</meta>

<summary>This page details the expansion of the Templar trials into Italy in 1311, specifically documenting the Council of Ravenna where seven knights maintained their innocence despite the use of torture on their companions.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, Council of Ravenna, Inquisition, torture, 1311, Archbishop of Ravenna, legal proceedings, heresy trials</keywords>

The result of the Council was therefore that these twelve knights were innocent. The decision regarding their fate was thus referred to the <term>Pope<note>Clement V, who was under significant pressure from King Philip IV of France to dissolve the Order.</note></term>, because it could easily be understood that, regardless of the guilt or innocence of the <term>Knights Templar<note>A Catholic military order founded in 1119, famous for its role in the Crusades and its eventual dissolution amidst accusations of heresy and corruption.</note></term>, the decision had already been made to abolish the Order.

<margin>
Düpüy <note>Refers to Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar and King's librarian who compiled historical documents regarding the trial of the Templars.</note>.
</margin>
However, this was not the intention of the fathers of this Council; their opinion was that those knights who were found guilty should be punished, and the innocent should be acquitted, but above all, the Order should be maintained if the majority were free of the crimes of which they were accused. The general Council was meant to deliver the final verdict on this matter.

In <term>Toscana<note>Tuscany, a region in central Italy.</note></term>, at the councils of Pisa and Florence, various knights were declared guilty and convicted of the crimes attributed to them. It is not known whether they had confessed to these, forced by <term>torture<note>The original text uses "Marter," referring to judicial torture used to extract confessions.</note></term>, and it is not recorded whether they were executed.

In Castile and Aragon. Düpüy.

<margin>1311.</margin>
They were not set at liberty, and the King had taken possession of all their estates. People were awaiting the decision of the general <term>Council<note>The Council of Vienne (1311–1312), called by Pope Clement V to decide the fate of the Templar Order.</note></term>.

In <term>Aragon<note>A kingdom in the northeast of modern-day Spain.</note></term>, the Chief Justice, **Don Artaud de Luna**, who had been appointed General of the Royal Army, pressed the Knights Templar hard. They had retreated to their strongholds and defended themselves bravely therein. But what could they achieve against the power of an entire kingdom, without assistance, and hated by all the people? De Luna thus besieged them in their fortresses one after another, and gradually captured <term>Monçon<note>Monzón Castle, the main Templar stronghold in Aragon.</note></term>, Miravette, Cantavieca, and Castelot. The siege of Monçon, the strongest of these cities, lasted a very long time; but finally, they had to surrender. All the knights were captured and taken to various prisons.

The Pope appointed the Bishop of Valencia to <term>instruct their trial<note>To prepare the legal case or conduct the preliminary investigation.</note></term>, of which no detailed account has been preserved.

<margin>1311.</margin> This bold and improper speech, and the sight of the knights' weapons—which, though half-concealed, were nonetheless visible—caused a great fright among the fathers of the <term>Council<note>original: *Concilium*. The provincial synod in Mainz gathered to decide the fate of the Knights Templar in the region.</note></term>, so much so that they feared a desperate strike might be carried out. The President hastily replied to them, saying he would grant them a formal legal <term>document<note>original: *Document*.</note></term> regarding their <term>appeal<note>original: *Appellation*. A legal challenge against a ruling, intended to be heard by a higher authority.</note></term>, notify the <term>Pope<note>original: *Pabste*. Referring to Clement V, who was instrumental in the suppression of the Templars.</note></term> of it, and <term>intercede<note>original: *intercediren*. To plead or mediate on another's behalf.</note></term> for them to the best of his ability, as the Council wished them no injustice nor intended to decree anything against them. The Count then retorted that justice would be done for them, and the Council could no longer doubt their innocence since a miracle had occurred: the flames had left several knights unharmed, even though they had been tied to a stake in the middle of a lit pyre.

The <term>Elector<note>original: *Churfürst*. The Archbishop of Mainz, who was one of the elite group of princes entitled to elect the Holy Roman Emperor.</note></term>, who was beside himself with fear, did not dispute this alleged miracle, though it was entirely false; he only repeated that they could all remain calm, and the Pope would certainly take the Council’s mediation into consideration.

Opinions regarding this miracle vary; some have claimed it occurred within the assembly itself, and that these twenty <term>Knights Templar<note>original: *Tempelherren*.</note></term> had placed fire upon their cloaks which did not burn, though this is likely a fairy tale.

The <term>Wildgrave<note>original: *Wildgraf*. A noble title (Latin: *comes ferus*) held by the leaders of certain Rhenish territories; here referring to Count Hugo von Grumbach.</note></term> and his friends then left the assembly, and the Elector held for them

<margin>1311.</margin>
He kept his word to them; he reported to the <term>Pope<note>Clement V, who was under significant pressure from the French King to dissolve the Templars.</note></term> what had occurred, represented to him how dangerous it would be to proceed against them, and presumably made him realize that these knights did not appear to be guilty. A Germanic military order that, unlike the Templars, maintained strong support from the German nobility.</note></term>.

This event shows that, if the knights had not been surprised and taken into custody by force all on the same day, they would have defended themselves; that one would have had great difficulty in overcoming them, and that the Order would have continued.

<margin>General Council of Vienne. <note>Sources: Pierre Dupuy, Turcelin, Claude Fleury, René-Aubert Vertot.</note></margin>
When at last the time appointed for the holding of a general <term>Council at Vienne<note>An ecumenical council of the Catholic Church that met between 1311 and 1312 to discuss the Templars and Church reform.</note></term> had arrived, the Pope traveled there as early as the beginning of September; he already found most of the Fathers there waiting for him; the others arrived bit by bit, and finally their number grew to three hundred, including Cardinals as well as Archbishops, Bishops, Prelates, Abbots, and Doctors.

The King of France appeared there in person with his three princes, <term>Louis<note>Louis X of France.</note></term>, King of Navarre, <term>Philip<note>Philip V of France.</note></term>, Count of Poitiers, <term>Charles<note>Charles IV of France.</note></term>, Count of La Marche, and with his brother <term>Charles<note>Charles of Valois.</note></term>, Count of Valois.

<margin>1311</margin>
A corps of troops accompanied him <note>Referring to Pope Clement V.</note>, though he did not take them with him into Vienne; he left them in neighboring places, yet close enough that they could carry out his orders.

The King of <term>Aragon<note>original: *Arragonien*. A kingdom in the northeast of modern-day Spain.</note></term> had sent his ambassadors to look after his interests and to prevent any disposal of the vast estates the Order possessed in his kingdom that might be to his disadvantage. These estates were the primary subject for which the <term>Council<note>original: *Concilium*. The Council of Vienne (1311–1312).</note></term> was held, and it was known to everyone that the abolition of the Order had been decided; it was now only a matter of deciding what to do with so many cities and <term>commanderies<note>original: *Comthureyen*. The administrative centers and estates managed by a Commander of a military order.</note></term> that the Order possessed.

<margin>The King proposes the establishment of a new order. <note>Sources: Pierre Dupuy and René-Aubert Vertot, prominent 17th/18th-century historians of the Templars.</note></margin>
Since King <term>Philip the Fair<note>original: *Philipp der Schöne*. King Philip IV of France, who orchestrated the downfall of the Templars.</note></term> had no further hope for the establishment of the royal order he had proposed—which would have granted his son, the <term>Count of Poitiers<note>Philip the Tall, the second son of Philip the Fair.</note></term>, a sovereignty or rather a very substantial kingdom—he restricted himself to asking the <term>Pope<note>Clement V.</note></term> that at least a new order might be founded. This new order would take the place of the <term>Order of the Templars<note>original: *Tempelherren-Orden*.</note></term> and serve as an edification to the Church, just as the former had (as he claimed) caused it scandal. The Pope was not of this opinion. The Order of the <term>Knights Templar<note>original: *Tempel-Ritter*.</note></term> had been established for the benefit of the <term>Holy Land<note>original: *gelobten Landes*.</note></term>; however, this was irretrievably lost. There was no indication that it could be reconquered; it would have

N 5

<margin>1310.</margin>
...it required much effort and many arrangements to found a new order, to prescribe its rules, and to find members for it. The <term>Order of Saint John of Jerusalem<note>Also known as the Hospitallers; a medieval and early modern Catholic military order.</note></term> was becoming more powerful by the day. It had conquered the island of <term>Rhodes<note>original: *Rhodus*. The order moved its headquarters here in 1310, becoming known as the Knights of Rhodes.</note></term>, and the knights had taken their name from this conquest. As neighbors to the infidels, they continued to wage war against them, and it was argued that the estates of the <term>Templars<note>original: *Tempelherren*.</note></term> could not be better used than for the benefit of the Hospitallers. It was said that they would thereby gain greater power and be encouraged to serve Christendom and serve as its bulwark. The <term>Pope<note>Clement V.</note></term> therefore resolved to unite the Templars with the Order of Saint John.

<margin>Reasons for convening the Council. <note>Sources: Pierre Dupuy and Claude Fleury, noted 17th-century historians.</note></margin>
The trial that had been brought against the Templars, the reform of the clergy, and the affairs of the <term>Holy Land<note>original: *gelobten Landes* (the Promised Land).</note></term> were the three primary reasons for convening the <term>Council<note>original: *Concilium*; specifically the Council of Vienne.</note></term>. However, little work was done regarding the latter two; the third point, assuming the abolition of the Templar Order, was linked to the first regarding the unification of their estates with the Order of Rhodes. Before the opening of the Council, the Pope commanded every <term>Father<note>Referring to the bishops and high-ranking clergy attending the council.</note></term> to fast for three days and to celebrate three Masses to obtain enlightenment from the Holy Spirit.

The Council was opened on Friday, October 16th; the Pope spoke there with dignity, but full of passion. He introduced...

<margin>1311.</margin> ...in his speech all the crimes with which the <term>Templars<note>original: *Tempelherren*; members of the Catholic military order founded in 1119.</note></term> were charged, and of which he believed them convicted. He gave a detailed account of the proceedings and of everything that had been undertaken against them, expressed his abhorrence for such a loathsome and scandalous order, and made the proposal to abolish it.

All the <term>Fathers<note>The high-ranking clergy, such as bishops and abbots, participating in the Council.</note></term> were consulted regarding this abolition. Opinion was divided into two camps; the first, which was more moderate and seemed the most just, suggested that the accused should be heard in their own defense, that all legal formalities in a matter of such great importance should be observed, and that the knights should be confronted with their accusers. The majority of the bishops from France, Italy, Spain, England, Scotland, Ireland, Germany, and Denmark held this view, maintaining that it would be harsh to abolish a member of the Church that had been part of it for so long and had rendered it such good service.

Supporting the second opinion was an equally large number, including the Archbishops of <term>Sens<note>An important ecclesiastical province in France; the Archbishop of Sens at the time was Philippe de Marigny.</note></term>, of Reims, and of Rouen, who deserved all the more attention as they were better informed about the case, having conducted all the proceedings themselves. To their party belonged Guillaume du Rondy, Bishop of <term>Abanne<note>Likely a reference to Guillaume Durand the Younger, the Bishop of Mende, who was a famous legal scholar and influential figure at the Council.</note></term>, a prelate of great scholarship who had investigated the matter thoroughly. They cried out that one should have no hesitation in abolishing such a loathsome order, which was convicted of so many crimes proven by two thousand witnesses <insert>already</insert>...

<margin>1311.</margin>
...[guilty of such] crimes, and made the name of Christians hateful and contemptible among the unbelievers. The session lasted until <term>Holy Week<note>original: *stille Woche*; the week before Easter, characterized by somber reflection.</note></term> without anything being decided.

<margin>
Determination of the property of the Templars.
Dupuy.
Walsingham.
Mariana.
Fleury.
</margin>
The <term>Pope<note>Pope Clement V, who presided over the council.</note></term> entered into a declaration with the Cardinals and the <term>Fathers of the Council<note>The gathered high-ranking clergy, including bishops and abbots.</note></term>, as well as with the King and the ambassadors of other princes, stating that he did not wish to establish a new religious order. He undoubtedly gave them his reasons, adding that he intended to transfer all the property of the Templars to the Order of the <term>Knights of Rhodes<note>original: *Rhodiser-Ritter*; also known as the Knights Hospitaller, who had recently conquered Rhodes in 1310.</note></term>. He argued they would thereby become powerful enough to wage a constant war against the unbelievers and serve Christendom with great success. The King did not oppose this; he only stipulated the preservation of the rights of himself and his crown. The other sovereigns were likewise satisfied with this, except for the King of <term>Aragon<note>James II of Aragon, whose kingdom was actively involved in the Reconquista against Muslim territories.</note></term>, whose ambassadors, on his command, made the representation that since it was His Holiness’s will that the Templars' goods be used to fight the unbelievers, he could not achieve this purpose better or more surely than by designating the goods lying within the states of this prince for the expulsion of the <term>Moors<note>original: *Mohren*; the Muslim inhabitants of the Iberian Peninsula.</note></term>. These Moors had weary the Christian princes for such a long time and spared no effort to seize their provinces; the King, their master, had decided upon this and requested the Pope and the Council to approve it.

<summary>The Council of Vienne reaches a stalemate over the Templars' fate until Pope Clement V proposes transferring their assets to the Knights Hospitaller. While most monarchs agree, the King of Aragon requests that Templar wealth in his lands be reserved for fighting the Moors in Spain.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the account of the Council of Vienne and the negotiations regarding the dissolution of the Knights Templar. The catchword "Die" from the previous page begins the next sentence in the original German structure.</meta>

page-num: 204

->- ) 8 ( -<-

<margin>1311.</margin>
The <term>Pope<note>Pope Clement V (reigned 1305–1314), who was under significant pressure from King Philip IV of France to dissolve the Templars.</note></term> did not agree to this compromise at all. His will was that the law be universal, and that the spoils of the <term>Order of the Temple<note>The Knights Templar.</note></term> should benefit the <term>Order of Rhodes<note>The Knights Hospitaller, who had established their headquarters on the island of Rhodes.</note></term>. He held long discussions about this with the ambassadors; however, the ambassadors of the King of <term>Aragon<note>James II of Aragon, a key figure in the Reconquista, the Christian reconquest of the Iberian Peninsula.</note></term> remained steadfast. They clearly informed the Pope on the King's behalf that this sovereign would not obey his <term>bull<note>original: *Bulle*; a formal proclamation or decree issued by the Pope.</note></term>. They argued that since he had to withstand all attacks of the <term>unbelievers<note>A common historical reference to the Muslim forces in the Iberian Peninsula.</note></term>, it was far more just and necessary that the ecclesiastical properties located within his states be used for their defense, rather than for increasing the power of the <term>Knights of Rhodes<note>The Knights Hospitaller.</note></term>. These knights, they claimed, were not as useful to Christendom as the King's own subjects, whose danger was more immediate. The Pope, who was prudent and perceived these same sentiments among all the other Spanish sovereigns, did not dare to challenge them; he also recognized that their claims were well-founded. He therefore relented and consented that the King of Aragon might found a new order of military knights in his kingdom to wage war against the <term>Moors<note>original: *Mohren*; refers to the Muslim inhabitants of the Maghreb and the Iberian Peninsula during the Middle Ages.</note></term>. To this new order, seventeen cities and castles that had belonged to the Templars were assigned. This is the origin of the <term>Knights of Montesa<note>Founded in 1317, this order was specifically created to manage Templar assets in the Kingdom of Valencia.</note></term>, who made the city of that name their headquarters. They are a branch of the <term>Order of Calatrava<note>A Spanish military order founded in the 12th century; the Knights of Montesa adopted their customs and dress.</note></term>, whose rule they adopted, while nevertheless remaining independent of them.

<vocab>Pope, Order of Rhodes, Order of the Temple, Aragon, Knights of Rhodes, Knights of Montesa, Order of Calatrava</vocab>

<summary>Pope Clement V settles a dispute with the King of Aragon regarding the redistribution of Templar property. To avoid conflict with Spanish rulers, the Pope allows the creation of the Order of Montesa to defend against Moorish attacks using former Templar assets.</summary>
<keywords>Pope Clement V, Knights Templar, Knights Hospitaller, Kingdom of Aragon, Order of Montesa, Order of Calatrava, Reconquista, ecclesiastical property</keywords>

The properties of the <term>Templars<note>The Knights Templar.</note></term> in Spain were thus exempted from the general law and designated for the war against the <term>Moors<note>A historical term for the Muslim populations of the Maghreb and the Iberian Peninsula.</note></term>, who still possessed the very flourishing Kingdom of Granada in Spain. The King of Portugal utilized these same properties in his realm for the founding of the <term>Order of Christ<note>The *Ordem de Cristo*, established in 1319 as the successor to the Templars in Portugal to protect the coast and fight the Moors.</note></term>. It is not found that the King of Castile, Don Ferdinand IV, turned them over to any of the military orders that had existed in his states for a long time. He claimed that because he was oppressed by the burden of a constant war against the <term>unbelievers<note>A historical reference to non-Christian forces, specifically the Islamic kingdoms during the Reconquista.</note></term>, he needed a fund to conduct this war. He thus united all the Templar properties with his own royal domains, without even having received the consent of the Pope, who closed his eyes to the proceedings of this self-willed prince, against whom he could have undertaken nothing without putting his own authority at risk.

After other matters were settled, it was decided to bring the matter of the Templars—which was the primary object of the Council—to a conclusion.

<margin>1312.</margin> <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's discussion of the Pope's strategy.</note> to postpone [it], as he had found that most of the <term>Fathers<note>Refers to the "Council Fathers," the high-ranking clergy (bishops and abbots) attending the Council of Vienne.</note></term> were opposed to the suppression; he therefore had to combine his prestige and skill. The presence of the King <note>King Philip IV "the Fair" of France, who was the primary driver behind the persecution of the Templars.</note> contributed much to a successful outcome; the Pope likewise brought a large number of prelates to his side in the secret and private <term>consistories<note>A formal meeting of the Pope and his cardinals; here, it refers to private lobbying sessions held during the Council.</note></term> which he held with the Cardinals and some of the Fathers, who allowed themselves to be won over by his words, and perhaps by his promises, so that all votes were united, and it was decided that all <term>Templars<note>The Knights Templar, a Catholic military order founded in 1119.</note></term> should be condemned and their Order abolished.

The <term>Bull<note>A formal public decree or charter issued by a Pope. This specific bull is known as *Vox in excelso*.</note></term> regarding this was made known on May 6th by the Pope, with general consent. Its content stated that, since the Pope and the holy Council had convicted the Knights of the Temple Order in Jerusalem of the greatest and most monstrous crimes, he declared this Order extinct and abolished—not through a formal final judgment, as the procedures undertaken against them did not allow for the knights to be lawfully condemned, but only provisionally, according to ecclesiastical decree and by the fullness of his power <note>original: "Fülle seiner Gewalt" / Latin: *plenitudo potestatis*. This is a crucial legal distinction: the Pope suppressed the order as an administrative act of "apostolic providence" rather than a judicial sentence, because the evidence was legally insufficient for a total condemnation.</note>. He forbade any and all from entering the Order, from using the name (which was now abolished), and from wearing the Order's robes, under penalty of <term>excommunication<note>original: "Kirchenbannes." This was a severe social and spiritual punishment that excluded a person from the sacraments and the community of the Church.</note></term>. In this, the disposition of the properties of the Templars was reserved for the Papal See; and, regarding their persons, he confirmed the judicial sentences of the provincial councils of <term>Sens<note>The Council of Sens in 1310 saw the first large-scale burnings of Templars as relapsed heretics.</note></term> and <term>Reims<note>Another location where provincial trials of individual Templars were held.</note></term>, whose decrees he repeated—namely:

It details the resulting Papal Bull, which abolished the Order not by a legal verdict of guilt, but through the Pope's administrative authority.</summary>

<margin>Notes regarding the first Bull.</margin>
Such then was the nature of this famous <term>Bull<note>Refers specifically to the papal decree *Vox in excelso* (March 1312), which dissolved the Order of the Templars.</note></term>, which abolished a highly regarded Order that was said to have been convicted of crimes, even though it was admitted that evidence was lacking to deliver a final verdict. Action was thus taken out of absolute power, with the admission that for secret reasons they wished to abolish an ancient, solemnly and authentically established Order that had rendered—and was still rendering—such great service to Christendom. Such was the nature of the judgment, which was so severe against the "hardened" ones, and quite pointlessly so, since they had already been executed in the previous year in a very premature manner, as it appeared, when the decision of the <term>General Council<note>The Council of Vienne (1311–1312), convened by Pope Clement V to decide the fate of the Templars.</note></term> should have been awaited—which now only had a retroactive effect.

They had recently established their headquarters on the island of Rhodes.</note></term> heard with great joy of the fortunate change that had occurred to the benefit of his Order, whereby, in a single moment and through the papal Bull, the estates which this Order possessed would be more than doubled, and which consequently in Europe, in quite

<meta>catchword: One (Man)</meta>

<margin>1312.</margin>
...unprecedented exceptions. Aside from the reservation of the rights of the King, the prelates, and the lords—which was very reasonable—the <term>Knights of Rhodes<note>original: "Rhodiser:Ritter"; the military order of the Hospitallers who held Rhodes at this time.</note></term> were required, due to the costs incurred during the trial of the <term>Templars<note>original: "Tempelherren"</note></term> and the transfer of their properties, to leave behind two-thirds of the movable assets and 260,000 <term>Livres Tournois<note>A standard currency used in medieval France, named after the city of Tours.</note></term> in silver coin—a staggering sum for those times. This does not even account for what it cost the Knights of Rhodes to free themselves from all the <term>chicanery<note>original: "Chicanen"; legal harassment or deceptive maneuvers.</note></term> and oppression of those who had held the Templar estates in custody and were reluctant to release them. Furthermore, they were burdened with the pensions of all Templars who had been declared innocent, as well as the <term>boarding costs<note>original: "Äzungskosten"; the expense of providing food and sustenance.</note></term>, maintenance, and expenses of all those who remained in prisons or were confined "between four walls." Thus, the benefit granted by the <term>Council<note>original: "Concilium"; referring to the Council of Vienne (1311–1312) which officially dissolved the Templars.</note></term> was initially very burdensome for the Order of Rhodes; yet, they had the prospect of a fortunate future.

<margin>In England. <note>Reference to historian Pierre</note> Dupüy.</margin>
In England, the proceedings were handled more nobly. There, King Edward II, in conjunction with the Parliament, placed them in possession of all the movable and immovable properties of the Templars; they even returned the income that had been collected in the interim. All privileges of the abolished Order were granted to them; the Grand Prior of the <term>Order of Malta<note>original: "Maltheser:Orden"; the same Hospitaller order, later known by this name after moving to Malta.</note></term> in England was appointed a Lord and received a seat and a vote in the Upper House, just as the Grand Prior of the Templars had held.

<margin>1312.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the account of the dissolution of the Order of the Templars in 1312. While the previous page discussed the financial burdens placed on the Knights of Rhodes in France and England, this page describes the legal status of former Templars in England and the political struggle over their property in Germany.</meta>

->— ) 0 ( —<-

<margin>1312.</margin>
Furthermore, in this country <note>Referring to England, as established on the previous page.</note>, one finds no record of a <term>Templar<note>original: "Tempelherrn"</note></term> being executed. Those who remained were assigned pensions. They even believed they had been released from their vows, and it is natural that they believed so. The Order was so gänzlich destroyed and abolished that even its very name was eradicated. Consequently, some of them wished to marry, but the bishops opposed this, maintaining that although they were released from their military vows, the vow of chastity they had taken was indelible and still continued.

<margin>
In Germany.
Dupuy <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French historian whose work on the trial of the Templars is a primary source for this text.</note>.
</margin>
In Germany, where so many princes rule and the King of the Romans, <term>Wenceslaus<note>Wenceslaus IV (1361–1419), King of Bohemia and King of the Romans. His authority was often challenged by the German princes.</note></term>, held no great prestige, opinions regarding the distribution of the Templars' estates varied. Every prince consulted his own advantage in this matter. In this land of Europe, there was a military order consisting of the younger sons of great houses, into which only Germans were admitted. This was the <term>Teutonic Order<note>original: "Orden der Deutschen." A Catholic military order founded in 1190 in Acre.</note></term>, knights famous for their bravery, who had performed great services for religion just as the Templars and the <term>Knights Hospitaller<note>original: "Johanniter-Ritter"</note></term> had done. All the German princes were inclined to grant the Templars' estates to this Order in order to enlarge and enrich it, and to provide a respectable livelihood for their own children.

The <term>Bull<note>original: "Bulle"; a formal decree issued by the Pope.</note></term> was opposed to this. The entire Council and the Pope had, for the best interest of the

<margin>1312.</margin>
The <term>Knights of Rhodes<note>original: "Rhodiser-Ritter," also known as the Knights Hospitaller or the Order of Saint John, who were the primary beneficiaries of the Templars' dissolved estates.</note></term> had received the decree, and the other states had conformed to it, with the exception of the Kings of Spain. However, their example was contagious, and the favor that had been shown to them made the German princes so bold as to demand that at least a portion of the <term>Templars'<note>original: "Tempel-herren"</note></term> estates be granted to the <term>Teutonic Order<note>original: "deutschen Orden"</note></term>, because these assets had originated from the generosity of their own ancestors. Since this demand was made in a tone which suggested that they would not obey if it were not granted, the <term>Pope<note>original: "Pabst"</note></term> gave in once more and agreed that these properties should be divided between the Teutonic Knights and the Knights of Rhodes. This was then carried out, and in the division, the rich <term>commandery<note>original: "Comthurey," an administrative district or manor belonging to a military order.</note></term> of Marienthal or Mergentheim, along with many other smaller commanderies, fell to the former <note>Referring to the Teutonic Knights.</note>. Furthermore, no death sentences were carried out in Germany.

<margin>
In Italy.
<term>Dupuy<note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar and King's librarian whose works on the Templars' trial are primary historical sources.</note></term>.
<term>Gürtler<note>Nicolaus Gürtler (1654–1711), a Swiss theologian and author of "Historia Templariorum," a history of the Templar Order.</note></term>.

In Italy, the <term>Union Bull<note>original: "Unions-Bulle," likely referring to the papal bull "Ad providam" (1312), which officially transferred Templar property to the Hospitallers.</note></term> regarding the Knights of Rhodes was literally fulfilled. They were placed in possession of all the Templars' estates, and they remain in possession of them still. In the states of the King of <term>Naples<note>original: "Neapolis"</note></term>, one must except all <term>movable assets<note>original: "Mobilien-Vermögen"</note></term>, which this lord seized for his own benefit—doubtless because of the expenses he had incurred in his realm, especially in Provence. The judgment that condemned the hardened ones <note>Unrepentant Templars who refused to confess to the charges brought against the Order.</note> to the fire was carried out with the utmost severity.

1312. **Movable goods**, which were of very great value, were offset against the costs expended on their trial. We have already seen that the King in France appropriated two-thirds of the estates in his realm, and there are writers who claim that the **Pope**, who was so greedy for wealth, did not forget himself in this matter, and that a portion of the money and the movable goods of these unfortunate men flowed into his coffers; and regardless of all this, the <term>Order of Rhodes<note>original: "Rhodiser-Orden," referring to the Knights Hospitaller, who had recently established their headquarters on the island of Rhodes.</note></term>, to whom all **immovable** and a large portion of the **movable goods** fell, attained through this union the great power and the excessive wealth which it still possesses to this day.

We have presented the history of the execution of the <term>Bull<note>original: "Bulle," a formal decree issued by the Pope, named after the "bulla" or lead seal attached to it.</note></term> regarding the distribution of these estates in Europe and **Cyprus**, where the same likewise occurred, in a continuous narrative so as not to interrupt the thread of the story, even though the entire year of 1313 was spent putting the Order of Rhodes in possession of them. We must now return to the beginning of this year and recount the **catastrophe** that concluded the condemnation and abolition of the <term>Templars<note>original: "Tempelherren."</note></term>.

In their statements, they had confessed to a portion of the crimes of which the Order was accused; yet, after so many bloody executions, one might have believed that their rank, their birth, and their long imprisonment—which is no minor punishment—would be taken into consideration. However, the authorities thought otherwise, and the **Pope**, by means of a <term>Bull<note>A formal decree or public letter issued by a Pope.</note></term>, appointed <term>Cardinal Legates<note>High-ranking papal representatives with the authority to act on the Pope's behalf.</note></term> as commissioners to judge them in his name and without the possibility of appeal; the first among these was ***Bernard d'Aux***, Bishop of Albano. He joined with them the Archbishop of Sens and the bishops who were assembled in Paris, instructing them to choose several Doctors of Theology to serve as assistants.

The four **Templars** over whom a judgment was to be passed, and whom the Pope had hitherto reserved for himself, were the Grand Master ***Jacques de Molay***, the Prince Dauphin ***Guido***, Grand Prior of Normandy, ***Hugo de Paralde*** or ***Peyraud***, Grand Prior of Aquitaine, and a commander named ***Benigne Coeur-de-Roi***, who had served as Minister of Finance.

The Cardinals and commissioners were struck with the utmost astonishment; they did not know how to fully carry out their mandate, nor how to announce a judgment upon a crime that had now changed its very nature. Yet, the astonishment of the vast multitude of people who had gathered for this ceremony was even greater. Among them, there reigned a silence even deeper than before, when the Cardinal was speaking—a silence of terror, fear, and pity. Everyone knew what must follow such a significant recantation <note>Under the laws of the Inquisition, "relapsing" by withdrawing a confession after sentencing typically resulted in immediate execution by burning.</note>. At their stubborn refusal, the commissioners went into council with one another, and since they could not immediately reach a decision, they agreed that the defendants should be led back to prison, and they would have to assemble again on the following day to decide their fate after more mature reflection. The King receives news of the retraction. The same [sources].

The King, who was in the Louvre <note>The royal palace in Paris, long before it became a museum</note>, very soon received news of everything that had occurred. He necessarily became very indignant over what the Grand Master <term>Grand Master: the supreme head of the Knights Templar, Jacques de Molay</term> had said; namely, that he had accused himself of such abominable crimes only because of the King's representations <note>Original: "Vorstellungen," implying the King's pressure, influence, or false promises</note>. This was bound to have a very bad effect on the public mind. Furthermore, he took into consideration that the Commissioners <term>Commissioners: the papal and royal judges appointed to the trial</term>, by virtue of the postponement they had undertaken regarding the judgment they were to pass against the guilty ones, were uncertain. This case was not new, because all Templars <term>Templars: members of the Order of the Temple, a powerful military and religious order</term> who, because of such a retraction which they had refused to take back, and who had been seen as "relapsed" or hardened, had been executed. In order, therefore, to put an end to the indecision of the Commissioners, to prevent their dangerous pity, and to complete the extermination of a hated Order—of which a remnant still seemed to remain as long as two members of such an exalted rank were still alive—he assembled those members of his Council who were with him and consulted with them, without involving even a single clergyman; and in this secret council, where the King's

<margin>1312.</margin> Since the King's will dictated the laws, it was decided that the two accused should be executed without loss of time, following the pattern of what had been done with the others who were deemed hardened <note>The term "hardened" or "relapsed" (relapsi) referred to those who retracted their confessions; under inquisitorial law, they were handed over to the secular authorities to be burned</note>. The King immediately gave the order to light a pyre on the island located between the Royal Gardens and the **Augustinian Monastery** <note>This was the Île aux Juifs, a small island in the Seine near the modern Pont Neuf</note>, to lead them there, and to burn them that very same day. This soon became known, and the immense crowd of people who had been in the courtyard of **Our Lady** <note>The Cathedral of Notre Dame de Paris</note> made their way to the place of execution.

<meta>This page continues the final speech of Jacques de Molay at the stake, where he recants his previous confessions and accepts his death. It follows the narrative of King Philip IV's immediate order for the execution of the Templar leaders after their public defiance at Notre Dame.</meta>

<margin>1313.</margin>
> ...[deference] to men, at the pleas and insinuations of the Pope and the King; an abominable crime that pierces my heart with the most vivid pain. Seized with terror, I most humbly ask my God for forgiveness for this. Oh! If only I had not committed this loathsome crime! But since what has happened is irrevocable, if this crime can be atoned for, God reconciled, and the scandal we have given be removed, we wish to suffer the punishment prepared for us—indeed, we would be willing to suffer an even more cruel punishment. I recognize that I have earned death through the dishonorable testimony I gave. Life is offered to me if I take back my retraction <note>Under inquisitorial law, if a "relapsed" heretic recanted their retraction and returned to their original confession, they might be spared death and instead receive life imprisonment</note>. What use would a life be to me that I obtained through a second crime, which would make me even more culpable? It would be hateful and unbearable to me."

<margin>Execution of the Grand Master and the Prince Dauphin. The same sources <note>Referring to the historians cited on the previous page: Mariana, Gürtler, Mezeray, Vertot, and Paul Emile</note>.</margin>
He wished to speak further; however, he was prevented from doing so, and as the pyre was lit, the executioner’s assistants seized him and bound him to a stake. The Prince Dauphin <note>Geoffroi de Charney, Preceptor of Normandy, whom the author continues to identify as the "Prince Dauphin" based on historical misconceptions in the cited sources</note> was also bound to it, who nearly repeated the words of the Grand Master and delivered a speech of similar content. They burned them both over a slow fire; this was evident from the fact that no great flames flared up that might have suffocated them, so that they felt the entire torment of this cruel punishment. They endured this sentence with an admirable composure and steadfastness, without outcry or groaning; they prayed...

<summary>This page records the final words and execution of Jacques de Molay and Geoffroi de Charney, who were burned at the stake for retracting their confessions. The account emphasizes their stoic endurance of a slow, agonizing death as a form of atonement.</summary>
