<margin>1313.</margin>
...called upon God’s mercy, and even while half-burned, they still renounced their false statements and bore witness to the holiness and innocence of their Order. They both died in these sentiments, and in a sense, with a kind of consolation. <note>original footnote: "Incomprehensible labyrinth of the human heart! that people who, to avoid torture, confess to fictional crimes, and yet endure the most terrifying punishment with such steadfastness. But we believe we see the cause of this. The most steadfast man, who is imprisoned and finds himself in the hands of a powerful enemy, is initially dejected and no longer himself. Imperceptibly, he becomes accustomed to his condition and regains his natural greatness of soul; indeed, even a greater one; he combines it with the powerful spring of indignation. We are far from approving the barbaric proceedings of Philip the Fair; the Order had to be destroyed, its goods kept, added to the royal domains, or better yet, leased to industrious subjects, and the former Templars given back their freedom with a pension. The alleged crimes had no shadow of probability. But so great was the misfortune of those times that such crimes were necessary to destroy a society that was connected with the clergy. — Author’s Note."</note>

<margin>Confusion, terror, and pain of the people. The same [sources].</margin>
It is easy to imagine what confusion, what abhorrence and pain seized the people and all the spectators at the sight of this execution. If one usually

<page-num>223</page-num>

<margin>1313.</margin>
—usually reveals emotion and pity even at the execution of the greatest wrongdoers, how great then must the stirrings of the soul have been among the French when they saw two gentlemen of such great prestige die such a cruel death! One was the <term>Grand Master</term><note>The highest-ranking official of the Knights Templar; at this time, Jacques de Molay.</note> of a famous Order, who held a rank equal to sovereign princes; and the other a prince, the son of a sovereign, who was related to all kings and was the son of a princess of the blood! <note>This refers to Geoffroi de Charney, the Preceptor of Normandy, whom the text identifies here as the "Prince Dauphin."</note> Did they not accuse the King of cruelty for having no respect even for his own blood? The Grand Master was still in his best years, and the Prince Dauphin was in the bloom of his age; he was only thirty-five years old. <note>Historical records suggest de Molay was actually in his 70s, though this account emphasizes their vitality to heighten the tragedy.</note> They asserted their innocence, and the words of the dying—when it is within their power not to die—are regarded as oracles. Almost the entire assembly also considered them innocent. They surrendered themselves to grief and shed streams of tears. Full of confusion, deep in sorrow, and filled with admiration for their steadfastness, they regarded these two gentlemen as two witnesses to the truth, and posterity has done them the same justice. After their death, the people gathered their ashes, and many took with them whatever they could retrieve of their bones as precious relics, which they kept as holy, and indeed even venerated.

<margin>The Summons. Gürtler. Mezerai.</margin> <note>References to historians Nicolaus Gürtler and François Eudes de Mézeray.</note>
It is said that the Grand Master, when he was almost consumed by the fire and only his tongue remained free, cried out: “Clement, un... <note>original: "Clemens, un..." This refers to the legend where de Molay summoned Pope Clement V and King Philip the Fair to appear before the judgment seat of God within a year; both died shortly thereafter.</note>

<margin>1313.</margin> "...righteous judge, I summon you to appear before the judgment seat of God within forty days, counting from today, and you likewise, unjust King Philip, within a year's time *) ." However, it is evident that this widely believed tradition is anything but certain, as this circumstance is reported by no contemporary author. And why would the <term>Grand Master</term><note>Jacques de Molay, the last head of the Knights Templar.</note> have made such a distinction between the two summons?

<margin>1314. Death of the Pope. Fleury. Gürtler. Mézeray.</margin> <note>The author cites three historians: Claude Fleury, Nicolaus Gürtler, and François de Mézeray.</note> Shortly after this tragic conclusion of the matter, the Pope, afflicted by various ailments, fell dangerously ill. He suffered from a discomfort <note>original: "Incommodität," likely referring to a chronic condition like a fistula or abdominal pain</note> that made life burdensome for him. To this were added stomach cramps and finally dysentery <term>Ruhr</term><note>An intestinal infection that was frequently fatal in the 14th century due to dehydration.</note>, from which he became completely emaciated at Carpentras, where he had traveled to attend to certain affairs. He believed the air of his birthplace would suit him better and set out in a sedan chair to be carried to Bordeaux, where he had been born; however, the movement of the sedan chair worsened his condition; he was forced to stop at Roquemaure <note>original: "Roquenande," a small town on the Rhône near Avignon, in the diocese of Nîmes</note>, and died there on April 20th, 1314—exactly forty days after the death of the Grand Master.

<margin>
1313.
</margin>

This Pope <note>Clement V, the first of the Avignon Popes.</note> possessed great intellect and many talents; however, he sacrificed everything to politics, ambition, and a thirst for gold. He was not even free from accusations of unchastity, and he was even accused of an unlawful passion for the Countess of Périgord, a daughter of the Count of Foix, who was considered the most beautiful woman in France. He left his cousins immeasurable treasures, which had been gathered through highly illicit means and were scattered in a very short time.

<margin>
Death of the King. <note>Historical references to the works of François de Mézeray and Père Anselme.</note>
</margin>

The King <note>Philip IV of France, known as Philip the Fair.</note> survived him by only eight months, although he was much younger and therefore, according to the natural order of things, should have lived much longer, as he was only forty-six years old. He died on November 29th, 1314, not without suspicion of poisoning. After the execution of the two <term>Templars</term><note>Referring to Jacques de Molay and Geoffroi de Charney.</note>, he had remained in a constant state of languishing and had been seized by a lingering fever that finally carried him off. This circumstance—that the Pope died within forty days of the Grand Master and the King's death followed within a year's time—perhaps gave rise to the invention of the "summons before God," which was spread by those who considered the cruel execution of these two lords to be unjust.

<margin>
The end of the accusers.
</margin>

It indeed had the appearance that divine justice pursued all those who had contributed to the eradication of the <term>Order of the Temple</term><note>The Knights Templar.</note> and to so many bloody executions:

...had. <note>Continuing from the previous page: "It indeed [had an effect they] had."</note> The two dishonorable denouncers and accusers, who had been given blind faith, met a miserable end. They had triumphed over the misfortune of the Order which they had abandoned. The first was convicted of a new crime and imprisoned, and the second was murdered by his enemies in a treacherous manner. <note>This refers to Esquiu de Floyran and Bernard Pelet, the two former Templars whose initial accusations sparked the investigation.</note>

<note>original footnote: "Crime is always punished by natural consequences that have nothing of the miraculous about them. This is a glorious truth that cannot be emphasized enough; nevertheless, the eradication of the Order was nonetheless good and lawful; (why, Mr. Author? <note>This parenthetical question is an editorial interjection in the German source, questioning the author's justification for the Order's destruction.</note>) only the means were punishable."</note>

Dupuy. <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar who wrote a history of the Templar trial.</note>

The two <term>Templars</term><note>Members of the Poor Fellow-Soldiers of Christ and of the Temple of Solomon.</note>, Peralde <note>Hugues de Pairaud, the Visitor of France and Grand Prior of Aquitaine.</note>, Grand Prior of Aquitaine, and the one who had oversight of the financial affairs <note>Likely referring to Godefroi de Gonneville, Preceptor of Aquitaine.</note>, also received the punishment their weakness deserved for failing to maintain the recantation of their first testimony. They were in the same situation as the <term>Grand Master</term><note>Jacques de Molay.</note> and the Prince Dauphin <note>Geoffroi de Charny, Preceptor of Normandy, often called the Dauphin of Auvergne.</note> and had before their eyes the magnanimous example of these gentlemen, according to which they should have preferred death over confessing to the crimes of which they were accused; but the sight of such an abominable death penalty terrified them. They remained silent when the <term>Commissioners</term><note>The papal or royal judges overseeing the trial.</note> read them their sentence and submitted to it. It appeared that the fear of such a cruel and imminent death excused them to some extent; however, there are sometimes occasions:

<margin>1313</margin>
...circumstances where the social man must depart from the rules of nature and sacrifice his life for the truth. <note>The text suggests that while most men seek self-preservation, there are moments of moral crisis where truth-telling requires the sacrifice of one's life—a choice these two leaders did not make until it was too late.</note> Both died a most sad death; that they were shown some leniency can be concluded from the fact that they were not, according to the content of the sentence, placed "between four walls" <note>This phrase refers to *immurement*, a form of life imprisonment where a person was literally walled into a small cell.</note>, even though they repented neither for their crimes (if they were guilty) nor for their base cowardice (if they were innocent).

This was the end of a famous Order <term>Knights Templar</term><note>A wealthy and powerful Catholic military order founded in 1119, primarily known for their role in the Crusades and as early bankers.</note>, which had maintained its splendor for two centuries, and through heroic bravery and brilliant deeds had rendered such great service to Christendom. However, they had acquired immeasurable wealth, which became for them an unfortunate source of pride and corruption; through this, the Order became as hateful to Christian princes as it was fearsome to the infidels. <note>In this context, "infidels" refers to the non-Christian forces, primarily Muslim armies, that the Templars fought in the Holy Land.</note> There can be no doubt that crimes and disorders had crept into many of the Templars; but that the entire Order was infected by them is against all probability—indeed, against the truth itself. It succumbed to the hatred of a proud, absolute, powerful, and vengeful prince. <note>This refers to King Philip IV of France, also known as Philip the Fair, who was heavily in debt to the Templars and engineered their downfall.</note> There can be no doubt that he [the King] was convinced <note>*) Or rather, he believed himself to be convinced. Note by the Translator.</note> that they were punishable, and that this conviction allowed him to find grace before God; but through a violent, cruel...

--- Page 252 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the legal proceedings against the Knights Templar, following the previous page's account of the Order's fall and the imprisonment of its leaders.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>
<header>— ) 0 ( —</header>

<margin>1313.</margin> ...unusual, unheard-of proceeding he initiated, even though appearances were heavily against him; although one must admit that if he had proceeded according to the letter of the law, the total extermination of such a large and numerous Order would never have been accomplished. The Pope <note>Pope Clement V (r. 1305–1314), who moved the papacy to Avignon and was often seen as a puppet of the French King.</note> was even more culpable than he, as he base-mindedly accommodated himself to his passions, and found himself driven to condemn this great Order prematurely, out of the fullness of his power <note>Referring to the "Apostolic fullness of power" (plenitudo potestatis) used to bypass standard legal trials.</note>, rather than through the proper forms of law. One cannot deny, therefore, that base self-interest played a large part in these unworthy *) proceedings, and that the Pope, the King, and their ministers seized for themselves a portion of the spoils taken from these unfortunate men.

The only benefit that religion derived from the abolition of the <term>Templars</term> <note>The Knights Templar, a Catholic military order.</note> is that all of their <term>commanderies</term> <note>Regional administrative centers and estates that provided income for the military orders.</note> have been merged with the Order of St. John, now the Order of Malta, which has inherited their properties and their bravery, and which serves as a shield and bulwark for Christendom. He <note>Likely an address to a student or noble reader, suggesting they learn from this historical moral failure.</note> must use this terrible example for his own benefit, persevere in virtue, and recommend himself more strongly every day through his character.

---

*) Unworthy, not because of the act itself, but because of the means employed; a King and a Pope may abolish an Order for no other reason than that the public good requires it.

<meta>This page serves as a concluding correction by the translator regarding the heraldry of the Knights Templar, following the previous discussion on the legal dissolution of the Order and the transfer of its assets to the Order of Saint John.</meta>

## Concluding Note from the Translator.

I find it necessary to correct an error here that the author committed in the preface. He attributes to the <term>knightly order</term> <note>Specifically the Knights Templar, whose dress was strictly regulated by the Latin Rule of the Templars.</note> a red cloak with a white cross, when in fact the exact opposite is true; they wore a white cloak with a red cross.

---

<gloss>Concluding Note, Translator, Author, Preface, Knightly Order, red cloak, white cross, opposite</gloss>

<summary>In this brief postscript, the translator corrects a factual error made by the author in the preface regarding the specific colors of the Knights Templar's iconic vestments.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, heraldry, white mantle, red cross, historical correction, translator's note</keywords>
